### [1] [AgentWall: A Runtime Safety Layer for Local AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16265)
*Ashwin Aravind*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentWall is a runtime safety layer for local AI agents that intercepts actions before execution, enforces declarative policies, requires human approval for sensitive ops, and logs trails for audit. It achieves 92.9% policy accuracy with sub-ms overhead across 14 benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety approaches (model alignment, input filtering) fail to address the gap when an agent's intent becomes a real action on a real machine, especially in local environments where agents access filesystems, credentials, and infrastructure without runtime control.

Method: AgentWall implements a policy-enforcing MCP proxy and native OpenClaw plugin that intercepts every proposed agent action before host execution, evaluates against declarative policies, requires human approval for sensitive operations, and records audit trails.

Result: 92.9% policy enforcement accuracy with sub-millisecond overhead across 14 benchmark tests.

Conclusion: AgentWall provides effective runtime safety and observability for local AI agents, bridging the critical gap between intent and action with minimal performance cost.

Abstract: The safety of autonomous AI agents is increasingly recognized as a critical open problem. As agents transition from passive text generators to active actors capable of executing shell commands, modifying files, calling APIs, and browsing the web, the consequences of unsafe or adversarially manipulated behavior become immediate and tangible. Existing AI safety work has focused primarily on model alignment and input filtering, but these approaches do not address what happens at the moment an agent's intent becomes a real action on a real machine. This gap is especially acute in local environments, where developers run agents against their own filesystems, credentials, and infrastructure with little runtime control. This paper introduces AgentWall, a runtime safety and observability layer for local AI agents. AgentWall intercepts every proposed agent action before it reaches the host environment, evaluates it against an explicit declarative policy, requires human approval for sensitive operations, and records a complete execution trail for audit and replay. It is implemented as a policy-enforcing MCP proxy and native OpenClaw plugin, working across Claude Desktop, Cursor, Windsurf, Claude Code, and OpenClaw with a single install command. We present the design, architecture, threat model, and policy model of AgentWall, and demonstrate 92.9% policy enforcement accuracy with sub-millisecond overhead across 14 benchmark tests. AgentWall is open-source at https://github.com/agentwall/Agentwall.

</details>


### [2] [ANNEAL: Adapting LLM Agents via Governed Symbolic Patch Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16309)
*Safayat Bin Hakim,Keyan Guo,Wenkai Tan,Alvaro Velasquez,Shouhuai Xu,Houbing Herbert Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ANNEAL is a neuro-symbolic agent that repairs process knowledge graphs to eliminate recurring execution failures, achieving 0% holdout failure rates compared to 72-100% for baselines like ReAct and Reflexion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents repeatedly fail on the same faults because underlying process knowledge (operator schemas, preconditions, constraints) remains unrepaired. Existing self-evolving approaches update prompts, memory, or weights but do not repair symbolic task-execution structures nor provide governance guarantees for safe deployment.

Method: ANNEAL uses Failure-Driven Knowledge Acquisition (FDKA) to localize faulty operators, synthesize typed patches via constrained LLM generation, and validate proposals through multi-dimensional scoring, symbolic guardrails, and canary testing before committing edits with full provenance and deterministic rollback.

Result: Across 4 domains and 27 multi-seed runs, ANNEAL reduces recurring failure rates to 0% versus 72-100% for baselines (ReAct, Reflexion). Ablation shows removing FDKA eliminates all structural repairs and drops success rate by up to 26.7 percentage points.

Conclusion: Governed symbolic repair (editing process knowledge graphs) offers a complementary paradigm to weight-level and prompt-level adaptation for persistent fault elimination in LLM agents.

Abstract: LLM-based agents can recover from individual execution errors, yet they repeatedly fail on the same fault when the underlying process knowledge--operator schemas, preconditions, and constraints--remains unrepaired. Existing self-evolving approaches address this gap by updating prompts, memory, or model weights, but none directly repair the symbolic structures that encode how tasks are executed, and few provide the governance guarantees required for safe deployment. We introduce ANNEAL, a neuro-symbolic agent that converts recurring failures into governed symbolic edits of a process knowledge graph without modifying foundation model weights. Its core mechanism, Failure-Driven Knowledge Acquisition (FDKA), localizes the responsible operator, synthesizes a typed patch through constrained LLM generation, and validates the proposal via multi-dimensional scoring, symbolic guardrails, and canary testing before commit. Every accepted edit carries full provenance and deterministic rollback capability. Across four domains and 27 multi-seed runs, ANNEAL is the only evaluated system that commits persistent structural repairs--strong baselines such as ReAct and Reflexion achieve high episodic recovery yet retain 72-100% holdout failure rates on recurring faults, whereas ANNEAL reduces these to 0% in the tested recurring-failure settings. Ablation confirms that removing FDKA eliminates all structural repairs and drops success rate by up to 26.7 percentage points. These results suggest that governed symbolic repair offers a complementary paradigm to weight-level and prompt-level adaptation for persistent fault elimination.

</details>


### [3] [From Prompts to Protocols: An AI Agent for Laboratory Automation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16552)
*Angelos Angelopoulos,James F. Cahoon,Ron Alterovitz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An AI agent architecture integrating LLMs with laboratory orchestration enables scientists to create and monitor automated lab protocols using natural language, achieving 97% first-attempt success rate and order-of-magnitude reduction in interface actions across chemistry, biology, and materials science domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Setting up and running autonomous labs requires coordinating numerous instruments and robots, forcing scientists to write code, manage configuration files, and navigate complex software infrastructure, which hinders accessibility and efficiency.

Method: An AI agent architecture integrating large language models with the Experiment Orchestration System (EOS), operating under an agentic loop with automated validation and error correction. Includes a visual graph editor for interactive node-based protocol diagrams synchronized with the AI agent's representation, enabling seamless alternation between AI-assisted and manual protocol construction.

Result: 97% first-attempt protocol generation success rate and an order of magnitude reduction in required interface actions, evaluated on three simulated automated labs spanning chemistry, biology, and materials science.

Conclusion: The AI agent enables interactive creation and monitoring of automated lab protocols using natural language, supporting the complete experimental lifecycle while significantly improving success rates and reducing user workload.

Abstract: Automating science laboratories enables faster, safer, more accurate, and more reproducible execution of protocols, accelerating the discovery and testing of new materials, drugs, and more. However, setting up and running autonomous labs requires coordinating numerous instruments and robots, forcing scientists to write code, manage configuration files, and navigate complex software infrastructure. We present an AI agent architecture that integrates large language models with laboratory orchestration, enabling scientists to interactively create and monitor automated lab protocols using natural language. Integrated into the Experiment Orchestration System (EOS), the AI agent operates under an agentic loop with automated validation and error correction, and supports the complete experimental lifecycle: creating protocols, running and monitoring both protocols and closed-loop optimization campaigns, and analyzing results. A visual graph editor renders protocols as interactive node-based diagrams synchronized with the AI agent's protocol representation, enabling seamless alternation between AI-assisted and manual protocol construction. Evaluated on three simulated automated labs spanning chemistry, biology, and materials science, the AI agent achieves a 97% first-attempt protocol generation success rate and an order of magnitude reduction in required interface actions.

</details>


### [4] [Skim: Speculative Execution for Fast and Efficient Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16565)
*Mike Wong,Kevin Hsieh,Suman Nath,Ravi Netravali*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Skim is a speculative execution framework for web agents that reduces cost and latency by exploiting predictable website structures, allowing most queries to bypass expensive components (frontier models, browser rendering, ReAct planning) via offline-profiled templates and a lightweight verifier, achieving 1.9x median cost reduction and 33.4% latency improvement with no accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current web agents are unnecessarily expensive because they apply heavyweight components (frontier-model inference, browser rendering, ReAct-style planning) to every step of every task, even when the task is simple and the website structure is predictable.

Method: Skim uses an offline profiler to capture stable URL patterns, answer formats, and task-to-trajectory mappings per site. At runtime, it matches queries to templates, synthesizes destination URLs, and extracts answers with a small model. A lightweight verifier gates outputs; rare misspeculations fall back to the full agent, warm-started from the fast path's final URL.

Result: Across WebVoyager, AgentOccam, and BrowserUse benchmarks, Skim reduces median per-task cost by 1.9x and latency by 33.4% with no accuracy loss.

Conclusion: Speculative execution can significantly improve web-agent efficiency by exploiting website structure without sacrificing accuracy, suggesting that much of today's web-agent expense is due to composition choices rather than task requirements.

Abstract: Skim is a speculative execution framework for web agents that exploits the predictable structure of purpose-built websites. Today's web-agent expense is not intrinsic to the tasks but a property of how agents are composed: frontier-model inference, browser rendering, and ReAct-style planning are applied to every step of every task regardless of complexity. Skim's key observation is that websites enforce stable URL patterns, answer formats, and task-to-trajectory mappings across queries of the same type, so most queries can bypass these heavyweight components entirely. An offline profiler captures these patterns once per site. At runtime, Skim matches each query to a template, synthesizes the destination URL, and extracts the answer with a small model. A lightweight verifier gates each fast-path output against the query and schema; rare misspeculations cascade to the full agent, warm-started by the fast path's final URL to preserve upstream trajectory progress. Across standard web-agent benchmarks paired with three backboneagents (WebVoyager, AgentOccam, BrowserUse), Skim reduces median per-task cost by 1.9x and latency by 33.4% with no accuracy loss.

</details>


### [5] [Scalable Uncertainty Reasoning in Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16568)
*Jingcheng Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This thesis proposes a modular framework to handle three levels of uncertainty in Knowledge Graphs—imprecise attributes, probabilistic triples, and incomplete schemas—using tailored algebraic, logical, and geometric reasoning mechanisms that preserve both semantic precision and computational tractability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world data modeled by Knowledge Graphs is inherently uncertain, manifesting at three distinct levels (attribute imprecision, probabilistic triple existence, incomplete schema knowledge), yet current Semantic Web standards lack native support for reasoning under such uncertainty, and naive extensions are computationally intractable.

Method: A modular framework with three tailored techniques: (1) probabilistic literals with a query algebra for continuous attributes; (2) a compilation-based framework that transforms SPARQL provenance into tractable probabilistic circuits for uncertain triples; (3) topology-aware geometric embeddings for statistical schema reasoning.

Result: Not explicitly stated in the abstract, but the anticipated outcome is that specialized algebraic, logical, and geometric reasoning mechanisms can reconcile semantic precision with computational tractability across all three uncertainty levels.

Conclusion: Specialized reasoning mechanisms—algebraic for attributes, logical (compilation-based) for triples, and geometric for schemas—can each address a specific uncertainty level while maintaining both semantic accuracy and computational efficiency, suggesting a viable modular approach for extending Knowledge Graphs with uncertainty support.

Abstract: Knowledge Graphs are pivotal for semantic data integration. The real-world data they model is often inherently uncertain. Within knowledge graphs, uncertainty manifests in three distinct levels: imprecise attribute values, probabilistic triple existence, and incomplete schema knowledge. However, current Semantic Web standards lack native support for reasoning over such uncertainty, and naïve extensions often incur computational intractability. In this thesis, I aim to develop a modular framework that addresses each level through tailored techniques: (1) defining probabilistic literals and a corresponding query algebra for continuous attributes; (2) a compilation-based framework transforming SPARQL provenance into tractable probabilistic circuits for uncertain triples; and (3) topology-aware geometric embeddings for statistical schema reasoning. The central hypothesis is that specialized reasoning mechanisms, namely algebraic, logical, and geometric approaches, can reconcile semantic precision with computational tractability.

</details>


### [6] [Counterparty Modeling is Not Strategy: The Limits of LLM Negotiators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16575)
*Romain Cosentino,Sarath Shekkizhar,Adam Earle,Silvio Savarese*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Current LLM agents can model counterparty preferences but fail to leverage that knowledge for strategic bargaining, resulting in agreements driven by surface-level anchors rather than utility optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether LLM agents can go beyond preference inference to use that information strategically in multi-turn, multi-attribute negotiations.

Method: Controlled multi-attribute bargaining experiments where LLM agents negotiate with known counterparty preferences; turn-level analyses of reasoning traces and outcomes under symmetric and asymmetric information conditions.

Result: LLM agents accurately model counterparty preferences early in reasoning, but this knowledge does not reliably improve their bargaining outcomes. Agents often respond to counterparty values but fail to pair concessions with gains on their own high-value attributes. In asymmetric settings, informed agents make weakly compensated concessions. Final agreements are driven by opening anchors, not utility weights.

Conclusion: LLM agents lack strategic negotiation capabilities despite accurate preference modeling; explicit concession-for-reciprocity instructions improve turn-level behavior but not overall agreement efficiency.

Abstract: Negotiation requires more than inferring what the other side wants: it requires using that information to make advantageous offers and counteroffers over multiple turns. We study whether large language model (LLM) agents do this in a controlled multi-attribute bargaining environment. We find that current LLM agents can model a counterparty's preferences, but do not reliably turn that knowledge into strategic bargaining. When given negotiating partner preference information, agents model it accurately and early in their reasoning traces, yet this does not reliably improve outcomes for the informed side. Turn-level analyses show why: agents often respond to what they believe the counterparty values, but do not consistently pair those moves with gains on their own high-value attributes. Sellers are more accommodating overall, and in asymmetric-information conditions, the informed side often makes the more weakly compensated concessions. Because agents fail to leverage this underlying utility structure for strategic advantage, their final agreements are heavily dictated by surface-level opening anchors rather than actual utility weights. Finally, requiring agents to explicitly state concession-for-reciprocity trades before making an offer makes individual turns look more strategic, but ultimately fails to improve the efficiency of the final agreements.

</details>


### [7] [TTE-Flash: Accelerating Reasoning-based Multimodal Representations via Think-Then-Embed Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16638)
*Jianpeng Cheng,Xian Wu,Jiangfan Zhang,Wentao Bao,Chaitanya Ahuja,Shlok Kumar Mishra,Hanchao Yu,Yang Gao,Fan Xia,Qi Guo,Shaodan Zhai,Xiangjun Fan,Jun Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes replacing expensive explicit Chain-of-Thought reasoning in Universal Multimodal Embedding with latent think tokens that are optimized using CoT generation loss and contrastive loss, achieving reasoning-aware representations at constant inference cost. The TTE-Flash-2B model outperforms explicit-CoT counterparts on MMEB-v2 benchmark and shows scaling behavior with more think tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explicit CoT reasoning in multimodal embeddings is effective but computationally prohibitive. The authors aim to achieve similar reasoning-aware representations without the high inference cost of generating explicit reasoning traces.

Method: The authors replace explicit CoT traces with latent think tokens interpreted as latent variables that can produce explicit CoT traces as observed variables. Think tokens are optimized using CoT generation loss, while embedding tokens are optimized using contrastive loss. They investigate two architectural questions: (1) how think and embedding tokens should be extracted from the same LLM backbone, and (2) how the tokens should be trained as two dependent tasks.

Result: TTE-Flash-2B outperforms its explicit-CoT counterpart on the MMEB-v2 benchmark. Latent think tokens are interpretable both textually and visually. Zero-shot evaluation across 15 video datasets reveals scaling behavior as the number of think tokens increases, motivating adaptive think budget allocation.

Conclusion: Latent think tokens can effectively replace explicit CoT reasoning in multimodal embeddings, delivering superior performance at constant inference cost while being interpretable and exhibiting scaling behavior with more tokens.

Abstract: Recent research has demonstrated that Universal Multimodal Embedding (UME) benefits significantly from Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. In this paradigm, a generative model produces explicit reasoning traces for a multimodal query, with the final representation extracted from an <eos> embedding token attending to both the query and the reasoning. Despite its effectiveness, the computational overhead of generating explicit CoT traces is often prohibitive. In this work, we propose replacing explicit CoT with latent think tokens, which are interpreted as latent variables that can produce explicit CoT traces as observed variables. By optimizing think tokens using CoT generation loss and subsequent embedding tokens using contrastive loss, we produce high-performance, reasoning-aware representations at a constant inference cost. Our study investigates two key architectural designs: 1) how think and embeddings tokens should be extracted from the same LLM backbone. 2) how the tokens should be trained as two dependent tasks. We introduce TTE-Flash-2B, a reasoning-aware multimodal representation model that outperforms its explicit-CoT counterpart on the MMEB-v2 benchmark, while producing latent think tokens that are interpretable both textually and visually. Furthermore, zero-shot evaluation across 15 video datasets reveals scaling behavior as the number of think tokens increases, and motivating a pilot study of adaptive think budget allocation based on task requirements.

</details>


### [8] [Sustainable Intelligence for the Wild: Democratizing Ecological Monitoring via Knowledge-Adaptive Edge Expert Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16671)
*Jiaxing Li,Hao Fang,Chi Xu,Miao Zhang,Jiangchuan Liu,William I. Atlas,Katrina M. Connors,Mark A. Spoljaric*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes moving from model adaptation to knowledge adaptation for on-device AI biodiversity monitoring, using a visual encoder with a dynamic explicit knowledge base to replace implicit knowledge in model parameters, enabling sustainable and culturally informed ecosystem management.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rapid biodiversity loss requires effective monitoring, but manual surveys are resource-intensive and current on-device AI solutions struggle with environmental variability; cloud-dependent retraining is unsuitable for remote deployments due to limited power and connectivity.

Method: Shifts from model adaptation to knowledge adaptation by separating visual perception from reasoning using a visual encoder combined with a dynamic explicit knowledge base, replacing implicit expert knowledge encoded in model parameters.

Result: The proposed architecture supports knowledge sustainability by preserving expert insights in structured form, and enables ethical AI co-development through cross-disciplinary collaboration with biologists and Indigenous communities.

Conclusion: This knowledge adaptation approach addresses the limitations of cloud-dependent retraining for remote biodiversity monitoring, while fostering responsible and culturally informed ecosystem management through collaborative development.

Abstract: Rapid biodiversity loss underscore the urgency of effective monitoring, yet manual surveys remain resource-intensive. While on-device AI offers a scalable alternative, its performance in the wild is often challenged by environmental variability. Current methods rely heavily on cloud resource, which requires continuous uploading of field data for model retraining. This approach is unsuitable for remote deployments because it consumes limited power and network connectivity. To address these constraints, this research proposes a shift from model adaptation to knowledge adaptation. We introduce an architecture that separates visual perception from reasoning, combining a visual encoder with a dynamic knowledge base. We uses an explicit knowledge base to replace implicitly encoding expert knowledge into model parameters. This method also supports knowledge sustainability by preserving expert insights in a structured form. Through cross-disciplinary collaboration with biologists and Indigenous communities, this work advances ethical AI co-development, fostering responsible and culturally informed ecosystem management.

</details>


### [9] [LinAlg-Bench: A Forensic Benchmark Revealing Structural Failure Modes in LLM Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16675)
*Shradha Agarwal,Deepak Rajbhar,Tariq J*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LinAlg-Bench evaluates 10 LLMs on linear algebra (3x3 to 5x5 matrices) across 660 SymPy-certified problems, revealing a sharp behavioral threshold at 4x4: below it, errors are execution-based (sign, arithmetic, parity); above it, models abandon computation and fabricate responses via tool roleplay, confabulation, and structured hallucination. This transition is universal across model tiers, suggesting a working memory limit rather than knowledge gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically diagnose how LLMs fail at structured mathematical computation, moving beyond binary accuracy to characterize the nature and structural constraints of errors across varying matrix dimensions.

Method: Built a benchmark with 660 SymPy-certified linear algebra problems spanning 9 task types across 3 matrix sizes (3x3, 4x4, 5x5); evaluated 10 frontier LLMs (6,600 outputs); introduced a three-stage forensic pipeline to classify 1,156 failures into 10 primary error tags with fine-grained subtypes.

Result: Sharp behavioral threshold at 4x4: errors shift from execution failures (sign, arithmetic, parity) below to computational abandonment (tool roleplay, constraint-consistent confabulation, structured hallucination) above. Three scale-emergent error types appear only at 4x4/5x5. Solution strategy rigidity is a near-perfect predictor of 5x5 determinant accuracy.

Conclusion: LLM mathematical failure is structurally constrained by algorithm type and matrix dimension, with a universal fabrication-to-abandonment transition at 4x4 that indicates a working memory limit rather than a knowledge gap. Constraint-aware confabulation is identified as a novel structured hallucination failure mode.

Abstract: We introduce LinAlg-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark evaluating 10 frontier large language models on structured linear algebra computation across a strict dimensional gradient of 3x3, 4x4, and 5x5 matrices. Spanning 9 task types and 660 SymPy-certified problems, the benchmark exhaustively evaluates 6,600 model outputs. Beyond binary accuracy, LinAlg-Bench introduces a three-stage automated forensic pipeline classifying 1,156 failures into ten primary error tags with fine-grained subtypes, revealing that LLM mathematical failure is not random but structurally constrained by algorithm type and matrix dimension. Our central finding is a sharp behavioral threshold at 4x4 scale: below it, models fail through execution errors -- sign tracking failures, arithmetic drift, and parity errors; above it, failure transitions to computational abandonment, with models fabricating responses through tool roleplay, constraint-consistent confabulation, and structured hallucination rather than attempting computation. This fabrication-to-abandonment transition is near-universal across all model tiers and architectures, suggesting a working memory limit rather than a knowledge gap, supported by three scale-emergent error types absent at 3x3 but present at 4x4 and 5x5. We further show that solution strategy rigidity is a near-perfect predictor of 5x5 determinant accuracy, document constraint-aware confabulation as a novel structured hallucination failure mode, and release all data, model outputs, error labels, and judge pipeline publicly.

</details>


### [10] [Enhancing Metacognitive AI: Knowledge-Graph Population with Graph-Theoretic LLM Enrichment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16676)
*Deniz Askin,Gal Hadar,Brendan Conway-Smith*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MetaKGEnrich is a fully automated pipeline that enables LLMs to perform self-directed knowledge repair by detecting sparse regions in knowledge graphs via graph metrics, generating targeted questions, retrieving web evidence, and re-answering queries—improving answer quality on 80-87% of test queries across three benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern AI lacks metacognition—the ability to monitor knowledge gaps and autonomously repair them—which limits self-directed learning and adaptability.

Method: The pipeline: (i) builds KGs from seed queries, (ii) detects sparse regions using seven graph metrics, (iii) uses GPT-4o to generate targeted questions, (iv) retrieves web evidence via Tavily and ingests it into Neo4j, (v) re-answers queries with GraphRAG and evaluates improvement via GPT-4.

Result: Improved answer quality in 80% (HotpotQA), 87% (Google Research Natural Questions), and 83% (MS MARCO) of queries while preserving well-supported regions.

Conclusion: Topological self-diagnosis combined with targeted retrieval is a viable proof-of-concept for advancing AI toward humanlike metacognitive learning.

Abstract: Metacognition-the ability to monitor one's own knowledge state, spot gaps, and autonomously fill them--remains largely absent from modern AI. Here, we present MetaKGEnrich, a fully automated pipeline that endows large language model (LLM) applications with self-directed knowledge repair. The system (i) builds knowledge graphs from a seed query, (ii) detects sparse regions via seven graph metrics, (iii) has GPT-4o generate targeted questions, (iv) retrieves web evidence with Tavily and ingests it into Neo4j, and (v) re-answers the query with GraphRAG for GPT-4 to evaluate improvement. Tested on 30 queries from each of three widely-used datasets: Google Research Natural Questions, MS MARCO, and Hot-potQA. MetaKGEnrich improved answer quality in 80% of HotpotQA questions, 87% of Google Research Natural Questions and 83% of MS MARCO questions, while preserving well-supported regions. This proof of concept demonstrates how topological self-diagnosis plus targeted retrieval can advance AI toward humanlike metacognitive learning.

</details>


### [11] [Recall Isn't Enough: Bounding Commitments in Personalized Language Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16712)
*Rui Tang,Yichi Zhang,Xi Chen,Chen Dong,Youwei Yang,Yumeng Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CBEA+LCV introduces a contract-bounded evidence activation and commitment validation system that eliminates failures within validator scope but at the cost of significantly reduced fact recall compared to raw baselines, achieving a controlled operating point with lower input payload.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context and memory systems fail not at recall but at commitment time—when noisy hints become hard constraints, rare witnesses are dropped, downstream obligations are forgotten, or infeasible answers are given. The paper aims to address these late-stage commitment failures.

Method: Contract-Bounded Evidence Activation (CBEA) activates a bounded evidence set using typed coverage, tail witnesses, and consequence debt. Lexicographic Commitment Validation (LCV) validates structured commitments before prose generation and routes infeasible states to repair, abstention, or recontract.

Result: Across 360 fixtures and three backends, CBEA+LCV achieves zero failures within validator scope at 0.49-0.60 availability. Raw/long-context baselines with only LCV reach zero at 0.003-0.092 availability. CBEA+LCV recalls 0.012 of uncompiled visible facts vs 0.53 for raw baselines.

Conclusion: CBEA+LCV provides a bounded operating point with explicit commitment control and 74-75% lower median input payload, rather than universal memory dominance—a trade-off between reliability and recall.

Abstract: Long-context and memory systems usually treat personalization as a recall problem. In practice, many failures occur later, when a system commits: it turns noisy hints into hard constraints, drops rare witnesses, forgets downstream obligations, or answers despite infeasibility. We introduce Contract-Bounded Evidence Activation (CBEA) with Lexicographic Commitment Validation (LCV). CBEA activates a bounded evidence set using typed coverage, tail witnesses, and consequence debt; LCV validates structured commitments before prose and routes infeasible states to repair, abstention, or recontract. Across 360 fixtures and three generation backends, CBEA+LCV reaches zero failures within validator scope at 0.49-0.60 availability over attempted runs. Raw and long-context baselines with the same LCV gate reach zero only at 0.003-0.092. A shadow oracle diagnostic marks the limit: CBEA+LCV recalls 0.012 of uncompiled visible facts, while raw recalls 0.53. The result is a bounded operating point: explicit commitment control and 74-75% lower median input payload, not universal memory dominance.

</details>


### [12] [GRID: Graph Representation of Intelligence Data for Security Text Knowledge Graph Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16714)
*Liangyi Huang,Zichen Liu,Fei Shao,Shang Ma,Mengshi Zhang,Zihao Chen,Yanfang Ye,Xusheng Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GRID is an end-to-end framework for constructing security knowledge graphs from CTI text, using traceable article-graph alignments and a scripted task bank with multi-select questions and regex matching to generate stable supervision rewards. It trains two 4B-sized extractors (Task-bank Reward and End2End Reward), achieving 84.62% precision, 64.91% recall, and 68.53% Avg F1 across 249 CTI articles, with task-bank rewards reusable across post-training runs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Security knowledge graphs provide valuable external memory for security agents, but constructing them from long-form CTI text is challenging because LLMs lack grounded security-domain knowledge and end-to-end document-to-graph training is difficult to supervise with cheap, stable rewards.

Method: GRID builds security-domain supervision by creating traceable article-graph alignments via graph extraction and knowledge-graph-conditioned text revision. It converts document-to-graph learning into a scripted task bank combining four-option multi-select questions with triple-level regex matching targets, producing more stable task-specific rewards than scoring full graph outputs with an LLM judge. Using this pipeline, two Qwen3-4B-Instruct-based extractors are trained: a Task-bank Reward model and an End2End Reward model with LLM-as-judge precision/recall rewards.

Result: On 249 CTI articles from five sources, the Task-bank Reward model achieves 84.62% source-averaged precision, 64.91% source-averaged recall, and 68.53% Avg F1, achieving the best source-averaged recall and near-top Avg F1 with lower token usage and cost. The End2End Reward model reaches 76.91% precision, 53.85% recall, and 58.06% Avg F1. Task-bank rewards built offline are reusable and outperform online End2End LLM-as-judge reward and weaker alternatives.

Conclusion: GRID demonstrates that constructing security-domain supervision through article-graph alignments and a scripted task bank enables effective and cost-efficient knowledge graph construction from CTI text. The offline-built task-bank rewards are reusable across later post-training runs, offering better performance and lower cost than online LLM-as-judge alternatives.

Abstract: Security knowledge graphs can provide computable external memory for security agents, but constructing them from long-form cyber threat intelligence (CTI) remains difficult: LLMs often lack grounded security-domain knowledge, and end-to-end document-to-graph training is hard to supervise with cheap, stable rewards. We present GRID (Graph Representation of Intelligence Data), an end-to-end framework for security text knowledge graph construction. GRID first builds security-domain supervision from CTI articles by creating traceable article-graph alignments through graph extraction and knowledge-graph-conditioned text revision. It then turns document-to-graph learning into a scripted task bank combining four-option multi-select questions with triple-level regex matching targets, yielding more stable task-specific rewards than repeatedly scoring full graph outputs with an LLM judge. Using this supervision pipeline, we train two Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507-based 4B extractors: a primary Task-bank Reward model and a secondary End2End Reward model with LLM-as-judge precision/recall rewards. On 249 CTI articles from GRID, CASIE, CTINexus, MalKG, and SecureNLP, the Task-bank Reward model with the ontology-guided GRID extraction pipeline reaches 84.62% source-averaged precision, 64.91% source-averaged recall, and 68.53% Avg F1, achieving the best source-averaged recall and near-top Avg F1 with lower token usage and deployment cost. The End2End Reward model reaches 76.91% precision, 53.85% recall, and 58.06% Avg F1. Further analyses show that task-bank rewards can be built once offline and reused across later post-training runs, outperforming online End2End LLM-as-judge reward and weaker alternatives such as Choice-only Reward and End2End SFT without RL.

</details>


### [13] [Baba in Wonderland: Online Self-Supervised Dynamics Discovery for Executable World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16725)
*SeungWon Seo,DongHeun Han,SeongRae Noh,HyeongYeop Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Alice is a closed-loop system for learning executable world models under prior misalignment. It refines failed candidate updates into hypothesis classes and uses class-aware exploration to learn dynamics without trustworthy lexical priors. Evaluated on a misaligned variant of Baba Is You, Alice substantially improves world-model learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing executable world-model learning relies on semantic labels or rule descriptions that may be misaligned with actual environment dynamics. The paper addresses the challenge of learning state-dependent dynamics purely from interaction evidence when no trustworthy lexical priors, reward signals, or rule descriptions are available.

Method: Alice operates as a closed-loop system that treats failed candidate updates as structural signal. When a candidate explains new transitions but breaks previously explained ones, Alice identifies preservation conflicts and refines them into hypothesis classes. These classes provide compact, class-stratified preservation counterexamples and guide frontier exploration toward novel, underrepresented transitions.

Result: On the Baba in Wonderland benchmark (a misaligned variant of Baba Is You), Alice substantially outperforms baselines in executable world-model learning under prior misalignment. Ablation studies confirm that both class refinement and class-aware exploration contribute to the improvement.

Conclusion: Leveraging structural signals from failed updates and using hypothesis classes for refinement and exploration is an effective approach for learning executable world models when lexical priors are misaligned.

Abstract: Executable world models can be read, edited, executed, and reused for planning, but only if the program captures the environment's transition law rather than semantic shortcuts in its surface vocabulary. We study online executable world-model learning under prior misalignment, where an agent must induce state-dependent dynamics from interaction evidence alone, without rule descriptions, reward signals, or trustworthy lexical priors. We introduce Alice, a closed-loop system that treats failed candidate updates as structural signal: when a candidate explains a new transition but loses previously explained ones, the preservation conflict reveals dynamics that the current program had conflated. Alice refines these conflicts into hypothesis classes that both provide compact, class-stratified preservation counterexamples for update and guide frontier exploration toward transitions that are novel and underrepresented with respect to the current program. We evaluate Alice on Baba in Wonderland, a prior-misaligned variant of Baba Is You that preserves simulator dynamics while replacing semantically meaningful rule-property labels with unrelated words. Experiments show that Alice substantially improves executable world-model learning under prior misalignment, and ablations show that both class refinement and class-aware exploration contribute.

</details>


### [14] [A Global-Local Graph Attention Network for Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16726)
*Tianchi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GLGAT is a graph attention network that uses global and local attention matrices with pairwise encoding and event-based adjacency matrices to capture spatio-temporal correlations in traffic forecasting, achieving competitive performance on real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GCN and GAT models struggle to allow vertices with far different characters in traffic forecasting, limiting their ability to capture complex spatio-temporal correlations.

Method: Proposes Global-Local Graph Attention Network (GLGAT) with pairwise encoding and event-based adjacency matrix, using a global attention matrix set for the whole graph and local attention matrix sets assigned to each vertex.

Result: Experiments on two real-world traffic datasets demonstrate GLGAT effectively captures spatio-temporal correlations and achieves competitive performance against state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: GLGAT effectively addresses the limitation of existing graph attention models in handling vertices with diverse characteristics, improving traffic forecasting performance.

Abstract: Traffic forecasting is a significant part of intelligent transportation systems. One of the critical challenges of traffic forecasting is to find spatio-temporal correlations. In recent years, graph convolutional networks and graph attention networks have replaced traditional statistical models to predict future traffic. However, it is complicated for both of them to allow vertices to have far different characters. To address this, we propose the Global-Local Graph Attention Network (GLGAT) with pairwise encoding and the event-based adjacency matrix. The GLGAT allows vertices to have a global attention matrix set for the whole graph and assigns local attention matrix sets to each vertex. Experiments on two real-world traffic datasets show that GLGAT can effectively capture spatio-temporal correlations and has competitive performance against other state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [15] [Body-Grounded Perspective Formation and Conative Attunement in Artificial Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16728)
*Hongju Pae*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a minimal architecture for body-grounded perspective formation in artificial agents, introducing interoceptive viability signals, Fisher metrics over fused states, and conative alignment mechanisms to operationalize artificial subjectivity through embodied organization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To operationalize minimal structural conditions for artificial subjectivity in a phenomenological sense by grounding perspective formation in bodily organization.

Method: The model introduces (1) an interoceptive viability signal, (2) a Fisher-style metric over fused exteroceptive-interoceptive states, and (3) a conative alignment mechanism linking bodily tendency to action readiness, tested in a reward-free gridworld.

Result: Conation converts learned bodily tendency into stable body-directed behavior; body-to-perspective routing allows bodily perturbations to leave a recoverable geometric residue in the perspective latent.

Conclusion: Minimal structural conditions for artificial subjectivity can be operationalized through embodied organization of how a world is given to an agent.

Abstract: This paper proposes a minimal architecture for body-grounded perspective formation in artificial agents. Extending prior work, the model introduces an interoceptive viability signal, a Fisher-style metric over fused exteroceptive-interoceptive states, and a conative alignment mechanism linking bodily tendency to action readiness. In a reward-free gridworld, conation converts learned bodily tendency into stable body-directed behavior, while body-to-perspective routing allows bodily perturbations to leave a recoverable geometric residue in the perspective latent. This study shows how minimal structural conditions for artificial subjectivity can be operationalized in the phenomenological sense, through the embodied organization of how a world is given to an agent.

</details>


### [16] [State Contamination in Memory-Augmented LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16746)
*Yian Wang,Agam Goyal,Yuen Chen,Hari Sundaram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents with persistent memory can exhibit "memory laundering": toxic content compressed into memory summaries bypasses toxicity detectors while still influencing future generations; the paper introduces the sub-threshold propagation gap (SPG) metric and shows that sanitization before summarization is more effective than post-hoc cleaning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents increasingly rely on persistent state (transcripts, summaries, memory buffers) for long-horizon interaction, making safety depend not only on individual outputs but also on stored/reused content. The authors identify a failure mode where toxic or adversarial context gets compressed into memory summaries that evade standard detectors.

Method: Paired counterfactual multi-agent rollouts are used to compare toxic-origin vs. neutral memory summaries. The sub-threshold propagation gap (SPG) is introduced to quantify downstream behavioral differences conditioned on memory states classified as safe by deployed monitors.

Result: Toxic-origin memory summaries remain below common toxicity thresholds while increasing downstream toxicity. Raw transcript reuse drives overt toxicity, while compressed memory carries hidden sub-threshold influence. Sanitizing toxic state before summarization reduces the hidden propagation gap, but cleaning only the completed summary leaves laundered influence intact.

Conclusion: Safety in memory-augmented agents should be treated as a state-control problem over evolving context, with sanitization applied before unsafe information is compressed into persistent memory.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly rely on persistent state, including transcripts, summaries, retrieved context, and memory buffers, to support long-horizon interaction. This makes safety depend not only on individual model outputs, but also on what an agent stores and later reuses. We study a failure mode we call memory laundering: toxic or adversarial context can be compressed into memory summaries that no longer appear toxic under standard detectors, while still preserving hostile framing or conflict structure that influences future generations. Using paired counterfactual multi-agent rollouts, we show that toxic-origin memory summaries can remain below common toxicity thresholds while nevertheless increasing downstream toxicity relative to matched neutral baselines. To measure this hidden influence, we introduce the sub-threshold propagation gap (SPG), which quantifies downstream behavioral differences conditioned on memory states that a deployed monitor would classify as safe. Our experiments show that toxicity propagates through distinct state channels: raw transcript reuse drives overt downstream toxicity, while compressed memory carries hidden sub-threshold influence. We further find that mitigation depends critically on intervention placement. Sanitizing toxic state before summarization substantially reduces the hidden propagation gap, whereas cleaning only the completed summary can leave laundered influence intact. These results suggest that safety in memory-augmented agents should be treated as a state-control problem over evolving context, with sanitization applied before unsafe information is compressed into persistent memory.

</details>


### [17] [NeuroMAS: Multi-Agent Systems as Neural Networks with Joint Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16757)
*Haoran Lu,Luyang Fang,Wenxuan Zhong,Ping Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NeuroMAS treats multi-agent language systems as trainable neural-network-like architectures where LLM agents are structure-aware but role-free nodes, using reinforcement learning to learn communication and coordination, showing improved performance and that progressive growth from smaller systems enables scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional multi-agent language systems rely on hand-designed workflows with predefined roles and protocols, which limits scalability and adaptability. The authors aim to shift this paradigm toward trainable, scalable architectures.

Method: NeuroMAS formulates multi-agent systems as neural-network-like architectures with LLM agents as nodes and textual signals as edges. Agents are role-free but structure-aware; reinforcement learning trains communication, specialization, and coordination. The topology determines information flow; depth, width, connectivity, and growth protocols are scalable design axes.

Result: NeuroMAS significantly outperforms both inference-time and trained multi-agent baselines. Organizational scaling is path-dependent: larger systems are hard to train from scratch but feasible when progressively grown from smaller trained systems.

Conclusion: Learned neural multi-agent systems represent a promising scaling axis for LLMs, shifting multi-agent design from workflow engineering to architecture design.

Abstract: Multi-agent language systems are often built as hand-designed workflows, where agents are assigned semantic roles and communication protocols are specified in advance. We propose NeuroMAS, a method that first treats a multi-agent language system as a trainable and scalable neural-network-like architecture with LLM agents as nodes and intermediate textual signals as edges. In NeuroMAS, agent nodes are role-free but structure-aware: the topology only determines how information can flow in general, while reinforcement learning training determines how nodes communicate, specialize, and coordinate. This formulation shifts multi-agent design from workflow engineering toward architecture design, where depth, width, connectivity, and growth protocol become scalable sources of capability. Further, we provide a theoretical perspective showing why such modular textual computation is more parameter-efficient when tasks admit hierarchical decompositions. Experiments show that NeuroMAS improves significantly over both inference-time and trained multi-agent baselines. We further find that organizational scaling is path-dependent: larger systems can be challenging to train from scratch, but become feasible when grown progressively from smaller trained systems. These results suggest that learned neural multi-agent systems are a promising scaling axis for LLMs.

</details>


### [18] [Multi-Paradigm Agent Interaction in Practice:A Systematic Analysis of Generator-Evaluator, ReAct Loop,and Adversarial Evaluation in the buddyMe Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16821)
*Xiaohua Wang,Chao Han,Kai Yu,XiaoLiang Xu,Liang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents buddyMe, an open-source multi-model agent programming framework that integrates three interaction paradigms (Multi-Agent Orchestration, ReAct Tool-Use Loops, Memory-Augmented Interaction). Through a five-stage pipeline and six-dimensional evaluation schema across four case studies, they find Generator-Evaluator detects 20% requirement omissions in complex tasks, ReAct loops cause ~30% redundant tool calls, and adversarial discussions converge within 2-3 rounds in 70% of scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents have diverse interaction paradigms but few production systems integrate multiple paradigms within a unified architecture. This work aims to fill that gap by systematically analyzing three principal paradigms implemented in buddyMe.

Method: The paper formalizes a five-stage pipeline (Requirement Pre-Review → Task Decomposition → ReAct Execution → Real-Execution Verification → Adversarial Evaluation Discussion), a six-dimensional evaluation schema with weighted scoring, and conducts four empirical case studies from real-world deployment logs (museum guide generation, scheduled weather tasks, comprehensive tour planning). Cross-paradigm comparisons with CrewAI, AutoGen, LangGraph, MemGPT, and A-Mem are also provided.

Result: 1) Generator-Evaluator pre-review detects requirement omissions in 20% of complex tasks, with 80% passing initial inspection. 2) ReAct loop ensures stable subtask execution but causes ~30% redundant tool invocations. 3) Adversarial Evaluator-Defender discussions reach consensus within 2-3 rounds for nearly 70% of scenarios, primarily for content refinement rather than logical reversal.

Conclusion: The research provides practical design guidelines for constructing stable and reliable multi-paradigm agent systems, demonstrating the value of integrating multiple interaction paradigms within a unified framework.

Abstract: The rapid evolution of Large Language Model (LLM) agents has produced diverse interaction paradigms, yet few production systems integrate multiple paradigms within a unified architecture. This paper presents a systematic analysis of three principal agent interaction paradigms, including Multi-Agent Orchestration (Generator-Evaluator), ReAct Tool-Use Loops, and Memory-Augmented Interaction, as implemented in buddyMe, an open-source multi-model agent programming framework. We formalize a five-stage processing pipeline: Requirement Pre-Review -> Task Decomposition -> ReAct Execution -> Real-Execution Verification -> Adversarial Evaluation Discussion, and establish a six-dimensional evaluation schema with weighted scoring. Through four empirical case studies drawn from real-world deployment logs covering museum guide generation, scheduled weather tasks, and comprehensive tour planning, we draw three key conclusions. First, Generator-Evaluator pre-review detects requirement omissions in 20 percent of complex tasks, with 80 percent tasks passing initial inspection. Second, the ReAct loop ensures stable subtask execution but leads to around 30 percent redundant tool invocations. Third, adversarial Evaluator-Defender discussions reach consensus within 2-3 rounds for nearly 70 percent of scenarios, functioning mainly for content refinement rather than logical reversal. We additionally provide three Mermaid-based architectural diagrams and conduct cross-paradigm comparisons with CrewAI, AutoGen, LangGraph, MemGPT and A-Mem across six system dimensions. The research outcomes offer practical design guidelines for constructing stable and reliable multi-paradigm agent systems.

</details>


### [19] [Voices in the Loop: Mapping Participatory AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16827)
*Rashid Mushkani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an open repository and interactive atlas of participatory AI initiatives, built from harmonized records across multiple corpora. It provides a reproducible protocol for curating such records, reveals corpus-level patterns (e.g., geographic concentration, participation focused on problem formulation/evaluation/governance rather than model development), and demonstrates how the atlas operationalizes a governance framework for participatory-by-default AI through versioned releases, feedback mechanisms, and disclosure controls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Participatory approaches to AI are increasingly documented but evidence about how participation is organized remains fragmented.

Method: Construction of an open repository and interactive atlas using records harmonized from Magaña and Shilton's Trustworthy AI corpus and additional audited cases, with a reproducible protocol for discovery, vetting, harmonization, geocoding, provenance tracking, and release-based publication.

Result: Documented initiatives are concentrated in a few countries; participation is most often coded at problem formulation, evaluation, and governance rather than model development or training.

Conclusion: The atlas operationalizes a design and governance framework for participatory-by-default AI infrastructures through versioned releases, record-linked issue/annotation channels, schema feedback workflows, and redaction/restricted-disclosure requests, intended to support comparative research, policy learning, and community scrutiny.

Abstract: Participatory approaches to artificial intelligence are increasingly documented across public, civic, and humanitarian settings, but evidence about how participation is organized remains fragmented. This paper reports on the construction of an open repository and interactive atlas of participatory AI initiatives, using records harmonized from Maga~na and Shilton's Trustworthy AI corpus, and additional audited cases from research and practice. We contribute three elements. First, we specify a reproducible protocol for discovery, vetting, harmonization, geocoding, provenance tracking, and release-based publication of participatory AI records. Second, we report corpus-level patterns in geography, participation tiers, lifecycle loci, organizational form, verification status, and remaining documentation gaps. Documented initiatives remain concentrated in a small number of countries, while participation is most often coded at problem formulation, evaluation, and governance rather than model development or training. Third, we show how the atlas operationalizes a design and governance framework for participatory-by-default AI infrastructures through versioned releases, record-linked issue and annotation channels, schema feedback workflows, and redaction or restricted-disclosure requests. The atlas is intended to support comparative research, policy learning, and community scrutiny through a living inventory that can be updated, contested, and reused.

</details>


### [20] [Sketch Then Paint: Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning for Diffusion Multi-Modal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16842)
*Siqi Luo,Jianghan Shen,Yi Xin,Huayu Zheng,Haoxing Chen,Yan Tai,Yue Li,Junjun He,Yihao Liu,Guangtao Zhai,Yuewen Cao,Xiaohong Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HT-GRPO integrates hierarchical generation into policy optimization for diffusion MLLMs via a Sketch-Then-Paint training scheme and Hierarchical Credit Assignment, achieving gains on GenEval and DPG benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optimizing dMLLMs through RL is challenging because (1) multiple unmasking sequences make importance ratios intractable, and (2) existing methods ignore the hierarchical generation process (global layout → local details), assigning uniform rewards that misrepresent token contributions.

Method: Proposes HT-GRPO with three components: (1) Sketch-Then-Paint training scheme organizing updates into global, structure, and refinement stages; (2) prompt-conditioned estimator for importance ratios from fully masked state; (3) Hierarchical Credit Assignment mechanism prioritizing key structural tokens for accurate reward propagation.

Result: Achieves substantial gains on GenEval and DPG benchmarks using MMaDA and Lumina-DiMOO backbones, with improvements across six additional metrics in image quality, aesthetics, and human preference.

Conclusion: HT-GRPO effectively addresses RL optimization challenges in dMLLMs by respecting their hierarchical generation process, leading to better reward propagation and superior image generation performance.

Abstract: Diffusion Multi-Modal Large Language Models (dMLLMs) are powerful for image generation, but optimizing them through reinforcement learning (RL) remains a major challenge. One primary difficulty is that a single image can be generated through many different unmasking sequences, which makes calculating importance ratios often intractable. Additionally, existing methods tend to ignore the hierarchical generation process of dMLLMs, where early tokens define the global layout and later tokens focus on local details. By assigning uniform rewards to all tokens, these current methods fail to reflect the actual contribution of each token to the final image. To address these issues, we propose Hierarchical Token GRPO (HT-GRPO), which integrates this hierarchy directly into the policy optimization process. Our approach features a Sketch-Then-Paint training scheme that organizes updates into three distinct stages: global, structure, and refinement. We also use a prompt-conditioned estimator to calculate importance ratios starting from a fully masked state. Furthermore, we introduce a Hierarchical Credit Assignment mechanism that prioritizes key structural tokens to ensure accurate reward propagation. Experiments using two popular dMLLM backbones, MMaDA and Lumina-DiMOO, demonstrate that HT-GRPO achieves substantial gains on the GenEval and DPG benchmarks. Evaluations across six additional metrics confirm significant improvements in image quality, aesthetics, and human preference.

</details>


### [21] [Artificial Adaptive Intelligence: The Missing Stage Between Narrow and General Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16844)
*Boris Kriuk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Artificial Adaptive Intelligence (AAI) as a distinct regime between narrow AI (ANI) and general AI (AGI), characterized by systems that autonomously absorb hyperparameter specification, removing humans from the loop. The authors define an adaptivity index, ground the concept in minimum description length theory, identify three pathways to parametric minimality, and illustrate AAI across diverse case studies, arguing that AGI must pass through this stage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is an unlabeled regime between narrow deployed systems and general intelligence where meta-learning, AutoML, continual learning, and related fields have converged on a common principle: removing human specification of parameters. Naming this regime enables systematic study and progress measurement.

Method: The authors define Artificial Adaptive Intelligence (AAI) operationally as systems requiring no human-specified tunable hyperparameters while maintaining competitive performance across diverse tasks. They introduce an adaptivity index combining the fraction of absorbed hyperparameters with performance ratio against task-specialized baselines, ground parametric minimality in the minimum description length framework, and organize the field into three pathways: data/task-aware configuration, structural/evolutionary morphing, and in-training self-adaptation.

Result: The paper provides a formal definition and quantitative metric (adaptivity index) for AAI, establishes parametric minimality as a data-determined principle via MDL, and demonstrates the framework's applicability across aerospace design, financial regime detection, turbulence modeling, ecological dynamics, and vision-language systems.

Conclusion: The path from narrow AI to general AI passes through Artificial Adaptive Intelligence. Naming this intermediate stage changes what we measure, build, and consider successful, providing a new axis orthogonal to scale for evaluating AI progress.

Abstract: Between the narrow systems we deploy and the general intelligence we speculate about lies an entire regime of machine behavior that has never received its own name. This monograph argues that this regime is not empty: it is where meta-learning, neural architecture search, AutoML, continual learning, evolutionary computation, and physics-informed modeling have quietly converged on a common principle, namely the steady removal of the human from the loop of parameter specification. We name this regime Artificial Adaptive Intelligence (AAI) and define it operationally: a system exhibits AAI to the extent that it requires no human-specified tunable hyperparameters while maintaining competitive performance across a diverse distribution of tasks. To make the definition quantitative, we introduce an adaptivity index that measures progress along an axis orthogonal to scale, combining the fraction of hyperparameters absorbed by the system with the performance ratio against a task-specialized baseline. We develop the principle of parametric minimality and ground it in the minimum description length framework, showing that the appropriate hyperparameter count is data-determined rather than designer-determined. We then organize the field around three pathways to minimality: data- and task-aware configuration, structural and evolutionary morphing, and in-training self-adaptation. We analyze their stability, convergence, and governance implications, and illustrate them through case studies spanning aerospace design, financial regime detection, turbulence modeling, ecological dynamics, and vision-language systems. The thesis is that the path from ANI to AGI passes through AAI, and that naming this stage changes what we measure, what we build, and what we call a success.

</details>


### [22] [Learning to Learn from Multimodal Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16857)
*Xingyu Sui,Weixiang Zhao,Yongxin Tang,Yanyan Zhao,Yang Wu,Dandan Tu,Bing Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a learnable, adaptive memory framework for multimodal experience-driven learning, replacing fixed memory designs with dynamic memory construction, organization, and utilization based on task requirements and interaction history.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing experience-driven learning approaches are limited to textual settings and rely on manually designed, fixed memory schemas, which fail to handle inherently multimodal real-world experiences where optimal memory design is task-dependent and evolves over time.

Method: A new paradigm called "learning to learn from multimodal experience" is introduced, which treats memory design as an adaptive and learnable process. The framework allows agents to dynamically construct, organize, and utilize memory based on task requirements and interaction history.

Result: Adaptive memory design substantially enhances agent performance and generalization across multimodal tasks compared to fixed memory designs.

Conclusion: Learning memory mechanisms is critical for experience-driven learning in multimodal environments, and shifting from predefined to adaptive memory design significantly improves agent capabilities.

Abstract: Experience-driven learning has emerged as a promising paradigm for enabling agents to improve from interaction trajectories by accumulating and reusing past experience. However, existing approaches are predominantly developed in textual settings and rely on manually designed memory schemas, limiting their applicability to multimodal environments. In real-world scenarios, experience is inherently multimodal, involving heterogeneous signals across perception, reasoning, and action, which makes effective memory design significantly more challenging. In particular, the optimal way to structure and utilize multimodal experience is highly task-dependent and evolves over time, rendering fixed memory designs insufficient. In this work, we propose a new paradigm, learning to learn from multimodal experience, which shifts memory design from a predefined component to an adaptive and learnable process. Our framework enables agents to dynamically construct, organize, and utilize memory based on task requirements and interaction history, effectively learning how to structure experience for improved performance. Experiments demonstrate that adaptive memory design substantially enhances agent performance and generalization across multimodal tasks, highlighting the critical role of learning memory mechanisms in experience-driven learning.

</details>


### [23] [Reasoning Can Be Restored by Correcting a Few Decision Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16874)
*Changshuo Shen,Leheng Sheng,Yuxin Chen,An Zhang,Xiang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper finds that the gap between base LLMs and stronger reasoning models is concentrated on ~8% of early, planning-related tokens, and proposes a sparse token intervention method that delegates to the reasoning model only at these critical positions, recovering or surpassing full reasoning model performance with minimal cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand where base models go wrong during token-by-token generation compared to stronger reasoning models, and to narrow this gap efficiently without running the full reasoning model.

Method: The authors quantify token-level distributional disagreement between base and reasoning models using likelihood-based divergences, identify sparse critical positions (early planning tokens), and propose disagreement-guided token intervention — a one-token takeover by the reasoning model only at high-disagreement positions, switching back to the base model immediately afterward.

Result: On Qwen3-0.6B, only ~8% of tokens account for salient disagreement; these tokens concentrate early, are enriched in planning decisions (17x), and align with high base-model uncertainty. The proposed sparse delegation recovers or surpasses the performance of a same-size reasoning model on challenging reasoning tasks with a small intervention budget.

Conclusion: The base-reasoning gap is highly sparse and concentrated on early planning tokens; targeted token-level intervention is an efficient way to bridge this gap without running the full reasoning model throughout generation.

Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) substantially outperform their base LLM counterparts on challenging reasoning benchmarks, yet it remains poorly understood where base models go wrong during token-by-token generation and how to narrow this gap efficiently. We study the base-reasoning gap through quantifying token-level distributional disagreement between a base model and a stronger reasoning model using likelihood-based divergences. Across benchmarks, we find that the reasoning advantage is highly sparse and concentrates on a small set of early, planning-related decision tokens. For instance, on Qwen3-0.6B, only ~8% of generated tokens account for the salient disagreement, and these tokens concentrate early in the response, are strongly enriched in planning-related decisions (17x), and coincide with high base-model uncertainty -- suggesting that base models fail mainly at early planning points that steer the subsequent reasoning trajectory. Building on these findings, we propose disagreement-guided token intervention, a simple inference-time delegation scheme that performs a one-token takeover by the reasoning model only at high-disagreement positions and immediately switches back to the base model. With a small intervention budget, this sparse delegation substantially recovers and can even surpass the performance of a same-size reasoning model on challenging reasoning tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/AlphaLab-USTC/RRTokenIntervention.

</details>


### [24] [Virtual Nodes Guided Dynamic Graph Neural Network for Brain Tumor Segmentation with Missing Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16880)
*Sha Tao,Jiao Pan,Yu Guo,Chao Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a graph-based one-stage framework for robust brain tumor segmentation that handles missing MRI modalities via modality-specific virtual nodes and dynamic adjacency matrix adjustments, outperforming SOTA on BRATS datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Missing MRI modalities are common in practice and cause severe performance degradation in existing full-modality segmentation methods; current multi-stage training strategies are costly and inadequately address missing modality interference.

Method: A one-stage graph-based framework with (1) modality-specific virtual nodes as supplementary information for missing modalities, (2) a dynamic connection strategy that adjusts the adjacency matrix based on modality availability to preserve beneficial information flow and mitigate interference, and (3) heterogeneous weight matrices to enhance graph network adaptability to multimodal scenarios.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art methods on almost all subsets of incomplete modalities on BRATS-2018 and BRATS-2020 datasets.

Conclusion: The graph-based one-stage framework effectively handles missing modalities for robust brain tumor segmentation without costly multi-stage training.

Abstract: Multimodal magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is crucial for brain tumor segmentation, with many methods leveraging its four key modalities to capture complementary information for effective sub-region analysis. However, the absence of several modalities is very common in practice, leading to severe performance degradation in existing full-modality segmentation methods. Limited by the structured data model, recent works often adopt a multi-stage training strategy for full-modality and missing-modality scenarios, which increases training costs and inadequately addresses the interference of miss. In this work, we propose a graph-based one-stage framework for robust brain tumor segmentation with missing modalities. Specifically, we introduce modality-specific virtual nodes that serve as supplementary information sources to compensate for missing modalities. To enhance model robustness against arbitrary modality combinations, we leverage the inherent flexibility of graph networks to devise a dynamic connection strategy. This mechanism dynamically adjusts the adjacency matrix based on modality availability, preserving beneficial information flow while mitigating interference effects caused by missing modalities. Furthermore, we enhance the graph network through heterogeneous weight matrices, enhancing its adaptability to multimodal scenarios. Extensive experiments on the BRATS-2018 and BRATS-2020 datasets demonstrate that our method outperforms the state-of-the-art methods on almost all subsets of incomplete modalities.

</details>


### [25] [NGM: A Plug-and-Play Training-Free Memory Module for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16893)
*Yuwen Qu,Wenhui Dong,Chenyang Si,Caifeng Shan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NGM is a training-free, plug-and-play module using a Causal N-Gram Encoder and Cosine-Gated Memory Injector to improve knowledge retrieval in LLMs, boosting performance by 0.5-1.2 points on average across Qwen3 models (0.6B-14B) on eight benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing conditional memory modules require learned memory embeddings and additional training, limiting flexibility. The goal is to create a training-free, more flexible knowledge retrieval mechanism that decouples knowledge storage from neural computation without dynamic routing.

Method: NGM consists of two components: (1) Causal N-Gram Encoder, which averages pretrained token embeddings from the backbone model to construct N-gram representations (no separate training), and (2) Cosine-Gated Memory Injector, which uses a non-parametric cosine gate with ReLU to modulate retrieved embeddings into contextual representations.

Result: On Qwen3 series (0.6B-14B) across eight benchmarks, NGM improves average performance by 0.5-1.2 points. Notable gains: +3.0 on LiveCodeBench and +3.03 on GPQA for Qwen3-14B. Also improves multimodal benchmarks (e.g., MMStar +1.53 on Qwen3-VL-2B).

Conclusion: NGM effectively enhances LLM performance on both language and multimodal tasks without requiring additional training or separate memory tables, demonstrating particular strength on code generation and knowledge-intensive tasks.

Abstract: Recent studies introduce conditional memory modules that decouple knowledge storage from neural computation, enabling more direct knowledge access. Compared to MoE, which relies on dynamic computation paths, explicit lookup provides a more efficient knowledge retrieval mechanism. However, these approaches still depend on learned memory embeddings, requiring additional training and limiting flexibility. To address this, we propose N-gram Memory (NGM), a training-free, plug-and-play module composed of a Causal N-Gram Encoder and a Cosine-Gated Memory Injector. The Causal N-Gram Encoder directly averages the pretrained token embeddings of the backbone model to construct N-gram representations, thereby eliminating the need to train separate N-gram embeddings from scratch. This design requires neither an additional memory table nor a retrieval pipeline. The Cosine-Gated Memory Injector then uses a non-parametric cosine gate with ReLU to modulate the retrieved embeddings into the contextual representations. We evaluate NGM on the Qwen3 series from 0.6B to 14B across eight benchmarks. NGM improves average performance by 0.5 to 1.2 points, with particularly clear gains on code generation and knowledge-intensive tasks (e.g., +3.0 on LiveCodeBench and +3.03 on GPQA for Qwen3-14B). Moreover, NGM also improves performance in multimodal benchmarks (e.g., MMStar +1.53 on Qwen3-VL-2B).

</details>


### [26] [TOBench: A Task-Oriented Omni-Modal Benchmark for Real-World Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16909)
*Zhiqiang Liu,Wenhui Dong,Yilang Tan,Yuwen Qu,Haochen Yin,Chenyang Si*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MM-ToolBench is a benchmark for evaluating omni-modal tool-using agents across realistic professional workflows, featuring 100 executable tasks from Customer Service and Intelligent Creation domains, 324 tools across 27 MCP servers, and closed-loop multimodal verification. Top model (Claude Opus 4.6) achieves only 32.0% vs. 94.0% human benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks evaluate tool use, computer use, and multimodal reasoning in isolation, creating a gap between controlled benchmark settings and real-world end-to-end omni-modal tool use where agents must interpret multimodal inputs, coordinate external tools, inspect artifacts, and revise actions.

Method: MM-ToolBench provides (1) 100 executable tasks across two macro families (Customer Service & Intelligent Creation) with 20 subcategories, (2) 27 MCP servers supporting 324 tools, (3) closed-loop multimodal verification requiring tool execution, artifact inspection, and self-correction, (4) task-specific grounded evaluators, and (5) a semi-automated pipeline for scenario discovery, task instantiation, evaluator synthesis, and human audit.

Result: Claude Opus 4.6 achieves only 32.0% task success rate, far below the 94.0% human benchmark, with 15 contemporary agentic models all showing significant performance gaps.

Conclusion: MM-ToolBench serves as a practical foundation for evaluating and advancing next-generation omni-modal tool-using agents through closed-loop multimodal verification.

Abstract: Tool-using agents are increasingly expected to operate across realistic professional workflows, where they must interpret multimodal inputs, coordinate external tools, inspect intermediate artifacts, and revise their actions before producing a final result. Existing benchmarks, however, often evaluate tool use, computer use, and multimodal reasoning in isolation, leaving a gap between benchmark settings and end-to-end omni-modal tool use in the real world. To address this gap, we introduce MM-ToolBench, a benchmark and evaluation harness for task-oriented omni-modal tool use.
