MM-ToolBench contains 100 executable tasks from two macro task families, Customer Service and Intelligent Creation, covering 20 subcategory slices and supported by 27 MCP servers with 324 tools. The central design of MM-ToolBench is closed-loop multimodal verification: agents must execute tools, inspect rendered or transformed artifacts, and self-correct when outputs fail task-specific requirements. To make such evaluation scalable and verifiable, MM-ToolBench couples MCP-based execution with task-specific grounded evaluators and a semi-automated construction pipeline for scenario discovery, task instantiation, evaluator synthesis, and human audit. Experiments on 15 contemporary agentic models show that MM-ToolBench remains highly challenging: Claude Opus 4.6, commonly regarded as one of the strongest coding-agent models, achieves only 32.0% task success, far below the 94.0% human benchmark. We envision MM-ToolBench as a practical foundation for evaluating and advancing next-generation omni-modal tool-using agents through closed-loop multimodal verification.

</details>


### [27] [From Static Risk to Dynamic Trajectories: Toward World-Model-Inspired Clinical Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16927)
*Pujun Feng,Xiaoyu Guo,Seyed Ehsan Saffari,Min Hun Lee,Siew-Kei Lam,Erik Cambria,Xibin Sun,Yangtao Zhou,Tong Yang,Xiaoyu Zhang,Tao Tan,Yue Sun,Bin Cui*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a unified framework for intervention-aware disease trajectory modeling in clinical AI, integrating forecasting, counterfactual estimation, and policy evaluation while addressing treatment confounder feedback and observation bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static prediction models fail clinically because they conflate disease biology with clinician behavior, particularly under treatment confounder feedback and irregular/informative observation patterns. There is a need for decision-grade models that account for how treatments alter disease trajectories.

Method: The authors organize the field around six linked components: three decision tasks (factual forecasting, counterfactual estimation, policy evaluation) and three data-generating mechanisms (disease evolution, treatment assignment, observation process). They synthesize method families including multistate/joint models, temporal point-process, deep sequence architectures, and longitudinal causal inference.

Result: A unified framework bridging forecasting, counterfactual trajectories, and policy evaluation across discrete/continuous time, with systematic mapping of method families to components and evaluation aligned with claim strength via overlap diagnostics, uncertainty quantification, off-policy robustness, and target-trial validation.

Conclusion: This synthesis advances benchmark prediction to decision-grade clinical evidence, enabling treatment-sensitive individualized futures, pre-deployment policy stress-testing, and safer closed-loop learning health systems that adapt/abstain when evidence is insufficient.

Abstract: Clinical decision-making is a feedback system where risk estimates influence treatment, which in turn changes disease trajectories, and both shape clinicians' measurement practices. Static prediction often fails clinically: models trained on observational care logs conflate disease biology with clinician behavior, particularly under treatment confounder feedback and irregular or informative observation. This Review focuses on intervention-aware disease trajectory modeling in clinical AI--methods estimating patient-specific longitudinal disease evolution and assessing trajectory changes under alternative treatments. We organize the field around six linked components: three decision tasks (factual forecasting, counterfactual estimation, policy evaluation) and three data-generating mechanisms (disease evolution, treatment assignment, observation process) that determine identifiability. We present the first unified framework bridging forecasting, counterfactual trajectories, and policy evaluation across discrete/continuous time, explicitly addressing treatment assignment, time-varying confounding, and observation bias. We synthesize key method families (multistate/joint models, temporal point-process, deep sequence architectures, longitudinal causal inference), map them to relevant components, and align evaluation with claim strength via overlap diagnostics, uncertainty quantification, off-policy robustness, and target-trial validation. This synthesis advances benchmark prediction to decision-grade clinical evidence, enabling treatment-sensitive individualized futures, pre-deployment policy stress-testing, and safer closed-loop learning health systems that adapt/abstain when evidence is insufficient.

</details>


### [28] [How do Humans Process AI-generated Hallucination Contents: a Neuroimaging Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16953)
*Shuqi Zhu,Yi Zhong,Ziyi Ye,Bangde Du,Yujia Zhou,Qingyao Ai,Yiqun Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study records EEG from 27 participants verifying MLLM-generated image descriptions, revealing distinct neural patterns for hallucinated vs. non-hallucinated content. Misjudged hallucinations fail to trigger standard neurocognitive fact verification pathways.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite known risks of AI-generated hallucinations, the cognitive mechanisms underlying human recognition or deception by these hallucinations remain unclear.

Method: EEG signals were recorded from 27 participants performing a verification task on MLLM-generated image descriptions, analyzed via averaged event-related potential (ERP) to examine neural dynamics.

Result: Multiple cognitive processes (semantic integration, inferential processing, memory retrieval, cognitive load) show distinct patterns for hallucinated vs. non-hallucinated content. Misjudged hallucinations exhibit significantly different neural responses compared to correctly judged ones.

Conclusion: Misjudged AI-generated hallucinations fail to activate the standard neurocognitive fact verification pathway, explaining why humans can be misled.

Abstract: While AI-generated hallucinations pose considerable risks, the underlying cognitive mechanisms by which humans can successfully recognize or be misled by these hallucinations remain unclear. To address this problem, this paper explores humans' neural dynamics to characterize how the brain processes hallucinated content. We record EEG signals from 27 participants while they are performing a verification task to judge the correctness of image descriptions generated by a multi-modal large language model (MLLM). Based on an averaged event-related potential (ERP) study, we reveal that multiple cognitive processes, e.g., semantic integration, inferential processing, memory retrieval, and cognitive load, exhibit distinct patterns when humans process hallucinated versus non-hallucinated content. Notably, neural responses to hallucinations that were misjudged versus correctly judged by human participants showed significant differences. This indicates that misjudged AI-generated hallucinations failed to trigger the standard neurocognitive fact verification pathway.

</details>


### [29] [Harnessing AI for Inverse Partial Differential Equation Problems: Past, Present, and Prospects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16966)
*Zhentao Tan,Yuze Hao,Boyi Zou,Mingsheng Long,Yi Yang,Gang Bao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper provides a comprehensive survey of AI methods for solving inverse PDE problems, organized into three categories (inverse problems, inverse design, control problems), reviewing methodological paradigms, representative approaches, and applications across scientific domains, while discussing future challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inverse PDE problems are fundamental across many scientific and industrial fields (medical imaging, geophysics, materials science, aerodynamics), but traditional numerical methods face key challenges. The authors aim to provide the first unified and systematic perspective on how AI-based approaches are reshaping these problems.

Method: The paper introduces basic formulation and challenges of inverse PDE problems, categorizes them into three types (inverse problems, inverse design, control problems), reviews methodological paradigms and representative state-of-the-art AI approaches for each category, and summarizes applications across domains.

Result: A comprehensive survey covering representative AI approaches for inverse PDE problems across three categories, with applications in mechanical systems, aerodynamics, thermal systems, full-waveform inversion, system identification, and medical imaging.

Conclusion: Open challenges and future prospects are discussed, including physics-informed architectures, limited real-world data, uncertainty quantification, and inverse foundation models, highlighting how modern learning-based methods are transforming inverse PDE problem-solving.

Abstract: Solving inverse partial differential equation (PDE) problems is a fundamental topic in scientific research due to its broad significance across a wide range of real-world applications. Inverse PDE problems arise across medical imaging, geophysics, materials science, and aerodynamics, where the goal is to infer hidden causes, design structures, or control physical states. In this paper, we provide a comprehensive review of recent advances in solving inverse PDE problems using artificial intelligence (AI). We first introduce the basic formulation, key challenges, and traditional numerical foundations of inverse PDE problems, and then organize it into three major categories: inverse problems, inverse design, and control problems. For each category, we further present a methodological paradigms, and review representative state-of-the-art approaches from recent years. We then summarize representative applications across scientific and industrial domains, including mechanical systems, aerodynamic problems, thermal systems, full-waveform inversion, system identification, and medical imaging. Finally, we discuss open challenges and future prospects, such as physics-informed architectures, limited real-world data, uncertainty quantification, and inverse foundation models. This survey aims to provide the first unified and systematic perspective on AI for inverse PDE problems, demonstrating how modern learning-based methods are reshaping inverse problems, inverse design, and control problems in PDE-governed systems.

</details>


### [30] [Brain Vascular Age Prediction Using Cerebral Blood Flow Velocity and Machine Learning Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16969)
*Anni Zhao,Alex Bateh,Tyler Baldridge,Sandra Billinger,Xiao Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study uses Transcranial Doppler (TCD)-derived features and machine learning regression models trained on healthy subjects to predict cerebrovascular age and assess accelerated aging in subjects with brain diseases (acute stroke, post-stroke, Alzheimer's, mild cognitive impairment). Healthy subjects' predicted age was 3.69 years above chronological age on average, while diseased subjects showed varying acceleration levels, suggesting TCD features are relevant for evaluating accelerated cerebrovascular aging. Imbalanced datasets were noted to affect model performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To estimate chronological age from TCD-derived cerebrovascular features and assess accelerated cerebrovascular aging in subjects with various brain diseases using regression models trained on healthy subjects.

Method: 168 healthy and 277 diseased subjects had bilateral TCD recordings of the middle cerebral artery analyzed via the MOCAIP algorithm. MOCAIP-generated features and heart rate variability features were inputs to regression models for predicting brain vascular age. Diseased groups included acute stroke (66), post-stroke (27), Alzheimer's (26), mild cognitive impairment (23), and established subjects (135).

Result: The model predicted healthy subjects' cerebrovascular age as 3.69 years above chronological age on average. Different disease conditions showed varying levels of age acceleration. Imbalanced datasets affected machine-learning-based brain age prediction performance.

Conclusion: TCD-generated features are relevant for evaluating accelerated cerebrovascular aging. However, imbalanced datasets pose challenges for brain age prediction models.

Abstract: Defining vascular age in terms of physiological function has become one focal point of the extensive studies to categorize and track chronological age. Transcranial Doppler (TCD) is a method by which cerebral blood flow velocity is measured along the major arteries feeding the human brain. This study aims to use features extracted from TCD to estimate chronological age and assess accelerated aging in subjects with various brain diseases. We predict subjects with various brain diseases to present with accelerated cerebrovascular aging when tested on various regression models trained by healthy subjects. 168 healthy subjects and 277 diseased subjects with bilateral TCD recordings of the middle cerebral artery were analyzed using the Morphological Analysis and Clustering of Intracranial Pressure (MOCAIP) algorithm. MOCAIP-generated features and heart rate variability features were used as input features for regression models to predict the brain vascular age. 66 subjects with acute stroke, 27 subjects with post stroke, 26 subjects with Alzheimer's disease, 23 subjects with mild cognitive impairment, and 135 established subjects were tested against the machine learning model to assess for accelerated cerebrovascular age. The trained model, on average, predicted healthy subjects' cerebrovascular age to be 3.69 years above their chronological age. Subjects with different disease conditions exhibited varying levels of age acceleration. The differences in healthy and diseased subjects' performances suggest that features generated using TCD may be relevant when evaluating accelerated cerebrovascular aging. Moreover, imbalanced datasets have been observed to affect the performance of machine-learning-based brain age prediction models.

</details>


### [31] [A Conflict-aware Evidential Framework for Reliable Sleep Stage Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17021)
*Yunzhi Tian,Dekui Wang,Qirong Bu,Wei Zhou,Xingxing Hao,Jun Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ConfSleepNet is a conflict-aware evidential framework for multi-view sleep stage classification that resolves inter-view conflicts in multi-modal data where modalities are not well-aligned, using multi-view evidence extraction with hybrid category structures and conflict-aware aggregation to produce reliable joint decisions with uncertainty quantification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-view sleep stage classification methods assume different modalities are well-aligned, which is often unrealistic in real-world scenarios, compromising the reliability of staging results. A framework that can dynamically resolve inter-view conflicts is needed.

Method: A two-phase framework: (1) Multi-view evidence extraction with hybrid category structures tailored to each modality's inherent characteristics to learn category-related evidence; (2) Conflict-aware aggregation that constructs view-specific opinions (predictions + uncertainty) and integrates them via a novel mechanism to resolve conflicts and produce reliable joint decisions.

Result: Both theoretical analysis and experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of ConfSleepNet in sleep staging tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed conflict-aware evidential framework successfully addresses modality misalignment in multi-view sleep staging by dynamically resolving inter-view conflicts, producing more reliable sleep stage classifications with uncertainty estimates.

Abstract: Multi-view learning has been widely applied for sleep stage classification using multi-modal data. However, existing methods typically assume that different modalities are well-aligned, which is often unattainable in real-world scenarios, thereby compromising the reliability of the staging results. In this paper, we propose ConfSleepNet, a conflict-aware evidential framework that dynamically resolves inter-view conflicts. The framework consists of multi-view evidence extraction and conflict-aware aggregation. In the first phase, it learns category-related evidence from different modalities, which represents the degree of support for individual sleep stages. Considering the inherent characteristics of varying modalities, we propose hybrid category structures for different modalities to promote more reasonable evidence learning. In the second phase, view-specific opinions, including prediction results and uncertainty, are constructed from the learned evidence. Notably, we propose a novel conflict-aware aggregation method that integrates these view-specific opinions into a reliable joint decision. This mechanism can effectively resolve conflicts among opinions and synthesize them into a reliable joint decision. Both theoretical analysis and experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of ConfSleepNet in sleep staging tasks. The code is available at https://github.com/By4te/ConfSleepNet_ICML2026/.

</details>


### [32] [Reliability and Effectiveness of Autonomous AI Agents in Supply Chain Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17036)
*Carol Xuan Long,David Simchi-Levi,Feng Zhu,Huangyuan Su,Andre P. Calmon,Flavio P. Calmon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies autonomous generative AI agents in the MIT Beer Game, finding that while reasoning models can exceed human performance and reduce costs, they suffer from an "agent bullwhip effect"—amplified decision unreliability across supply chain echelons. The authors propose a GRPO-based reinforcement learning post-training framework that significantly reduces tail risks and improves reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate the performance and reliability of autonomous generative AI agents in multi-echelon supply chains, particularly identifying failure modes that emerge from multi-agent coordination.

Method: Used the MIT Beer Game as a testbed; identified four inference-time levers (model selection, policies/guardrails, centralized data sharing, prompt engineering); developed a mathematical framework for the agent bullwhip effect; proposed GRPO-based RL post-training using system-level supply-chain rewards.

Result: Reasoning models exceed human-level performance out-of-the-box; optimized models reduce costs by up to 67% vs. human teams; however, strong averages mask reliability risks due to the agent bullwhip effect (decision variance across facilities and over time); repeated sampling fails to mitigate it; GRPO post-training substantially reduces tail events and improves reliability.

Conclusion: Autonomous AI agents can outperform humans in supply chain management but introduce new reliability challenges. GRPO-based post-training effectively addresses the agent bullwhip effect, offering a path toward more dependable autonomous supply chain agents.

Abstract: This paper studies autonomous generative AI agents in multi-echelon supply chains using the MIT Beer Game. We identify four inference-time levers that shape performance: model selection, policies and guardrails, centralized data sharing, and prompt engineering. Model capability is the dominant factor: an out-of-the-box reasoning model exceeds human-level performance, and optimized reasoning models reduce costs by up to 67% relative to human teams. However, strong average performance masks substantial reliability risks. We introduce the agent bullwhip effect, the amplification of decision unreliability across echelons, manifesting along two dimensions: decision variance increases both across facilities at the same point in time and within the same facility across time. We develop a mathematical framework showing that this phenomenon is inherent to multi-agent systems that involve coordination and information delays, and we demonstrate that repeated sampling fails to meaningfully reduce it. To address this limitation, we propose a Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO)-based reinforcement-learning post-training framework that trains a shared base LLM using system-level supply-chain rewards. GRPO post-training substantially reduces tail events, curtails agent bullwhip, and improves the reliability of autonomous supply-chain agents.

</details>


### [33] [Evidential Information Fusion on Possibilistic Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17038)
*Qianli Zhou,Ye Cui,Zhen Li,Witold Pedrycz,Yong Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a reversible transformation between belief functions and possibilistic structures using a belief evolution network, then develops a general evidential fusion framework based on triangular norms that is more flexible than Dempster's rule, especially for non-distinct sources, conflict management, and heterogeneous fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dempster's rule has strong structural restrictions that limit its flexibility in handling complex source states and diverse information fusion scenarios.

Method: A reversible transformation based on the isopignistic principle between belief functions and a possibilistic structure on the power set, characterized by a belief evolution network, combined with triangular norms to create a general evidential fusion framework.

Result: The proposed framework supports more flexible combination behaviors than Dempster-based methods.

Conclusion: The new framework exhibits advantages in non-distinct source fusion, conflict management, parametric combination design, and heterogeneous information fusion.

Abstract: Dempster's rule is a fundamental tool for combining belief functions from distinct and reliable sources. However, its intersection-based semantics imposes strong structural restrictions, which limits its flexibility in handling complex source states and diverse information fusion scenarios. To overcome this limitation, we propose a reversible transformation, derived from the isopignistic principle, between belief functions and a possibilistic structure defined on the power set. In this transformation, the relationships among subsets are explicitly characterized by a belief evolution network, which provides a more flexible representation of evidential information beyond the conventional mass function structure. On this basis, we further introduce the triangular norm family to develop a general and adaptive evidential information fusion framework. Unlike fusion methods rooted in Dempster semantics, the proposed framework supports more flexible combination behaviors and exhibits advantages in non-distinct source fusion, conflict management, parametric combination design, and heterogeneous information fusion.

</details>


### [34] [PersonaArena: Dynamic Simulation for Evaluating and Enhancing Persona-Level Role-Playing in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17044)
*Wenlong Shi,Jianxun Lian,Mingqi Wu,Haiming Qin,Mingyang Zhou,Xing Xie,Naipeng Chao,Hao Liao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PersonaArena is a dynamic simulation framework for evaluating and improving LLMs' persona-level role-playing in realistic social scenarios, using a large filtered persona bank, multi-turn interactions, and a multi-agent debating judge for holistic assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM role-playing research focuses on character-level settings with static evaluations, failing to capture the complexity of realistic everyday social interactions; there is a need for dynamic, persona-level evaluation.

Method: The framework uses a large filtered corpus of user-generated social content to build a nuanced persona bank, elicits multi-turn context-rich interactions in simulated social environments, and employs a multi-agent debating judge for holistic, unbiased assessment.

Result: Extensive experiments show that PersonaArena enables rigorous evaluation and enhancement of LLMs' role-playing capabilities.

Conclusion: PersonaArena advances the development of more authentic and socially adept AI agents by enabling better evaluation and improvement of persona-level role-playing.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly serve as interactive social agents, yet their ability to maintain coherent and authentic persona-level role-playing remains limited, particularly in realistic social scenarios. Existing research predominantly focuses on character-level settings and relies on static evaluation formats, failing to capture the complexity of everyday social interactions. In this work, we present PersonaArena, a dynamic simulation framework for evaluating and improving persona-level role-playing in LLMs. PersonaArena leverages a large, filtered corpus of user-generated social content to construct a nuanced persona bank, and elicits multi-turn, context-rich interactions within simulated social environments. Our framework features a multi-agent debating judge for holistic and unbiased assessment. Through extensive experiments, we demonstrate that PersonaArena enables rigorous evaluation and enhancement of LLMs' role-playing capabilities, advancing the development of more authentic and socially adept AI agents.

</details>


### [35] [Towards Human-Level Book-Writing Capability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17064)
*Jan Zierstek,Matteo Batelic,Maya Medjad,Tim Schönenberger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a dataset and training framework for book-scale creative writing that converts supervised fine-tuning into prompt-to-book generation using public-domain novels. It creates multi-resolution planning scaffolds (premise → chapter → scene → full text) and trains long-context language models on these trajectories to shift away from assistant-style prose toward human literary writing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models optimized for instruction-following and agentic tasks are poorly aligned with creative writing requirements. Fiction often depends on behaviors (deception, moral ambiguity, unreliable narration) that assistant-tuned models are trained to avoid, resulting in stories that are structurally correct but stylistically generic, overly explanatory, or lacking human literary grounding.

Method: Starting from public-domain novels, the authors derive a multi-resolution planning scaffold by summarizing each book at progressively finer levels (premise → chapter → scene). They invert this hierarchy during training: the model learns to expand a prompt into increasingly detailed plans and finally into the original human-authored book text. A long-context language model is trained on these prompt-to-book trajectories.

Result: The framework preserves human prose as the final supervised target while using intermediate summaries to make book-scale generation learnable. The study examines whether this prompt-to-book objective shifts generation away from assistant-style prose toward human literary writing.

Conclusion: The paper proposes that by structuring training as hierarchical expansion from high-level premise to full book text, models can learn to produce more human-like literary writing rather than generic assistant-style prose, addressing the misalignment between instruction-tuned models and creative fiction requirements.

Abstract: Large language models optimized for instruction following and agentic tasks remain poorly aligned with the requirements of high-quality creative writing. Fiction frequently depends on behaviors that assistant-tuned models are explicitly trained to avoid, particularly deception, moral ambiguity, and unreliable narration. As a result, generated stories often appear structurally correct while remaining stylistically generic, overly explanatory, or weakly grounded in human literary behavior. We present a dataset construction and training framework for book-scale creative writing that reframes supervised fine-tuning as a prompt-to-book generation task grounded in human-authored fiction. Starting from public-domain novels, we derive a multi-resolution planning scaffold by summarizing each book at progressively finer levels, from a high-level premise to chapter- and scene-level structure. We then invert this hierarchy during training: the model learns to expand a prompt into increasingly detailed plans and finally into the original human-authored book text. This formulation preserves human prose as the final supervised target while using intermediate summaries to make book-scale generation learnable. We train a long-context language model on these prompt-to-book trajectories and study whether this objective shifts generation away from assistant-style prose and toward human literary writing.

</details>


### [36] [AnchorDiff: Topology-Aware Masked Diffusion with Confidence-based Rewriting for Radiology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17071)
*Shiying Yu,Jielei Wang,Guoming Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AnchorDiff is the first masked-diffusion framework for radiology report generation (RRG). It integrates knowledge-graph-derived clinical anchors into diffusion language modeling, using bidirectional context and iterative refinement to overcome the sequence bias and stereotypical outputs of autoregressive models. Key innovations include topology-aware training (protecting clinically important tokens) and inference-time rewriting (detecting/revising unstable tokens). SOTA results on MIMIC-CXR and MIMIC-RG4 benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive (AR) language models in RRG suffer from sequence bias due to their causal left-to-right dependency structure, causing models to follow stereotypical token orders and high-frequency report templates rather than fully grounding generation in image-specific evidence.

Method: (1) A masked-diffusion framework with bidirectional context and iterative refinement for RRG. (2) Topology-aware training strategy using RadGraph-derived entity hierarchies to assign differentiated masking protection and loss weights to clinically important tokens. (3) Inference-time rewriting strategy that detects unstable committed tokens via perturbation-based testing and selectively revises them during denoising.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on MIMIC-CXR and MIMIC-RG4 benchmarks.

Conclusion: Clinically anchored masked diffusion effectively addresses the limitations of autoregressive decoding in RRG, demonstrating the benefits of bidirectional context and iterative refinement grounded in clinical knowledge for radiology report generation.

Abstract: Radiology report generation (RRG) aims to automatically produce clinically accurate textual reports from medical images. Existing methods predominantly rely on autoregressive (AR) language models, whose causal dependency structure restricts generation to a unidirectional left-to-right process. This paradigm can induce sequence bias, where models tend to follow stereotypical token orders and high-frequency report templates rather than fully grounding generation in image-specific evidence. In this paper, we propose AnchorDiff, the first masked-diffusion framework for RRG that integrates knowledge-graph-derived clinical anchors into diffusion language modeling. By leveraging bidirectional context and iterative refinement, AnchorDiff mitigates the limitations of fixed-order autoregressive decoding. Specifically, we introduce a topology-aware training strategy that uses RadGraph-derived entity hierarchies to assign clinically important tokens differentiated masking protection and loss weights. We further design an inference-time rewriting strategy that detects unstable committed tokens through perturbation-based testing and selectively revises them during denoising. Extensive experiments on the MIMIC-CXR and MIMIC-RG4 benchmarks demonstrate that AnchorDiff achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance, showing the effectiveness of clinically anchored masked diffusion for radiology report generation.

</details>


### [37] [RAGA: Reading-And-Graph-building-Agent for Autonomous Knowledge Graph Construction and Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17072)
*Chengrui Han,Zesheng Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RAGA is an LLM-based autonomous framework that integrates knowledge graph construction and retrieval, using a cognitive constraint loop (Read-Search-Verify-Construct) and hybrid symbolic-vector retrieval to improve KG quality, cross-chunk semantic capture, and auditable provenance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-driven KG construction methods use stateless batch processing, which fails to capture cross-chunk semantic relations, handle entity disambiguation, and provide interpretability, leading to poor KG quality and low trust in high-stakes deployments.

Method: RAGA provides an atomic toolset for full KG lifecycle CRUD, embeds a Read-Search-Verify-Construct cognitive constraint into a ReAct tool loop, and implements a KG-vector synchronization mechanism for hybrid symbolic-vector retrieval with evidence-anchored verification linking entries to source text.

Result: Preliminary experiments on a subset of QASPER show RAGA's fusion retrieval outperforms zero-shot baselines, with KG integration improving both answer and evidence quality.

Conclusion: RAGA's framework design and experimental baseline serve as a reference for agent-driven autonomous KG construction, demonstrating measurable gains in retrieval performance and provenance quality.

Abstract: Existing LLM-driven knowledge graph (KG) construction methods predominantly employ stateless batch processing pipelines, exhibiting structural deficiencies in cross-chunk semantic relation capture, entity disambiguation, and construction process interpretability. These limitations undermine KG quality, retrieval precision, and deployment trust in high-stakes domains.
  We propose RAGA (Reading And Graph-building Agent), an LLM-based autonomous KG construction and retrieval fusion framework. RAGA provides an atomic toolset supporting full KG lifecycle CRUD operations and embeds a Read-Search-Verify-Construct cognitive constraint into a ReAct tool loop. A KG-vector synchronization mechanism enables hybrid symbolic-vector retrieval, while evidence-anchored verification links every knowledge entry to its source text for auditable provenance.
  Preliminary experiments on a subset of the QASPER scientific QA dataset indicate that RAGA's fusion retrieval outperforms zero-shot baselines, with KG integration providing measurable gains in both answer and evidence quality. The framework design and experimental baseline serve as a reference for agent-driven autonomous KG construction.

</details>


### [38] [Scientific Logicality Enriched Methodology for LLM Reasoning: A Practice in Physics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17104)
*Zhaoxin Yu,Nan Xu,Kun Chen,Jiahao Zhao,Lei Wang,Wenji Mao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates the internal logicality of LLM scientific reasoning, develops a logicality-enriched methodology with assessment criteria and data sampling methods, uses physics as an exemplar discipline, and shows that training with logicality-enriched data improves both logical faithfulness and task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM scientific reasoning research focuses on boosting QA performance with larger datasets and extended reasoning chains, but neglects the essence of scientific reasoning—logicality, which is the rational foundation ensuring valid reasoning steps leading to reliable conclusions.

Method: The authors develop a scientific logicality-enriched methodology including assessment criteria and data sampling methods for logicality-guided training. They extract scientific problems from academic literature and sample a high-quality dataset exhibiting strong logicality, using physics as an exemplar discipline.

Result: Experiments on three different backbone LLMs show that: 1) the constructed training data effectively improves scientific logicality in LLM reasoning; and 2) enriched scientific logicality plays a critical role in solving scientific problems.

Conclusion: Logicality-enriched training is effective for improving both the logical faithfulness and task performance of LLMs in scientific reasoning, with physics serving as a successful testbed for the proposed methodology.

Abstract: With the continuous advancement of reasoning abilities in Large Language Models (LLMs), their application to scientific reasoning tasks has gained significant research attention. Current research primarily emphasizes boosting LLMs' performance on scientific QA benchmarks by training on larger, more comprehensive datasets with extended reasoning chains. However, these approaches neglect the essence of the scientific reasoning process -- logicality, which is the rational foundation to ensure the validity of reasoning steps leading to reliable conclusions. In this work, we make the first systematic investigation into the internal logicality underlying LLM scientific reasoning, and develop a scientific logicality-enriched methodology, including a set of assessment criteria and data sampling methods for logicality-guided training, to improve the logical faithfulness as well as task performance. Further, we take physics, characterized by its diverse logical structures and formalisms, as an exemplar discipline to practise the above methodology. For data construction, we extract scientific problems from academic literature and sample a high-quality dataset exhibiting strong logicality. Experiments based on three different backbone LLMs reveal that: 1) the training data we constructed can effectively improve the scientific logicality in LLM reasoning; and 2) the enriched scientific logicality plays a critical role in solving scientific problems. Code is available at \href{https://github.com/ScienceOne-AI/PhysLogic}{https://github.com/ScienceOne-AI/PhysLogic}.

</details>


### [39] [Capturing LLM Capabilities via Evidence-Calibrated Query Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17110)
*Fangzhou Wu,Sandeep Silwal,Qiuyi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ECC is an algorithm that calibrates semantic embeddings with posterior model comparisons to create capability-aware query clusters for LLM evaluation, outperforming existing methods by ~18 percentage points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing query clustering methods rely on semantic taxonomies or embeddings that misalign with actual model performance, failing to capture latent capability requirements needed for capability-aware LLM evaluation.

Method: ECC calibrates prior semantic embeddings using limited posterior model comparisons, characterizes clusters via capability profiles parameterized by a Bradley-Terry model, and uses trainable mixture weights to handle queries with mixed capability demands, jointly learning a flexible clustering structure.

Result: ECC outperforms human-labeled and embedding-based baselines by an average of 17.64 and 18.02 percentage points respectively, and proves effective in downstream tasks such as query routing.

Conclusion: ECC significantly improves LLM capability ranking quality and demonstrates practical utility in downstream applications, validating that bridging semantic embeddings with model performance data yields better capability-aware clustering.

Abstract: Query clustering organizes queries into groups that reflect shared latent capability demands, enabling capability-aware LLM evaluation. Existing clustering methods, which primarily rely on semantic taxonomies or embeddings, often fail to capture such latent capability requirements due to a misalignment between surface-level semantics and actual model performance. We propose ECC, an algorithm that calibrates prior semantic embeddings using limited posterior model comparisons to bridge the gap between surface-level semantics and latent capability requirements. ECC characterizes each cluster through a capability profile parameterized by a Bradley-Terry model and uses trainable mixture weights to accommodate queries with mixed capability demands, jointly learning a flexible, capability-aware clustering structure that supports query-specific inference of LLM capabilities. Extensive quantitative and qualitative evaluations demonstrate that ECC significantly improves LLM capability ranking quality, outperforming human-labeled and embedding-based baselines by an average of 17.64 and 18.02 percentage points, respectively, and proves effective in downstream tasks such as query routing.

</details>


### [40] [F2IND-IT! -- Multimodal Fuzzy Fake Indian News Detection using Images and Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17115)
*Kushal Trivedi,Murtuza Shaikh,Khushi Singh,Jeevaraj S.*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multimodal fake news detection framework for Indian media combining ResNet-50 (visual), DistilBERT (textual), ANFIS (fuzzy scoring), and attention-based fusion, outperforming prior methods on the IFND dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biased manipulation of facts across regional and national media outlets complicates misinformation detection in diverse landscapes like India, necessitating a robust multimodal approach.

Method: The framework uses ResNet-50 CNN for visual feature extraction, DistilBERT for textual semantic embeddings, an ANFIS for fuzzy reliability scoring, and a lightweight attention-based fusion module to assign learnable weights to each modality before classification.

Result: Evaluated on the IFND dataset, the model demonstrates superior performance in accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-scores compared to previous research.

Conclusion: The multimodal architecture with attention-based fusion and fuzzy scoring is effective for fake news detection in the Indian media context.

Abstract: Biased manipulation of facts across regional and national media outlets complicates misinformation detection in diverse landscapes like India. This paper introduces a novel multimodal framework combining visual and textual modalities for enhanced fake news detection on Indian media. The architecture utilizes a ResNet-50 Convolutional Neural Network to extract visual features from news images, a DistilBERT encoder to obtain textual semantic embeddings, and an Adaptive Neuro-Fuzzy Inference System (ANFIS) to generate a fuzzy reliability score. A lightweight attention-based fusion module assigns learnable weights to each modality prior to classification. Evaluated on the IFND dataset, the proposed model is validated through an in-depth comparative analysis against previous research. Experimental results demonstrate superior performance across accuracy, precision, recall, and $F_1$-scores, confirming the efficacy of the architecture.

</details>


### [41] [Latent Heuristic Search: Continuous Optimization for Automated Algorithm Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17137)
*Cheikh Ahmed,Mahdi Mostajabdaveh,Zirui Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a continuous heuristic discovery framework that shifts optimization from discrete program syntax to a learned latent manifold using an encoder, differentiable surrogate model, normalizing flow, and learned mapper to condition a frozen LLM, achieving competitive performance on TSP, CVRP, KSP, and OBP against discrete evolutionary baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based evolutionary methods search in discrete program syntax space via stochastic sampling, which navigates a highly non-convex optimization landscape inefficiently.

Method: (1) Encode discrete programs into continuous embeddings; (2) Train a differentiable surrogate model for gradient-based performance prediction; (3) Use invertible normalizing flow to map embeddings to a structured Gaussian prior for gradient ascent; (4) Project optimized latent vectors via a learned mapper into soft prompts; (5) Condition a frozen LLM to synthesize executable heuristics.

Result: Continuous latent-space optimization achieves performance competitive with state-of-the-art discrete evolutionary baselines on TSP, CVRP, KSP, and OBP.

Conclusion: The proposed continuous heuristic discovery framework offers a complementary methodological alternative for automated algorithm design, demonstrating that gradient-based search in a learned latent manifold is viable and competitive.

Abstract: The integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) into evolutionary frameworks has established a new paradigm for automated heuristic discovery. Despite their promise, these methods typically search in the discrete space of program syntax, relying on stochastic sampling to navigate a highly non-convex optimization landscape. This work proposes a continuous heuristic discovery framework that shifts optimization to a learned latent manifold. We employ an encoder to map discrete programs into continuous embeddings and train a differentiable surrogate model to predict performance, enabling gradient-based search. To regularize the optimization trajectory, an invertible normalizing flow maps these embeddings to a structured Gaussian prior, where we perform gradient ascent. The resulting optimized latent vectors are projected through a learned mapper into soft prompts, which condition a frozen LLM to synthesize novel executable heuristics. We evaluate the proposed method on the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP), the Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP), the Knapsack Problem (KSP), and Online Bin Packing (OBP). Empirical results demonstrate that continuous latent-space optimization achieves performance competitive with state-of-the-art discrete evolutionary baselines while offering a complementary methodological alternative for automated algorithm design. The implementation code is available at \url{https://github.com/cheikh025/LHS}.

</details>


### [42] [Dynamics of collective creativity in AI art competitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17141)
*Mason Youngblood,Jeff Nusz,Joel Simon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study analyzes 130,882 images from 368 Artbreeder "remix parties" over 13 months, finding that human-AI co-created images become simpler and converge toward common thematic attractors. While novel parents produce more novel/complex children that attract more likes, users paradoxically prefer remixing less novel/complex images. Larger parties generate more novelty but lower complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how creativity and novelty emerge in networked, human-AI collaborative cultural production systems, as mechanisms of cultural evolution are difficult to infer from historical records, and prior iterated learning experiments have focused on linear transmission chains rather than network dynamics involving AI.

Method: Analysis of a large dataset (130,882 images from 368 remix parties over 13 months) from the Artbreeder platform, where users iteratively build on each other's AI-generated images in branching lineages. Measures included image complexity, novelty, thematic convergence, and user engagement (likes, remix preferences).

Result: Images become simpler and converge toward common thematic attractors over iterations. Novel parent images yield more novel/complex children that receive more likes, yet users preferentially remix less novel/complex images. Larger remix parties produce greater novelty but at the cost of lower complexity.

Conclusion: Human-AI collaborative cultural evolution exhibits tension between novelty production and complexity maintenance, with network size and user remix preferences shaping the trajectory of creative artifacts in ways distinct from linear transmission chains.

Abstract: Creativity is a fundamental aspect of how culture evolves, yet the mechanisms by which groups produce novelty are notoriously difficult to infer from the historical record. Iterated learning experiments have shown that cultural transmission reliably distorts artifacts toward the inductive biases of learners, but most of this work uses linear chains between human participants, leaving open how these dynamics play out in the networked, human-AI systems that increasingly shape cultural production. In this study, we leverage one such system, Artbreeder, which hosts daily "remix parties" where users iteratively build on each other's work from a single seed image, producing branching lineages of human-AI co-created images. We analyze a dataset of 130,882 images from 368 remix parties over 13 months and find that images become simpler and converge toward common thematic "attractors" (e.g., steampunk scenes, alien architecture). We also find that while more novel "parent" images produce more novel and complex "children" that attract more likes, users paradoxically prefer to remix images that are less novel and complex. Finally, larger remix parties produce more novelty at the cost of lower complexity.

</details>


### [43] [MADP: A Multi-Agent Pipeline for Sustainable Document Processing with Human-in-the-Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17159)
*Diego Gosmar,Giovanni Zenezini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MADP is a multi-agent architecture for automated document processing that combines deep learning, LLMs, and human validation, achieving 97% automation rate, 70% FTE reduction, and ~69% reduction in environmental impact compared to manual processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Document processing automation in enterprise settings remains challenging due to the labor-intensive and error-prone nature of traditional manual approaches. The authors aim to address this by developing a system that reduces human effort while maintaining high accuracy.

Method: MADP uses five specialized agents (Classificator, Splitter, Parser, Extraction, Validator) with a Human-in-the-Loop mechanism and a novel Prompt Fine Tuning with Feedback Inheritance (PFTFI) approach. Multiple LLM backends (Granite-Docling, Mistral-Small, DeepSeek-OCR) are benchmarked.

Result: Production deployment on 955 documents achieved 97.0% full-pipeline automation rate (3% non-AI fallback). Ablation on 100-document subset showed 98.5% accuracy with HITL. Operational analysis on 100K invoices/year indicates ~70% FTE reduction. Sustainability analysis shows 69% reduction in CO2 and energy, 63% reduction in water usage.

Conclusion: The hybrid AI+HITL approach demonstrates significant improvements in automation efficiency, accuracy, and environmental sustainability over traditional manual document processing, with practical deployment insights from multiple LLM backends.

Abstract: Document processing automation remains a critical challenge in enterprise environments, where traditional manual approaches are labor-intensive and error-prone. We present MADP, a multi-agent architecture that addresses the challenge of automating document processing in enterprise settings by combining deep learning-based classification and parsing with large language model extraction, while maintaining accuracy through selective human validation. Our system integrates five specialized agents--Classificator, Splitter, Parser, Extraction, and Validator--with a Human-in-the-Loop (HITL) mechanism and a novel Prompt Fine Tuning with Feedback Inheritance (PFTFI) approach. The operational analysis on a production use-case scenario of 100,000 invoices per year indicates a potential reduction of Full-Time Equivalent (FTE) requirements by approximately 70%. Production deployment on 955 real-world documents processed through January 2026 achieves a 97.0% full-pipeline automation rate, with only 3% requiring non-AI fallback. Ablation evaluation on a stratified 100-document subset (5 documents per each of 20 supplier/document-type categories) demonstrates that the full MADP configuration with Human-in-the-Loop supervision attains 98.5% document-level accuracy. Additionally, we present a comprehensive sustainability analysis showing that our hybrid AI+HITL approach reduces CO2 emissions by 69%, energy consumption by 69%, and water usage by 63% compared to traditional manual processing. Benchmark comparisons of multiple LLM backends (Granite-Docling, Mistral-Small, DeepSeek-OCR) provide practical insights for deployment in production environments.

</details>


### [44] [From Imitation to Interaction: Mastering Game of Schnapsen with Shallow Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17162)
*Ján Klačan,Sizhong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether shallow neural network agents can master the card game Schnapsen and challenge a strong search-based baseline (RdeepBot). Supervised learning (MLPBot) fails to generalize against strong opponents, while reinforcement learning (RLBot) produces substantially stronger agents. Best performance is achieved when RLBot's learned value function is combined with deeper lookahead during gameplay.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether shallow neural network agents can master the card game Schnapsen and challenge a strong search-based baseline using Monte Carlo sampling and lookahead search.

Method: Progressive experimental design: (1) supervised learning agent (MLPBot) trained on replay data, (2) reinforcement learning agent (RLBot) with shallow architecture trained via asynchronous Monte Carlo updates and experience replay.

Result: Supervised imitation does not generalize well against strong RdeepBot opponents; reinforcement learning produces substantially stronger agents. Best performance achieved when RLBot's learned value function is combined with deeper lookahead. In sample-based setting, gains are conditional with strongest performance at a relatively lower num_samples parameter.

Conclusion: Shallow neural network agents can challenge strong search-based baselines in Schnapsen, but only through reinforcement learning (not supervised imitation), and optimal performance requires combining learned value functions with deeper lookahead search.

Abstract: This paper investigates whether shallow neural network agents can master the card game Schnapsen and challenge a strong search-based baseline, RdeepBot, which uses Monte Carlo sampling and lookahead search. Guided by a progressively more complex experimental design, we first evaluate a supervised learning agent (MLPBot) trained on replay data and then a reinforcement learning agent (RLBot) with the same shallow architecture trained through asynchronous Monte Carlo updates and experience replay. The results show that supervised imitation does not generalize well enough to defeat strong RdeepBot opponents, whereas reinforcement learning produces substantially stronger agents. In the setting that focuses on the depth parameter of RdeepBot, the best performance is achieved when the learned value function is combined with deeper lookahead during gameplay, allowing RLBot to achieve statistically significant higher winning rates against the strongest evaluated RdeepBot baseline. In the sample-based setting, the gains are more conditional: the strongest performance appears at a relatively lower training num_samples parameter rather than increasing uniformly with stronger sampling.

</details>


### [45] [Responsible Agentic AI Requires Explicit Provenance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17169)
*Jinwei Hu,Xinmiao Huang,Qisong He,Youcheng Sun,Yi Dong,Xiaowei Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that explicit provenance across the full agentic lifecycle is necessary to make responsibility computable, traceable, and actionable in agentic AI systems, addressing responsibility gaps through causal attribution and intervention mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public trust in agentic AI is eroding because responsibility remains subjective and unenforced; no current framework produces quantifiable, traceable, or interventionable provenance needed to assign responsibility when harm emerges from compositions no single party designed.

Method: The paper advances four axes: (1) establishing why provenance is structurally necessary via sociotechnical responsibility gap analysis, (2) formalizing what provenance encodes through a causal attribution function and responsibility tensor, (3) discussing how provenance is computable across four lifecycle layers with preliminary online experiments, and (4) examining who bears responsibility via a concrete incident.

Result: Preliminary experiments show provenance is estimable and interveneable online before irreversible harm accumulates.

Conclusion: Explicit provenance is not a discretionary refinement but a necessary condition for responsible agentic AI, and no stakeholder in its ecosystem can treat it as optional.

Abstract: Agentic AI is rapidly proliferating across diverse real-world domains such as software engineering, yet public trust has not kept pace. The central reason is that responsibility, despite being widely discussed, remains a subjective and unenforced concept, as no current agentic framework produces the quantifiable, traceable, and interventionable provenance needed to assign it when harm emerges from compositions no single party designed. We position that what is missing is not better benchmark-level evaluation but $\textbf{explicit provenance}$ across the full agentic lifecycle, which is the only viable basis for making responsibility computable and actionable. We advance this agenda along four axes: establishing $\textit{why}$ such provenance is a structural necessity by identifying responsibility gaps across sociotechnical dimensions, formalizing $\textit{what}$ it must encode through a causal attribution function and responsibility tensor, discussing $\textit{how}$ it can be made computable across four lifecycle layers with preliminary experiments showing that provenance is estimable and interveneable online before irreversible harm accumulates, and examining $\textit{who}$ bears responsibility through a concrete agentic incident. Explicit provenance is not a discretionary refinement but the necessary condition for responsible agentic AI, and no stakeholder across its ecosystem can afford to treat it as optional.

</details>


### [46] [CAREBench: Evaluating LLMs' Emotion Understanding by Assessing Cognitive Appraisal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17176)
*Zhaoyue Sun,Hainiu Xu,Andero Uusberg,James J. Gross,Petr Slovak,Yulan He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAREBench is a benchmark for evaluating LLMs' emotion understanding through complete inferential chains based on appraisal theory, revealing that while strong models match humans on some tasks, they fall short on appraisal reasoning, positive emotion recognition, and capturing subjective heterogeneity, suggesting that standard emotion prediction metrics overestimate true understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLMs' emotion understanding rely on discrete emotion label prediction, which fails to capture the cognitive processes underlying emotion generation. The authors aim to create a benchmark grounded in appraisal theory that assesses the complete inferential chain of emotion understanding.

Method: The authors introduce CAREBench, a benchmark with complete inferential chain annotations (appraisal reasoning, appraisal ratings, multi-label emotion) from first- and third-person perspectives on real-world narratives. They propose a process-level evaluation framework and conduct systematic experiments across six LLMs organized around four research questions.

Result: Stronger models match or surpass human observers on certain tasks but fall short on appraisal reasoning and positive emotion recognition. Performance across chain steps and sensitivity to appraisal interventions exhibit dissociations across models. Current models have not internalized mechanisms needed to capture human subjective heterogeneity.

Conclusion: Downstream emotion prediction metrics may overestimate LLMs' true emotion understanding. CAREBench provides a foundation for more diagnostically informative evaluation of LLMs' affective cognitive capabilities.

Abstract: Emotion understanding is a core capability for LLMs to interact effectively with humans, yet existing evaluation paradigms rely on discrete emotion label prediction and fail to capture the cognitive processes underlying emotion generation. Grounded in appraisal theory, we introduce CAREBench, the first benchmark with complete inferential chain annotations from both first- and third-person perspectives on real-world narratives, spanning appraisal reasoning, appraisal ratings, and multi-label emotion annotation. We propose a process-level evaluation framework and conduct systematic experiments across six LLMs organized around four research questions. We find that stronger models match or surpass human observers on certain tasks, yet fall short on appraisal reasoning and positive emotion recognition; performance across chain steps and sensitivity to appraisal interventions exhibit dissociations across models; and current models have not internalized the mechanisms needed to capture human subjective heterogeneity. These findings suggest that downstream emotion prediction metrics may overestimate LLMs' true emotion understanding, and CAREBench provides a foundation for more diagnostically informative evaluation of LLMs' affective cognitive capabilities.

</details>


### [47] [Towards Robust Argumentative Essay Understanding via TIDE: An Interactive Framework with Trial and Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17247)
*Zheqin Yin,Yupei Ren,Yadong Zhang,Yujiang Lu,Man Lan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TIDE is a framework that improves prompt optimization for argument analysis tasks by integrating trial and debate mechanisms, achieving better performance on essay scoring, argument detection, and relation identification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Limited work exists on accurately understanding and evaluating argumentative essays via prompt optimization; existing criteria-based prompt optimization suffers from noisy training data and optimization instability.

Method: TIDE integrates TrIal and DEbate mechanisms into criteria-based prompt optimization to mitigate noisy data influence and enhance optimization stability.

Result: Improved performance across three tasks: Automated Essay Scoring, Argument Component Detection, and Argument Relation Identification.

Conclusion: Combining prompt-based methods with trial-and-debate mechanisms shows strong potential for advanced argument understanding in text analysis.

Abstract: Argumentative essays serve as a vital medium for assessing critical thinking and reasoning skills, yet there is limited works on accurately understanding and evaluating such texts via prompt. In this work, we propose TIDE, a novel framework designed to improve criteria-based prompt optimization for argument-related tasks by integrating TrIal and DEbate mechanism. Our method addresses key limitations of criteria-based prompt optimizing by mitigating the influence of noisy training data and enhancing optimization stability. We evaluate TIDE on three core tasks: Automated Essay Scoring, Argument Component Detection, and Argument Relation Identification. Results demonstrate that our framework improves performance across tasks. These findings underscore the potential of combining prompt-based methods for advanced argument understanding.

</details>


### [48] [CatalyticMLLM: A Graph-Text Multimodal Large Language Model for Catalytic Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17254)
*Yanjie Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QE-Catalytic-V2 is a unified graph-text multimodal LLM for catalytic materials that integrates property prediction and inverse structural design into a single model with shared representation space, enabling closed-loop optimization that outperforms decoupled baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional approaches decouple property prediction and inverse design, leading to representation space inconsistencies, training objective mismatches, data distribution shifts, and evaluator bias that limit closed-loop optimization stability.

Method: Proposes QE-Catalytic-V2, a unified graph-text multimodal large language model that jointly models property prediction and inverse design within the same model and shared representation space, leveraging 3D structures and textual information.

Result: The unified paradigm outperforms decoupled baselines on both catalytic relaxed-energy prediction and inverse design tasks.

Conclusion: Jointly modeling property prediction and structure generation within a single multimodal model is effective for catalytic materials, enabling a stable closed-loop optimization workflow of inverse design, prediction, screening, and redesign.

Abstract: Property prediction and inverse structural design of catalytic materials are typically modeled as two independent tasks: the former predicts target properties from given structures, whereas the latter generates candidate structures according to desired properties. Although the decoupled paradigm facilitates the implementation of a ``generation--evaluation--screening'' workflow, the inconsistency between the generative model and the property prediction model in terms of representation spaces and training objectives can readily introduce data distribution shifts and evaluator bias, thereby limiting the stability of closed-loop optimization.
  In this work, we propose QE-Catalytic-V2, a unified graph--text multimodal large language model for catalytic materials, which integrates property prediction and inverse design within the same model and shared representation space. Under this unified framework, QE-Catalytic-V2 can not only perform reliable property prediction by leveraging three-dimensional structures and textual information, but also generate and screen physically feasible CIF candidates conditioned on target properties, thereby forming a closed-loop optimization workflow of ``inverse design--prediction--screening--redesign.'' Experimental results demonstrate that this unified paradigm outperforms decoupled baselines on both catalytic relaxed-energy prediction and inverse design tasks, validating the effectiveness of jointly modeling property prediction and structure generation within a single multimodal model.

</details>


### [49] [CAM-Bench: A Benchmark for Computational and Applied Mathematics in Lean](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17255)
*Wentao Long,Yunfei Zhang,Chenyi Li,Li Zhou,Chumin Sun,Zaiwen Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAM-Bench is a Lean 4 theorem-proving benchmark with 1,000 proof targets in computational and applied mathematics (optimization, numerical linear algebra, numerical analysis), built via a dependency-recovery pipeline that reconstructs local textbook context. Evaluation reveals LLMs struggle with tracking local assumptions, applying elementary results, decomposing proofs, and long-horizon control in Lean.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing formal theorem-proving benchmarks focus on Olympiad-style problems and algebraic domains, leaving computational and applied mathematics (which rely on locally introduced definitions, notation, algorithms, and elementary results) underrepresented.

Method: A dependency-recovery pipeline reconstructs local textbook context, normalizes each problem into a standalone informal theorem, and translates it into a Lean 4 target. Validation is done through Lean compilation and semantic review checking both formal correctness and semantic alignment.

Result: 1,000 Lean proof targets in computational and applied mathematics; evaluation of LLMs and formalization agents showing common failure modes in tracking local assumptions, applying elementary results, decomposing proofs, and long-horizon control.

Conclusion: CAM-Bench complements existing formal mathematics benchmarks by targeting applied mathematics problems that rely on textbook concepts and elementary theorems not directly available as standard Mathlib4 lemmas, revealing significant gaps in current LLM formalization capabilities.

Abstract: Formal theorem-proving benchmarks enable mechanically verifiable evaluation of mathematical reasoning in large language models. However, existing benchmarks mainly focus on Olympiad-style problems and algebraic domains, leaving computational and applied mathematics underrepresented. We introduce CAM-Bench, a Lean 4 theorem-proving benchmark of 1,000 Lean proof targets in computational and applied mathematics, with coverage spanning optimization, numerical linear algebra, and numerical analysis. These problems are adapted from textbook exercises and often depend on locally introduced definitions, notation, algorithms, and elementary results. To construct CAM-Bench, we develop a dependency-recovery pipeline that reconstructs the local textbook context needed to state each problem faithfully. It then normalizes each problem into a standalone informal theorem and translates it into a Lean target. We validate the resulting formal problems through Lean compilation and semantic review, checking both formal correctness and semantic alignment with the original exercises. For each problem, we release the raw exercise, recovered context, normalized informal theorem, and final Lean target. CAM-Bench complements existing formal mathematics benchmarks by targeting applied mathematics problems that rely on textbook concepts and elementary theorems, many of which are not directly available as standard Mathlib4 lemmas. We evaluate widely used large language models and formalization agents on CAM-Bench, and analyze common failure modes in tracking local assumptions, applying elementary results, decomposing proofs, and maintaining long-horizon control in Lean.

</details>


### [50] [Is VLA Reasoning Faithful? Probing Safety of Chain-of-Causation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17268)
*Nicanor Mayumu,Xiaoheng Deng,Patrick Mukala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper systematically evaluates faithfulness in VLA driving models, finding only 42.5% reasoning fidelity, 94 missed pedestrians in 1/3 of scenes, 97.7% trajectory fragility under mild perturbations, and 48.3% reasoning-action consistency—with 37.9% of "stop" claims resulting in continued driving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To conduct the first systematic study of faithfulness in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) driving models, addressing the gap in understanding whether these models' reasoning and actions are reliable and aligned with reality.

Method: Analysis of 300 Alpamayo-R1-10B inferences across 100 PhysicalAI-AV scenarios, using information-theoretic formalization of faithfulness, entity/action fidelity verification criteria, and evaluation of reasoning fidelity, pedestrian detection, trajectory robustness, and reasoning-action consistency.

Result: Overall reasoning fidelity of 42.5%, 94 missed pedestrians across one-third of pedestrian-relevant scenes, 97.7% trajectory fragility under mild visual perturbations, and only 48.3% mean reasoning-action consistency with 53.3% of inferences exhibiting low consistency—including 37.9% of stop-claimed cases where the model continues driving.

Conclusion: VLA driving models exhibit significant unfaithfulness across multiple dimensions, motivating a proposed four-component safety architecture to address these reliability gaps.

Abstract: We present the first systematic study of faithfulness in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) driving models, analyzing 300 Alpamayo-R1-10B inferences across 100 diverse PhysicalAI-AV scenarios. Our main finding is that output natural-language rationales with trajectories may be significantly unfaithful: (i) overall reasoning fidelity is only 42.5%, with Chain-of-Causation matching scene reality less than half the time; (ii) 94 missed pedestrians in one-third of pedestrian-relevant scenes; (iii) 97.7% trajectory fragility under mild visual perturbations; and (iv) only 48.3% mean reasoning-action consistency, with 53.3% of inferences exhibiting low consistency, including 37.9% of stop-claimed cases where the model continues instead. We formalize faithfulness information-theoretically, define entity and action fidelity with verification criteria, and outline a four-component safety architecture aligned with these results.

</details>


### [51] [A2RBench: An Automatic Paradigm for Formally Verifiable Abstract Reasoning Benchmark Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17278)
*Qingchuan Ma,Yuexiao Ma,Yongkang Xie,Tianyu Xie,Xiawu Zheng,Rongrong Ji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A2RBench is an automated pipeline for generating, expanding, evaluating, and analyzing abstract reasoning tasks for LLMs. It uses programmatic verification (cycle consistency) to eliminate hallucinations, and finds that current LLMs significantly underperform humans (39.8% vs. 68.5%), struggle with high-dimensional (3D) tasks, and counterintuitively benefit from higher-complexity inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for abstract reasoning rely on expensive manual annotation (limiting scale) or risk measuring memorization rather than genuine reasoning. There is a need for an automated, scalable method that reliably measures abstract reasoning without confounds.

Method: A2RBench pipeline: (1) Generation: LLMs create diverse reasoning tasks. (2) Expansion: LLMs reuse validated rules and expand input spaces to scale tasks. (3) Programmatic verification via cycle consistency (inverse operation reverses forward operation) eliminates hallucinations and guarantees unique solutions. (4) Evaluation and analysis across mainstream LLMs.

Result: (1) Top LLMs score 39.8% vs. humans 68.5% on representative subsets. (2) LLMs fall far short on 3D tasks compared to 2D and 1D, showing lack of high-dimensional understanding. (3) Higher information complexity in inputs can simplify the reasoning process for LLMs.

Conclusion: Current LLMs have fundamental deficiencies in abstract reasoning, especially in high-dimensional tasks. The cycle-consistency verification is an effective method for ensuring valid task generation at scale. The counterintuitive finding about input complexity suggests new directions for designing reasoning benchmarks.

Abstract: Abstract reasoning ability reflects the intelligence and generalization capacity of LLMs to extract and apply abstract rules. However, accurately measuring this ability remains challenging: existing benchmarks either rely on expensive manual annotation, limiting their scale, or risk measuring memorization rather than genuine reasoning. To address this, we introduce an automated pipeline named A2RBench, encompassing generation, expansion, evaluation, and analysis. Specifically, in the generation stage, LLMs create diverse tasks demanding genuine reasoning; in the expansion stage, LLMs reuse validated rules and expand new input spaces to generate task variations, achieving scaling. However, such a process may cause hallucinations. To eliminate it, we further establish a theoretical framework and prove that programmatic verification--testing whether the inverse operation perfectly reverses the forward operation (cycle consistency)--guarantees a unique solution. Through extensive evaluations on mainstream LLMs, we find: (1) Current LLMs exhibit fundamental deficiencies in abstract reasoning, with top models significantly underperforming humans on a representative subset (39.8% vs. 68.5%). (2) Current LLMs fall far short of 2D and 1D in the complexity of generated 3D tasks, revealing their lack of understanding of high-dimensional tasks. (3) Counterintuitively, inputs with higher information complexity can simplify the reasoning process.

</details>


### [52] [MetaCogAgent: A Metacognitive Multi-Agent LLM Framework with Self-Aware Task Delegation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17292)
*Chenyu Wang,Yang Shu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MetaCogAgent is a multi-agent LLM framework that uses metacognitive self-assessment to enable agents to evaluate their own competence before accepting tasks, outperforming existing systems with 82.4% accuracy while using fewer API calls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent LLM systems assign tasks based on predefined roles without agents assessing their own competence boundaries, leading to overconfident execution on tasks beyond expertise.

Method: MetaCogAgent equips each agent with a Metacognitive Self-Assessment Unit featuring: (1) a self-assessment mechanism combining verbalized uncertainty with historical capability profiles; (2) an adaptive delegation protocol routing low-confidence tasks via cross-agent evaluation; and (3) a capability boundary learning module refined through cybernetic feedback.

Result: On the MetaCog-Eval benchmark (700 tasks across 5 cognitive dimensions), MetaCogAgent achieves 82.4% task accuracy (8.7% above best routing baseline) using 5% fewer API calls than AutoGen and 34% fewer than ensemble voting.

Conclusion: Incorporating metacognitive self-assessment into multi-agent LLM systems improves both task accuracy and computational efficiency, with ablation studies confirming each metacognitive component contributes to overall performance.

Abstract: Multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems have shown promise for solving complex tasks through agent collaboration. However, existing frameworks assign tasks based on predefined roles without considering whether an agent can accurately assess its own competence boundaries, leading to overconfident execution on tasks beyond its expertise. Inspired by metacognition theory from cognitive science, we propose MetaCogAgent, a multi-agent LLM framework where each agent is equipped with a Metacognitive Self-Assessment Unit that evaluates task-capability alignment before execution. The framework introduces three contributions: (1) a self-assessment mechanism that estimates per-task confidence by combining verbalized uncertainty with historical capability profiles; (2) an adaptive delegation protocol that routes low-confidence tasks to better-suited agents through cross-agent evaluation; and (3) a capability boundary learning module that iteratively refines each agent's competence model via cybernetic feedback. Experiments on our constructed MetaCog-Eval benchmark (700 tasks across 5 cognitive dimensions) demonstrate that MetaCogAgent achieves 82.4% task accuracy -- 8.7% above the best routing baseline -- while using 5% fewer API calls than AutoGen and 34% fewer than ensemble voting. Ablation studies confirm that each metacognitive component contributes to overall system performance.

</details>


### [53] [CyberCorrect: A Cybernetic Framework for Closed-Loop Self-Correction in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17305)
*Yuning Wu,Yingmin Liu,Yang Shu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CyberCorrect formalizes LLM self-correction as a cybernetic closed-loop control system with tri-modal error detection, type-directed correction, and convergence control, achieving 79.8% final accuracy (+6.2% over SOTA) and 41% reduction in over-correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
