TL;DR: HASP upgrades LLM agent skills from passive textual advice into executable Program Functions (PFs) that actively intervene during failure-prone states, achieving substantial improvements on web-search, math reasoning, and coding tasks over both training-free and training-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches encode reusable skills as textual guidance that remains largely advisory, lacking explicit mechanisms for when and how to intervene in the agent loop during execution.

Method: HASP introduces Program Functions (PFs) as executable guardrails that activate on failure-prone states to modify the next action or inject corrective context; it operates modularly at inference time, during post-training, or for self-improvement via evolving validated, teacher-reviewed PFs.

Result: Inference-time PFs improve average performance by 25% over ReAct Agent on web-search reasoning; post-training and controlled evolution achieve a 30.4% gain over Search-R1.

Conclusion: HASP bridges the gap between advisory skill guidance and actionable agent intervention, with mechanism analysis revealing how PFs trigger and intervene, how skills are internalized, and requirements for stable skill library evolution.

Abstract: Equipping LLM agents with reusable skills derived from past experience has become a popular and successful approach for tackling complex and long-horizon tasks. However, such lessons are often encoded as textual guidance that remains largely advisory, lacking explicit mechanisms for when and how to intervene in the agent loop. To bridge the gap, we introduce HASP(Harnessing LLM Agents with Skill Programs), a new framework that upgrades skills into executable Program Functions (PFs). Rather than offering passive advice, PFs act as executable guardrails that activate on failure-prone states and modify the next action or inject corrective context. HASP is highly modular: it can be applied at inference time for direct agent-loop intervention, during post-training to provide structured supervision, or for self-improvement by evolving validated, teacher-reviewed PFs. Empirically, HASP drives substantial gains compared to both training-free and training-based methods on web-search, math reasoning, and coding tasks. For example, on web-search reasoning, inference-time PFs alone improve the average performance by 25% compared to (multi-loop) ReAct Agent, while post-training and controlled evolution achieve a 30.4% gain over Search-R1. To provide deeper insights into HASP, our mechanism analysis reveals how PFs trigger and intervene, how skills are internalized, and the requirement for stable skill library evolution.

</details>


### [79] [Agents for Experiments, Experiments for Agents: A Design Grammar for AI-Enabled Experimental Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17746)
*Yingjie Zhang,Chun Feng,Weizhang Zhu,Tianshu Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SEED is a framework that represents experimental conditions for human-AI and multi-agent workflows as typed actor-flow graphs, enabling description, novelty evaluation, and constrained design generation to address the challenges of workflow representation, traceability, and governance in AI-enabled knowledge production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current experimental conditions for human-AI and agentic workflows are largely specified in prose, making them difficult to compare, reuse, or audit. There is a need for structured representations that support rigorous experimentation as AI systems become active participants in organizational and knowledge work.

Method: The authors propose SEED (Structural Encoding for Experimental Discovery), which encodes experimental conditions as typed actor-flow graphs. It supports three functions: describing conditions as interaction structures, evaluating structural novelty against prior designs, and generating candidate designs under feasibility and governance constraints.

Result: A lightweight empirical feasibility test comparing graph-blind and SEED-guided generation in a medical-triage design task showed that SEED-guided designs produced clearer actor-flow changes, assumptions, and governance checks.

Conclusion: The grammar-based approach is feasible as a design aid, but governance tensions around novelty, replication, validity, diversity of inquiry, and accountability remain open challenges.

Abstract: AI systems are becoming active participants in organizational and knowledge work. They increasingly interact with humans, coordinate workflows, and operate in multi-agent arrangements. Understanding their effects therefore requires more than measuring output accuracy; it requires evidence about mechanisms, delegation, feedback, and control. Experiments remain central to this task, but they also face a recursive challenge: we need experiments for agents to study these arrangements, and we may need agents for experiments to help search the expanding space of possible designs. Yet experimental conditions for human-AI and agentic workflows are still largely specified in prose, making them difficult to compare, reuse, or audit. We frame this as a problem of workflow representation, traceability, and governance in AI-enabled knowledge production. We introduce SEED (Structural Encoding for Experimental Discovery), a framework that represents experimental conditions as typed actor-flow graphs. SEED supports three design functions: describing conditions as interaction structures, evaluating structural novelty relative to encoded prior designs, and generating candidate designs under feasibility and governance constraints. We report a lightweight empirical feasibility test that compares graph-blind and SEEDguided generation in a medical-triage design task. In this diagnostic contrast, SEED-guided candidate designs show clearer actor-flow changes, assumptions, and governance checks, supporting the feasibility of the grammar as a design aid. The commentary closes by identifying governance tensions around novelty, replication, validity, diversity of inquiry, and accountability.

</details>


### [80] [Surface-Form Neural Sparse Retrieval: Robust Fuzzy Matching for Industrial Music Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17762)
*Paul Greyson,Zhichao Geng,Wei Zhang,Yang Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a robust neural sparse retrieval system for Amazon Music that addresses query robustness challenges (misspellings, phonetic variations) under strict latency constraints. By adapting an inference-free sparse retrieval architecture with a domain-specific subword tokenization strategy (max 3 chars), they achieve 91.4% recall@10 (vs. 57.7% for trigrams) with zero additional query encoding latency, and +0.8% higher stabilized recall in production feedback loop simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Music search at Amazon scale faces challenges where queries deviate from indexed metadata due to misspellings, transpositions, and phonetic variations. Existing HCI system relies on n-gram matching for exploration but suffers from poor semantic robustness and high noise, limiting learning from long-tail queries. The goal is to maximize exploration efficiency while maintaining strict millisecond-level latency.

Method: They adapt an inference-free sparse retrieval architecture to the music domain, combining it with domain-specific granular subword tokenization (max 3 chars). Neural embeddings and term expansions are pre-computed during offline indexing, so online processing is reduced to minimal tokenization and IDF weighting. The short-length token constraints enforce learning of surface-form robustness over lexical memorization.

Result: On a 6M-document production corpus: 91.4% recall@10 (vs. 57.7% for trigrams) at comparable throughput. Simulation of HCI feedback loop shows +0.8% higher stabilized recall than production trigrams. Ablation studies show sparse training methodology drives performance gains, and domain-specific pretraining is a cost-effective alternative to large-scale general-purpose pretraining.

Conclusion: The proposed neural sparse retrieval system significantly improves exploration efficiency and recall for music search at scale, with zero additional latency overhead, making it suitable for production deployment in the HCI feedback loop.

Abstract: Music search at the scale of Amazon Music presents a unique challenge: queries frequently deviate from indexed metadata due to misspellings, transpositions, and phonetic variations, yet the retrieval system must operate under strict millisecond-level latency constraints. Our existing learning-to-retrieve system, the High Confidence Index (HCI), learns query-entity associations from customer behavior, relying on continual ``exploration'' to choose candidates. Traditional n-gram matching enables this exploration but suffers from poor semantic robustness and high noise, limiting the system's ability to learn from long-tail queries. In this work, we present a \textbf{robust neural sparse retrieval system} designed to maximize exploration efficiency. We adapt a state-of-the-art \textbf{inference-free} sparse retrieval architecture to the music domain, combining it with an effective \textbf{domain-specific granular subword tokenization strategy}. Our approach utilizes short-length token constraints (max 3 chars) to enforce the learning of surface-form robustness over lexical memorization. By pre-computing the neural embeddings and term expansions during the offline indexing phase, online processing is reduced to minimal tokenization and IDF weighting, achieving effectively zero latency overhead for query encoding. Evaluations on a 6M-document production corpus show an aggregate \textbf{91.4\%} recall@10 (vs. \textbf{57.7\%} for trigrams) at comparable throughput. Simulation of the HCI feedback loop demonstrates improved exploration efficiency, with \textbf{+0.8\%} higher stabilized recall than production trigrams. Ablation studies indicate that our sparse training methodology drives the performance gains, while domain-specific pretraining provides a cost-effective alternative to large-scale general-purpose pretraining.

</details>


### [81] [Entropy-Gradient Inversion: Moving Toward Internal Mechanism of Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17770)
*Junyao Yang,Chen Qian,Kun Wang,Linfeng Zhang,Quanshi Zhang,Yong Liu,Dongrui Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LRMs improve reasoning by shifting from fast to slow thinking, but face gaps in analysis and RL instability. The paper identifies Entropy-Gradient Inversion as a geometric fingerprint of reasoning, and proposes CorR-PO to embed it into RL reward regularization, outperforming baselines across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the fundamental gap between token-level behavioral analysis and internal reasoning mechanisms, and the instability of RL for reasoning optimization that relies on costly external verifiers.

Method: Formally defines Entropy-Gradient Inversion (negative correlation between token entropy and logit gradients) as a geometric fingerprint of LRM reasoning. Proposes Correlation-Regularized Group Policy Optimization (CorR-PO) which embeds this inversion signature into RL reward regularization.

Result: CorR-PO consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across multiple reasoning benchmarks and model scales. Stronger inversion directly correlates with superior reasoning performance.

Conclusion: Entropy-Gradient Inversion serves as a reliable geometric signature of LRM reasoning capability, and incorporating it as a regularization signal in RL optimization yields state-of-the-art reasoning performance without costly external verifiers.

Abstract: The advancement of Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) has catalyzed a paradigm shift from reactive ``fast thinking'' text generation to systematic, step-by-step ``slow thinking'' reasoning, unlocking state-of-the-art performance in complex mathematical and logical tasks. However, the field faces \textit{the fundamental gap between token-level behavioral analysis and internal reasoning mechanisms, and the instability of reinforcement learning (RL) for reasoning optimization relying on costly external verifiers}. We identify and formally define \textbf{Entropy-Gradient Inversion}, a robust negative correlation between token entropy and logit gradients that acts as a definitive geometric fingerprint for LRM reasoning capability. Building on this, we propose \textbf{Correlation-Regularized Group Policy Optimization (CorR-PO)}, which embeds this inversion signature into RL reward regularization. Extensive experiments on various reasoning benchmarks across multiple model scales show CorR-PO consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, confirming that stronger inversion directly correlates with superior reasoning performance.

</details>


### [82] [STRIDE: A Self-Reflective Agent Framework for Reliable Automatic Equation Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17790)
*Jiarui Su,Songjun Tu,Bei Sun,Xiaojun Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STRIDE is a self-reflective agent framework for LLM-based symbolic equation discovery that improves reliability through data-aware generation, mixed-fitting evaluation, critic-executor repair, and diversity-preserving semantic memory, outperforming existing methods on symbolic regression benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based equation discovery systems rely on generation-centered loops that suffer from three key issues: (1) misjudging useful skeletons under unreliable fitting, (2) discarding near-correct equations that only require minor repair, and (3) accumulating redundant memories that provide limited guidance for future search.

Method: STRIDE is a self-reflective agent framework with four core components: (1) data-aware generation, (2) mixed-fitting evaluation that turns fitted scores and candidate behavior into shared feedback, (3) critic-executor repair to fix near-correct equations, and (4) diversity-preserving semantic memory for effective reuse of past candidates.

Result: Experiments on representative symbolic-regression benchmarks and LSR-Synth suites show that STRIDE improves accuracy, out-of-distribution (OOD) robustness, and structural recovery across multiple LLM backbones. Ablation studies confirm the contribution of each core component.

Conclusion: The self-reflective closed-loop framework enables equations to be proposed, assessed, refined, and reused more reliably, addressing critical failure modes in prior LLM-based symbolic regression approaches.

Abstract: LLM-based equation discovery offers a promising route to recovering symbolic laws from data, but many systems still rely on generation-centered loops that propose candidates, fit parameters, score results, and reuse selected examples. Such loops can misjudge useful skeletons under unreliable fitting, discard near-correct equations that require repair, and accumulate redundant memories that provide limited guidance. We propose STRIDE, a self-reflective agent framework that improves reliability by coordinating data-aware generation, mixed-fitting evaluation, critic--executor repair, and diversity-preserving semantic memory. By turning fitted scores and candidate behavior into shared feedback, STRIDE enables equations to be proposed, assessed, refined, and reused within a closed-loop discovery process. Experiments on representative symbolic-regression benchmarks and LSR-Synth suites show that STRIDE improves accuracy, OOD robustness, and structural recovery across multiple LLM backbones, with ablations and analyses confirming the contribution of its core components.

</details>


### [83] [Accelerating AI-Powered Research: The PuppyChatter Framework for Usable and Flexible Tooling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17809)
*Chun-Hsiung Tseng,Hao-Chiang Koong Lin,Andrew Chih-Wei Huang,Yung-Hui Chen,Jia-Rou Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PuppyChatter is a novel software framework that balances the simplicity of vendor-specific SDKs with the vendor-neutrality of model abstraction frameworks, offering a streamlined and flexible paradigm for developing LLM-based AI applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for developing AI applications with LLMs face trade-offs: vendor-specific APIs/SDKs require complex manual requests or cause vendor lock-in, while model abstraction frameworks add complexity and security concerns. The research aims to reconcile these conflicting factors.

Method: The study introduces PuppyChatter, a software framework designed to preserve the intuitive simplicity of vendor-specific SDKs while adhering to vendor-neutrality principles characteristic of model abstraction frameworks.

Result: PuppyChatter offers a more streamlined and flexible development paradigm, balancing ease of use (from SDKs) with independence (from abstraction frameworks).

Conclusion: PuppyChatter effectively reconciles the trade-off between vendor lock-in and development complexity, providing a novel approach for LLM-based AI application development.

Abstract: This research addresses the challenges inherent in developing Artificial Intelligence (AI) applications, particularly those leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs). While AI vendors provide Application Programming Interfaces (APIs) and Software Development Kits (SDKs) to facilitate developer interaction, the former often requires intricate manual request construction, and the latter can lead to significant vendor lock-in. Furthermore, existing model abstraction frameworks, though mitigating vendor dependency, introduce an additional layer of complexity and potential security concerns. To reconcile these conflicting factors, the study introduces PuppyChatter, a novel software framework designed to preserve the intuitive simplicity of vendor-specific SDKs while simultaneously adhering to the vendor-neutrality principles characteristic of model abstraction, thereby offering a more streamlined and flexible development paradigm.

</details>


### [84] [Going Headless? On the Boundaries of Vertical AI Firms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17812)
*Muhammad Zia Hydari,Farooq Muzaffar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Vertical AI firms face an unbundling dilemma where general-purpose AI agents separate workflow from domain expertise. This article argues that "going headless" (ceding workflow to agents while exposing domain expertise as services) is beneficial for some firms but destructive for others, depending on whether the accountability boundary—rather than the interface boundary—can be moved. Using Coase, platform envelopment, and complementary assets theories, it shows that value capture concentrates in cospecialized accountability assets (e.g., professional signoff, regulated workflows). It introduces a three-position taxonomy (component, integrated platform, dual-track), the concept of "rule debt" (future governance burden from migrating rules into agent prompts), and four design principles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the strategic tension between "going headless" (ceding workflow to AI agents) versus maintaining integrated vertical applications, by identifying when unbundling destroys versus preserves value capture for vertical AI firms.

Method: Conceptual analysis drawing on Coase's theory of the firm, Eisenmann/Parker/Van Alstyne's platform envelopment framework, and Teece's complementary assets and appropriability theory. Introduces a three-position taxonomy (component, integrated software platform, dual-track) and the formal construct of rule debt.

Result: Value capture concentrates in cospecialized accountability assets (professional signoff, regulated workflows, evidence trails, trusted systems of record). Orchestrators gain envelopment power through open protocols. The appropriate architectural choice depends on the task-accountability regime, not the sector.

Conclusion: Four principles: decompose by accountability not interface, invert edges while retaining core, position rule debt as the cost integrated platforms prevent, and avoid single-orchestrator dependence.

Abstract: Vertical AI firms in accounting, law, healthcare, procurement, and similar domains historically bundled workflow, domain logic, and accountability into a single application. General-purpose AI agents are now unbundling that package, prompting founders and investors to advocate "going headless": cede the workflow and interface to agents and expose domain expertise as callable services. This article argues that going headless is correct for some firms and destructive for others, and that the latter often cede their value capture inadvertently through architectural choices that look like interface decisions. This is a boundary question, and the answer turns on distinguishing the interface boundary, which can often move, from the accountability boundary, which often must not. Drawing on Coase's theory of the firm, Eisenmann, Parker, and Van Alstyne's platform envelopment framework, and Teece's analysis of complementary assets and appropriability, the article shows that orchestrators operating through open protocols acquire envelopment power even as technical interoperability improves, and that durable value capture concentrates in cospecialized accountability assets: professional signoff, regulated workflows, evidence trails, and trusted systems of record. The article proposes a three-position taxonomy (component, integrated software platform, dual-track) determined not by sector but by task-accountability regime, and formalizes the construct of rule debt: the future governance, maintenance, and accountability burden that accrues to customer organizations when business rules and professional standards migrate from governed systems into prompts and agent instructions. Four principles follow: decompose by accountability not interface, invert the edges while retaining the core, position rule debt as the customer cost the integrated platform prevents, and avoid single-orchestrator dependence.

</details>


### [85] [Interactive Evaluation Requires a Design Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17829)
*Keyang Xuan,Peiyang Song,Pan Lu,Pengrui Han,Wenkai Li,Zhenyu Zhang,Zexue He,Wenyue Hua,Manling Li,Jiaxuan You,Adrian Weller,Yizhong Wang,Jiaxin Pei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that interactive evaluation of LLM-based systems should be treated as a principled evaluation paradigm, not just a new set of agent benchmarks. It defines evaluation as a mapping from evidence to judgments, shows how interactive evaluation changes both sides of this mapping, and proposes a taxonomy, design principles, and reporting standards for trajectory-level evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation practices for LLMs inherit assumptions from static, response-centered benchmarks (fixed inputs, isolated outputs) while LLMs are increasingly deployed as interactive systems acting over time through tools, environments, users, and other agents. The landscape of interactive benchmarks is fragmented with inconsistent artifacts, scoring methods, and claims.

Method: The paper proposes defining evaluation as an autonomous mapping from evidence to judgments, and analyzes how interactive evaluation changes both evidence (interaction-generated trajectories) and evaluation procedures (assessing process, recoverability, coordination, robustness, system-level performance). It develops a two-axis taxonomy, design principles, and reporting standards.

Result: The paper identifies that interactive evaluation requires assessing process, recoverability, coordination, robustness, and system-level performance rather than single-response correctness. It provides a structured taxonomy and principles for designing and reporting interactive evaluations.

Conclusion: Interactive evaluation should be recognized as a distinct evaluation paradigm requiring its own methodological foundations. The field needs principled design and reporting standards to move beyond fragmented, ad-hoc benchmarking approaches.

Abstract: AI evaluation is undergoing a structural change. Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as systems that act over time through tools, environments, users, and other agents, while many evaluation practices still inherit assumptions from response-centered benchmarks (e.g., fixed inputs, isolated outputs, and outcome judgments that can be made from a single response). The field has begun to build interactive benchmarks, but the resulting landscape is fragmented: benchmarks differ in what interaction artifacts they admit, how trajectories are scored, and what claims their results support. This position paper argues that interactive evaluation should be treated as a principled evaluation paradigm, not merely a new family of agent benchmarks. Simply adopting previous evaluation paradigms does not suffice. We define evaluation as an autonomous mapping from evidence to judgments, and show that interactive evaluation changes both sides of this mapping: the evidence becomes interaction-generated trajectories, while the evaluation procedure must assess process, recoverability, coordination, robustness, and system-level performance. Building on this definition, we propose a two-axis taxonomy, derive design principles and reporting standards, examine representative scenarios, and analyze how longstanding evaluation challenges reappear at the trajectory level.

</details>


### [86] [Remembering More, Risking More: Longitudinal Safety Risks in Memory-Equipped LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17830)
*Ahmad Al-Tawaha,Shangding Gu,Peizhi Niu,Ruoxi Jia,Ming Jin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how memory accumulation across many independent tasks affects LLM agent safety over time, introducing a "temporal memory contamination" failure mode and a trigger-probe protocol to measure it. Experiments show memory-enabled agents exhibit increasing violation rates as memory grows, and this risk is detectable from retrieval state before generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety evaluations of memory-equipped LLM agents only measure within-task safety under adversarial conditions, ignoring that in deployment, a single agent accumulates memory across many independent tasks over time, which can affect behavior on later unrelated tasks.

Method: A trigger-probe protocol evaluates a fixed probe set against read-only memory snapshots at varying prefix lengths, with a NullMemory counterfactual baseline. Applied across three deployment scenarios, eight memory architectures, and Claw-like AI agents with native memory mechanisms.

Result: Memory-enabled agents consistently exceed the NullMemory baseline, memory-induced violation rates show a robust upward trend with exposure length, and order-randomization experiments show the effect is driven by accumulated content rather than encounter order. Memory-induced risk is detectable from retrieval state before generation.

Conclusion: Memory safety should be treated as a longitudinal property requiring temporal evaluation, not a single-state property captured by a snapshot.

Abstract: Safety evaluations of memory-equipped LLM agents typically measure within-task safety: whether an agent completes a single scenario safely, often under adversarial conditions such as prompt injection or memory poisoning. In deployment, however, a single agent serves many independent tasks over a long horizon, and memory accumulated during earlier tasks can affect behavior on later, unrelated ones. Studying this regime requires evaluation along the temporal dimension across tasks: not whether an agent is safe at any single memory state, but how its safety profile changes as memory accumulates across many independent interactions. We call this failure mode temporal memory contamination. To isolate memory exposure from stream non-stationarity, we introduce a trigger-probe protocol that evaluates a fixed probe set against read-only memory snapshots at varying prefix lengths, together with a NullMemory counterfactual baseline for identifying memory-induced violations. We apply this protocol across three deployment scenarios spanning records, memos, forms, and email correspondence and eight memory architectures, and additionally on Claw-like AI agents, such as OpenClaw, using the platform's native memory mechanism. Memory-enabled agents consistently exceed the NullMemory baseline, and memory-induced violation rates show a robust upward trend with exposure length on both agent classes. Order-randomization experiments indicate that the effect is driven primarily by accumulated content rather than encounter order. Finally, a structural consequence of the event decomposition is that memory-induced risk is detectable from retrieval state before generation, which we confirm with a high-recall diagnostic monitor. Our results argue for treating memory safety as a longitudinal property that requires temporal evaluation, not a single-state property that can be captured by a snapshot.

</details>


### [87] [KISS - Knowledge Infrastructure for Scientific Simulation: A Scaffolding for Agentic Earth Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17856)
*Ziwei Li,Liujun Zhu,Yuchen Liu,Yichen Zhao,Birk Li,Ruiqi Wu,Junliang Jin,Jianyun Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Knowledge Infrastructure (KI), a scaffold that enables AI agents to autonomously execute process-based simulation models by externalizing domain expertise. Across 119 models and 14 Earth-science domains, KI-equipped agents achieved up to 84% success in end-to-end simulations, compared to <40% without KI. The approach generalizes across disciplines and lowers access/integration barriers for non-specialists.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The communities most exposed to climate risk and resource scarcity are the least able to use process-based simulation models, which encode decades of scientific understanding but remain inaccessible to non-specialists.

Method: The authors construct Knowledge Infrastructure (KI) — an agent-actionable scaffold externalizing expertise via validated modelling operators, staged domain protocols, and diagnostic recovery mechanisms. They also develop a Knowledge Dissection Toolkit (KDT) that autonomously produces KI for additional models.

Result: In a 3,000-trial coupled-hydrology benchmark, KI-equipped agents produced physically plausible simulations in up to 84% of trials vs. <40% without KI. The approach generalized to 117 additional models across 14 Earth-science domains (119 total KIs).

Conclusion: Operational expertise is structured and extractable rather than ad hoc. KI lowers both the access barrier for non-specialist users and the integration barrier between modelling communities, enabling process-based science to evolve as a living scientific commons.

Abstract: Process-based simulation models encode decades of scientific understanding across the Earth sciences, yet the communities most exposed to climate risk and resource scarcity are the least able to use them. Here, we introduce knowledge infrastructure (KI), an agent-actionable scaffold that externalizes expertise into validated modelling operators, staged domain protocols, and diagnostic recovery mechanisms. Across a 3,000-trial coupled-hydrology benchmark, agents equipped with KI produced physically plausible, verifiable end-to-end simulations in up to 84% of trials, while agents without KI plateaued below 40%. KI generalizes across disciplines. We packaged its construction into a Knowledge Dissection Toolkit (KDT) that autonomously produced KI enabling end-to-end agent execution of 117 additional process-based models across 14 Earth-science domains. Across all 119 KIs, modelling decisions and failure remedies converged despite different underlying physics, showing that operational expertise is structured and extractable rather than ad hoc. Demonstrations show KI-equipped agents lowering both the access barrier between non-specialist users and process-based simulation, and the integration barrier between modelling communities. Through this scaffold, process-based science can then evolve as a living scientific commons, answerable to whoever needs to know and extendable by whoever can contribute.

</details>


### [88] [PAIR: Prefix-Aware Internal Reward Model for Multi-Turn Agent Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17877)
*Wonjoong Kim,Yeonjun In,Sangwu Park,Dongha Lee,Chanyoung Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes PAIR, a two-stage internal reward model that combines hidden-state probes (for belief-consistency) and attention-based features (for robustness to contamination) to provide dense, step-level reward signals for GRPO training without external LLM judges, ground-truth answers, or full rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs struggle with complex multi-stage tasks; GRPO relies on sparse outcome rewards that poorly handle credit assignment over intermediate steps, and existing remedies (full rollouts, external LLM judges, intrinsic rewards) are costly or impractical.

Method: PAIR is a two-stage model: (1) a frozen hidden-state probe estimates belief-consistency (sensitive to prefix contamination), and (2) a lightweight attention-based head corrects it toward grounded correctness (robust to contamination). This leverages the complementary strengths and weaknesses of the two probing approaches.

Result: PAIR achieves the highest AUROC on contaminated trajectories, operates at negligible inference cost, and enables dense step-level reward signals for GRPO without external models, ground-truth dependencies, or full-trajectory rollouts.

Conclusion: Internal correctness probing over LLM hidden states can be effectively repurposed as a step-level reward signal for multi-step reasoning, addressing the limitations of sparse outcome rewards in GRPO.

Abstract: A significant hurdle for current LLMs is the execution of complex, multi-stage tasks. Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has been emerging as a leading choice, but its reliance on sparse outcome rewards severely limits credit assignment across intermediate steps. Existing remedies such as running full rollouts to assign step-level advantages, calling external LLM judges at each step, or computing intrinsic rewards that require ground-truth answers at every evaluation introduce significant costs or practical constraints. We hypothesize that internal correctness probing over LLM hidden states can be repurposed as a step-level reward signal, potentially addressing all of these limitations at once. However, existing probing research assumes clean inputs, and we first show that this assumption breaks down in multi-step settings: hidden-state probes degrade severely under prefix contamination tracking coherence with the (possibly corrupted) prefix rather than grounded correctness, while attention-based features remain robust to contamination but underperform on clean prefixes. Building on this complementary relationship, we propose the Prefix-Aware Internal Reward (PAIR), a two-stage model with a frozen hidden-state probe estimating belief-consistency and a lightweight attention-based head correcting it toward grounded correctness. Experimental results show that PAIR achieves the highest AUROC on contaminated trajectories while operating at negligible inference cost, enabling dense step-level reward signals for GRPO training without external model calls, ground-truth dependencies, or full-trajectory rollouts.

</details>


### [89] [Evaluating Cognitive Age Alignment in Interactive AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17894)
*Yifan Shen,Jiawen Zhang,Jian Xu,Junho Kim,Ismini Lourentzou,Xu Cao,Meihuan Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ChildAgentEval is a psychometrically grounded benchmark inspired by the WISC that evaluates cognitive age alignment of MLLM-based agents by comparing their reasoning performance against age-specific human developmental stages, revealing gaps in simulating human-like cognition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite advances in MLLM-based agents, they fail at simple tasks that children can solve, highlighting a profound gap between artificial and human intelligence. The paper aims to systematically evaluate and expose where current agentic AI systems fall short in simulating age-appropriate cognitive behavior.

Method: The authors introduce ChildAgentEval, a psychometrically grounded interactive benchmark inspired by the Wechsler Intelligence Scale for Children (WISC), which systematically compares reasoning performance of various MLLM-based interactive agents against age-specific human developmental stages.

Result: The benchmark reveals where current agentic AI systems can and cannot simulate age-specific cognitive behavior, exposing failures in foundational, seemingly simple tasks that children can easily resolve.

Conclusion: There remains a significant gap between AI agent performance and human cognitive development across age stages, with the benchmark providing a structured way to assess these deficiencies.

Abstract: While agentic AI and its core multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable promise in language and visual reasoning across domains ranging from daily life to advanced scientific research, a profound gap remains between artificial and human intelligence. Despite the integration of powerful tools and advanced MLLMs, state-of-the-art AI agents frequently fail at foundational, seemingly simple tasks that a child can resolve with ease. Inspired by the Wechsler Intelligence Scale for Children (WISC), we introduce ChildAgentEval, the first psychometrically grounded interactive benchmark for evaluating cognitive age alignment in MLLM-based agents. ChildAgentEval systematically compares the reasoning performance of various MLLM-based interactive agents against age-specific human developmental stages, exposing where current agentic AI systems can and cannot simulate age-specific cognitive behavior.

</details>


### [90] [LAST-RAG: Literature-Anchored Stochastic Trajectory Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-Conditioned Degradation Model Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17902)
*Hanbyeol Park,Hyerim Bae*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LAST-RAG is a knowledge-conditioned framework for degradation model selection that uses both observed health indicator trajectories and domain-specific literature evidence, outperforming statistical and prognostic baselines in stochastic process classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing model selection methods rely on statistical fit of observed HI trajectories, which can select models inconsistent with underlying degradation mechanisms when observation windows are short or signals are noisy. The paper aims to reframe degradation model selection from a purely statistical problem to a knowledge-conditioned decision-making problem.

Method: LAST-RAG uses both observed HI trajectory and domain-specific context, hierarchically conditions candidate degradation model space via theoretical/mechanical evidence from a local evidence bank, and introduces RCRUS (Rule-based Confidence Reasoning with Uncertain State) to prevent premature elimination of candidate models under uncertain hierarchical decisions.

Result: Simulation-based experiments show LAST-RAG outperforms statistical, prognostic, and uncertainty-aware baselines in both Wiener/gamma family classification and detailed degradation model classification tasks.

Conclusion: The study reframes degradation model selection from a statistical goodness-of-fit problem into a knowledge-conditioned decision-making problem that integrates observed data with domain knowledge, addressing limitations of purely data-driven approaches.

Abstract: Stochastic-process-based degradation modeling is a core approach for estimating the distribution of remaining useful life (RUL); however, the selection of an appropriate stochastic process has not been sufficiently addressed. Existing model selection methods mainly rely on the statistical fit of the observed health indicator (HI) trajectory, but this approach may select a model that is inconsistent with the underlying degradation mechanism when the observation window is short or the signal is highly noisy. To address this issue, this paper proposes Literature-Anchored Stochastic Trajectory Retrieval-Augmented Generation (LAST-RAG). The proposed method uses both the observed HI trajectory and domain-specific context, and hierarchically conditions the candidate degradation model space based on theoretical and mechanical evidence retrieved from a local evidence bank. In addition, Rule-based Confidence Reasoning with Uncertain State (RCRUS) is introduced to prevent candidate models from being prematurely eliminated when hierarchical decisions are uncertain. Simulation-based experiments demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms statistical, prognostic, and uncertainty-aware baselines in both Wiener/gamma family classification and detailed degradation model classification. Ultimately, this study reframes degradation model selection from a purely statistical goodness-of-fit problem into a knowledge-conditioned decision-making problem that integrates observed data with domain knowledge.

</details>


### [91] [Agentic Chunking and Bayesian De-chunking of AI Generated Fuzzy Cognitive Maps: A Model of the Thucydides Trap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17903)
*Akash Kumar Panda,Olaoluwa Adigun,Bart Kosko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: We generate causal fuzzy cognitive maps (FCMs) from text by using LLM agents to chunk text, mixing chunk FCMs via convex combination into a cyclic knowledge graph, enabling Bayesian-like de-chunking. Applied to Thucydides Trap, 7/8 FCMs predict war when stimulating rising power ambition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To automatically extract structured causal knowledge graphs (FCMs) from unstructured text in a scalable, principled way that supports Bayesian inference and dynamical system analysis.

Method: LLM agents (Gemini 3.1) chunk text into overlapping segments, build sparse causal matrices per chunk, then convexly mix them into a cyclic FCM. Operator-level Bayesian inference enables de-chunking (posterior-like FCMs) for iterative updating.

Result: When applied to Allison's Thucydides Trap essay, 7 out of 8 mixed FCM knowledge graphs predicted war (fixed-point or limit-cycle attractors) upon stimulating the "rising power's ambition and entitlement" concept node.

Conclusion: The mixing and de-chunking framework is scalable, enables iterative Bayesian reasoning over causal graphs from text, and successfully recovers known geopolitical dynamics from the Thucydides Trap scenario.

Abstract: We automatically generate feedback causal fuzzy cognitive maps (FCMs) from text by teaching large-language-model agents to break the text into overlapping chunks of text. Convex mixing of these chunk FCMs gives a representative cyclic FCM knowledge graph. The text chunks can have different levels of overlap. The chunk FCMs still mix to form a new FCM causal knowledge graph. The mixing technique scales because it uses light computation with sparse causal chunk matrices. The mixing structure allows an operator-level type of Bayesian inference that produces "de-chunked" or posterior-like FCMs from the mixed FCM. These de-chunked FCMs are useful in their own right and allow further iterations of Bayesian updating. We demonstrate these mixing techniques on the essay text of Allison's "Thucydides Trap" model of conflict between a dominant power such as the United States and a rising power such as China. The FCM dynamical systems predict outcomes as they equilibrate to fixed-point or limit-cycle attractors. Seven out of 8 FCM knowledge graphs predicted a type of war when we stimulated them by turning on and keeping on the concept node that stands for the rising power's ambition and entitlement. Gemini 3.1 LLMs served as the chunking AI agents.

</details>


### [92] [SVFSearch: A Multimodal Knowledge-Intensive Benchmark for Short-Video Frame Search in the Gaming Vertical Domain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17946)
*Lingtao Mao,Huangyu Dai,Xinyu Sun,Zihan Liang,Ben Chen,Chenyi Lei,Wenwu Ou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SVFSearch is the first open benchmark for short-video frame search in the Chinese gaming domain, containing 5,000 test and 4,198 training examples. It evaluates multimodal LLMs as agents on paused game scenes using a frozen offline retrieval environment. Results show a large gap between direct QA (66.4%), practical agentic search (79.1%), and oracle knowledge (95.4%), revealing bottlenecks in visual grounding, retrieval quality, and tool-use behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks rarely evaluate multimodal LLMs as agents in short-video applications, where paused frames are visually ambiguous and answering requires vertical, long-tail, and fast-evolving domain knowledge.

Method: SVFSearch provides a frozen offline retrieval environment with a game-domain text corpus, topic-linked image gallery, and text/image/multimodal retrieval interfaces. It includes 5,000 four-choice test examples and 4,198 auxiliary training examples centered on paused game scenes from real short-video clips.

Result: Best open-source direct-QA model: 66.4%; best practical agent: 79.1%; oracle knowledge: 95.4%. Bottlenecks include visual grounding, retrieval quality, evidence-grounded reasoning, and problematic tool-use behaviors (over-search, answer-only shortcuts, retrieval-induced misleading).

Conclusion: There is a significant gap between model-only answering, practical agentic search, and oracle knowledge, highlighting key bottlenecks that need to be addressed for effective multimodal agentic search in short-video domains.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models are increasingly used as agent backbones that understand multimodal inputs, plan retrieval actions, invoke external tools, and reason over retrieved information. Yet existing benchmarks rarely evaluate this ability in short-video applications, where a paused frame is often visually ambiguous and answering requires vertical, long-tail, and fast-evolving domain knowledge. We introduce SVFSearch, the first open benchmark for short-video frame search in the Chinese gaming domain. SVFSearch contains 5,000 four-choice test examples and 4,198 auxiliary training examples, each centered on a paused game scene from a real short-video clip. To support fair and reproducible evaluation, SVFSearch provides a frozen offline retrieval environment with a game-domain text corpus, a topic-linked image gallery, and text, image, and multimodal retrieval interfaces, avoiding reliance on uncontrolled web search APIs. We evaluate representative paradigms ranging from direct QA and RAG workflow to Plan-Act-Replan agents and learned search models. Results reveal a large gap between model-only answering, practical agentic search, and oracle knowledge: the best open-source direct-QA model reaches 66.4%, the best practical agent achieves 79.1%, and oracle knowledge reaches 95.4%. Further analysis exposes bottlenecks in visual grounding, retrieval quality, evidence-grounded reasoning, and tool-use behavior, including over-search, answer-only shortcuts, and retrieval-induced misleading.

</details>


### [93] [Reconciling Contradictory Views on the Effectiveness of SFT in LLMs: An Interaction Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17967)
*Junpeng Zhang,Lei Cheng,Guoxi Zhang,Hua Cai,Qing Xu,Quanshi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SFT primarily removes noise-like interactions in LLMs rather than acquiring reliable new ones, and this beneficial denoising stage is very brief, after which overfitting occurs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why SFT is effective for small-scale DNNs but can produce inconsistent or detrimental effects when applied to LLMs, using interaction-based explanations as a faithful metric.

Method: Analyzes the evolution of word/token interactions during SFT across multiple LLMs and datasets, using interaction-based explanations to quantify inference patterns.

Result: (1) SFT primarily removes noise-like interactions while rarely acquiring reliable new interactions. (2) This beneficial denoising stage is extremely brief; continued fine-tuning introduces overfitted interactions.

Conclusion: Provides new insights into early stopping and offers practical guidance for LLM training.

Abstract: This paper explores a scientific question in supervised fine-tuning (SFT): why SFT is broadly effective for small-scale deep neural networks, yet can produce inconsistent or even detrimental effects when applied to large language models (LLMs). Recent advances in interaction-based explanations suggest that interactions between words/tokens provide a faithful metric for quantifying the inference patterns encoded by LLMs. We find that the evolution of interactions during SFT can effectively explain the inconsistent effectiveness of SFT for LLMs. Specifically, we find that (1) SFT primarily removes noise-like interactions, while rarely acquiring reliable new interactions. (2) This denoising stage is extremely brief, after which continued fine-tuning tends to introduce overfitted interactions. We validate these findings across multiple LLMs and datasets. Our findings provide new insights into early stopping and offer practical guidance for LLM training.

</details>


### [94] [Unleashing LLMs in Bayesian Optimization: Preference-Guided Framework for Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17976)
*Xinzhe Yuan,Zhuo Chen,Jianshu Zhang,Huan Xiong,Nanyang Ye,Yuqiang Li,Qinying Gu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LGBO integrates LLM preferences into Bayesian Optimization via a region-lifted preference mechanism, achieving faster convergence and superior performance across scientific benchmarks, including wet-lab battery electrolyte optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific discovery is limited by costly experiments and resource constraints. Traditional BO suffers from slow cold-start and poor scalability in high-dimensional settings, limiting its real-world scientific applicability.

Method: LGBO introduces a region-lifted preference mechanism that embeds LLM-driven preferences into every BO iteration, shifting the surrogate model's mean in a stable and controllable manner.

Result: Outperforms existing methods across physics, chemistry, biology, and materials science benchmarks. In wet-lab Fe-Cr battery electrolyte optimization, achieves 90% of best observed value within 6 iterations vs. 10+ for baselines.

Conclusion: LGBO offers a promising direction for integrating LLMs into scientific optimization workflows, with theoretical guarantees of not performing significantly worse than standard BO while achieving faster convergence when preferences align.

Abstract: Scientific discovery is increasingly constrained by costly experiments and limited resources, underscoring the need for efficient optimization in AI for science. Bayesian Optimization (BO), though widely adopted for balancing exploration and exploitation, often exhibits slow cold-start performance and poor scalability in high-dimensional settings, limiting its applicability in real-world scientific problems. To overcome these challenges, we propose LLM-Guided Bayesian Optimization (LGBO), the first LLM preference-guided BO framework that continuously integrates the semantic reasoning of large language models (LLMs) into the optimization loop. Unlike prior works that use LLMs only for warm-start initialization or candidate generation, LGBO introduces a region-lifted preference mechanism that embeds LLM-driven preferences into every iteration, shifting the surrogate mean in a stable and controllable way. Theoretically, we prove that LGBO does not perform significantly worse than standard BO in the worst case, while achieving significantly faster convergence when preferences align with the objective. Empirically, LGBO consistently outperforms existing methods across diverse dry benchmarks in physics, chemistry, biology, and materials science. Most notably, in a new wet-lab optimization of Fe-Cr battery electrolytes, LGBO attains \textbf{90\% of the best observed value within 6 iterations}, whereas standard BO and existing LLM-augmented baselines require more than 10. Together, these results suggest that LGBO offers a promising direction for integrating LLMs into scientific optimization workflows.

</details>


### [95] [Shared Backbone PPO for Multi-UAV Communication Coverage with Connection Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17999)
*Z. Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Shared Backbone PPO algorithm that shares base modules between Actor and Critic networks, integrated with a graph information aggregation module, for multi-UAV swarm communication coverage tasks, achieving superior performance and cooperation over standard PPO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve training efficiency and performance of multi-agent reinforcement learning for connectivity-preserving multi-UAV swarm communication coverage tasks, by addressing limitations of standard PPO's separate Actor-Critic architecture.

Method: Shared Backbone PPO algorithm: shares base module between Actor and Critic networks; incorporates a graph information aggregation module to model inter-agent communication conditions.

Result: Outperforms standard PPO in connectivity-preserving multi-UAV swarm communication coverage; maintains effectiveness with graph aggregation module; trained agent swarm shows higher cooperation level.

Conclusion: Shared Backbone PPO with graph information aggregation enables efficient training and superior cooperative performance in multi-UAV communication coverage tasks.

Abstract: This paper proposes a Shared Backbone Proximal Policy Optimization (Shared Backbone PPO) algorithm. By sharing the base module between the Actor and Critic networks, the algorithm achieves efficient training and improved performance. The algorithm is implemented in a connectivity-preserving multi-UAV swarm communication coverage task and compared with the standard PPO algorithm. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method achieves superior performance. Furthermore, a graph information aggregation module is incorporated into the model architecture to accommodate the communication conditions among agents. With the integration of this module, the algorithm remains effective, and the trained agent swarm exhibits a higher level of cooperation.

</details>


### [96] [TeleCom-Bench: How Far Are Large Language Models from Industrial Telecommunication Applications?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18025)
*Jieting Xiao,Yun Lin,Huizhen Qiu,Rui Ma,Chen Zhong,Dongyang Xu,Xiao Long,Chaoyu Zhang,Qiaobo Hao,Ding Zou,Zhiguo Yang,Yanqin Gao,Fang Tan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TeleCom-Bench is a comprehensive benchmark with 22,678 curated samples across 12 evaluation sets for assessing LLMs in telecommunications. It evaluates models on multi-dimensional knowledge comprehension (fundamentals, 3GPP protocols, 5G architecture, proprietary product knowledge) and end-to-end knowledge application (6 core tasks from live agent workflows). Evaluation of 8 SOTA LLMs reveals an "Execution Wall": ~90% accuracy on linguistic tasks but ~30% on procedural execution tasks like solution generation, showing LLMs are good diagnosticians but poor field engineers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current telecom benchmarks focus on static foundational knowledge and isolated atomic skills, neglecting equipment-specific documentation and end-to-end industrial workflows needed for real-world production systems. There is no standardized evaluation framework for LLMs in the telecommunications domain.

Method: The benchmark comprises 12 evaluation sets with 22,678 curated samples evaluating LLMs across a synergistic hierarchy: (1) Multi-dimensional Knowledge Comprehension (telecom fundamentals, 3GPP protocols, 5G network architecture, proprietary product knowledge across wired/core/wireless networks via knowledge graph-driven synthesis); and (2) End-to-End Knowledge Application (six core tasks from authentic live network agent workflows: intent recognition, entity extraction, event verification, tool invocation, root cause analysis, solution generation across network optimization and fault maintenance scenarios).

Result: Evaluation of eight state-of-the-art LLMs reveals a universal "Execution Wall": models achieve ~90% accuracy in linguistic interface tasks (intent recognition, entity extraction) but performance collapses to ~30% in procedural execution tasks (solution generation).

Conclusion: Current LLMs function competently as diagnosticians but fail as field engineers in telecommunications. TeleCom-Bench provides standardized diagnostics to pinpoint this capability gap and offers actionable guidance for domain-specific alignment toward production-ready telecom agents.

Abstract: While Large Language Models have achieved remarkable integration in various vertical scenarios, their deployment in the telecommunications domain remains exploratory due to the lack of a standardized evaluation framework. Current telecom benchmarks primarily focus on static, foundational knowledge and isolated atomic skills, neglecting the equipment-specific documentation and end-to-end industrial workflows essential for real-world production systems. To bridge this gap, we present TeleCom-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark comprising 12 evaluation sets with 22,678 curated samples, which evaluates LLMs across a synergistic hierarchy: (1) Multi-dimensional Knowledge Comprehension, which integrates telecommunication fundamentals, 3GPP protocols, and 5G network architecture with proprietary product knowledge across wired, core, and wireless networks via knowledge graph-driven synthesis; and (2)End-to-End Knowledge Application, which formalizes six core tasks on authentic trajectories from live network agent workflows, including intent recognition, entity extraction, event verification, tool invocation, root cause analysis, and solution generation-across network optimization and fault maintenance scenarios. Evaluations of eight state-of-the-art LLMs reveal a universal Execution Wall: while models achieve 90% accuracy in linguistic interface tasks such as intent recognition and entity extraction, performance collapses to approximately 30% in procedural execution tasks like solution generation. This capability gap demonstrates that current LLMs function competently as diagnosticians but fail as field engineers. TeleCom-Bench provides standardized diagnostics to precisely pinpoint this deficit, offering actionable guidance for domain-specific alignment toward production-ready telecom agents. The dataset and evaluation code have been released at https://github.com/ZTE-AICloud/TeleCom-Bench.

</details>


### [97] [New Insight of Variance reduce in Zero-Order Hard-Thresholding: Mitigating Gradient Error and Expansivity Contradictions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18035)
*Xinzhe Yuan,William de Vazelhes,Bin Gu,Huan Xiong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a generalized variance-reduced zeroth-order hard-thresholding algorithm that eliminates the restriction on the number of random directions in ZO gradients, overcoming the conflict between ZO gradient deviation and hard-thresholding operator expansivity, achieving improved convergence rates over the existing SZOHT method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The existing SZOHT algorithm suffers from a notable limitation on the number of random directions in zeroth-order gradients due to an inherent conflict between the deviation of ZO gradients and the expansivity of the hard-thresholding operator.

Method: The authors provide a new insight into variance reduction by mitigating the unique conflicts between ZO gradients and hard-thresholding, and propose a generalized variance reduced ZO hard-thresholding algorithm with generalized convergence analysis under standard assumptions.

Result: The new algorithm eliminates the restrictions on the number of random directions, leading to improved convergence rates and broader applicability compared with SZOHT.

Conclusion: The utility of the method is demonstrated on a ridge regression problem and black-box adversarial attacks.

Abstract: Hard-thresholding is an important type of algorithm in machine learning that is used to solve $\ell_0$ constrained optimization problems. However, the true gradient of the objective function can be difficult to access in certain scenarios, which normally can be approximated by zeroth-order (ZO) methods. The SZOHT algorithm is the only algorithm tackling $\ell_0$ sparsity constraints with ZO gradients so far. Unfortunately, SZOHT has a notable limitation on the number of random directions % in ZO gradients due to the inherent conflict between the deviation of ZO gradients and the expansivity of the hard-thresholding operator. This paper approaches this problem by considering the role of variance and provides a new insight into variance reduction: mitigating the unique conflicts between ZO gradients and hard-thresholding. Under this perspective, we propose a generalized variance reduced ZO hard-thresholding algorithm as well as the generalized convergence analysis under standard assumptions. The theoretical results demonstrate the new algorithm eliminates the restrictions on the number of random directions, leading to improved convergence rates and broader applicability compared with SZOHT. Finally, we illustrate the utility of our method on a ridge regression problem as well as black-box adversarial attacks.

</details>


### [98] [DocOS: Towards Proactive Document-Guided Actions in GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18048)
*Jingjing Liu,Ziye Huang,Zihao Cheng,Zeming Liu,Jiahong Wu,Yuhang Guo,Kehai Chen,Yunhong Wang,Haifeng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Proactive Document-Guided Action for GUI agents, enabling them to autonomously search and utilize documentation for long-tailed tasks, along with a benchmark called DocOS to evaluate this capability. Experiments reveal bottlenecks in information location and instruction grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GUI agents rely solely on static parametric knowledge from pre-training or instruction tuning, which limits their ability to handle long-tailed tasks requiring explicit procedural knowledge not stored in model parameters. This forces agents to resort to inefficient trial-and-error exploration.

Method: The paper proposes Proactive Document-Guided Action, a paradigm where GUI agents autonomously search for relevant online documentation to resolve long-tailed tasks. They introduce DocOS, a benchmark for evaluating document-guided problem solving in fully interactive web environments, requiring agents to navigate browsers, locate documentation, comprehend instructions, and ground them into executable actions.

Result: Experiments identify dual bottlenecks: agents struggle to reliably locate relevant information during proactive search, and frequently fail to faithfully ground retrieved instructions into precise actions.

Conclusion: Document-guided interaction is a crucial pathway for enabling self-evolving GUI agents in dynamic environments.

Abstract: While Graphical User Interface (GUI) agents have shown promising performance in automated device interaction, they primarily depend on static parametric knowledge from pre-training or instruction tuning. This reliance fundamentally limits their ability to handle long-tailed tasks that require explicit procedural knowledge absent from model parameters, often forcing agents to resort to inefficient and brittle trial-and-error exploration. To mitigate this limitation, we introduce \textbf{Proactive Document-Guided Action} for GUI agents in dynamic, open-web environments, a novel paradigm that mirrors human problem-solving by enabling agents to autonomously search for relevant documentation to resolve long-tailed tasks. To evaluate agents' capability in this paradigm, we propose \textbf{DocOS}, a benchmark designed to assess document-guided problem solving in fully interactive environments. DocOS requires agents to autonomously navigate a web browser, locate relevant online documentation, comprehend procedural instructions, and faithfully ground them into executable GUI actions. Extensive experiments reveal that progress is strictly constrained by dual bottlenecks: agents struggle to reliably locate relevant information during proactive search and frequently fail to faithfully ground retrieved instructions into precise actions, pointing toward document-guided interaction as a crucial pathway for enabling self-evolving GUI agents in dynamic environments.

</details>


### [99] [LLM-Guided Communication for Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18077)
*Sangjun Bae,Yisak Park,Sanghyeon Lee,Seungyul Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LMAC uses LLM-based reasoning to iteratively design a communication protocol that improves state reconstruction and reduces knowledge disparities among agents in MARL, achieving substantial performance gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior MARL communication methods either rely on inefficient information exchange or fail to transmit sufficient state information under partial observability.

Method: LMAC leverages an LLM's reasoning capability to design a communication protocol that enables all agents to reconstruct the underlying state accurately and uniformly, iteratively refining the protocol using an explicit state-awareness criterion.

Result: LMAC improves state reconstruction across agents and yields substantial performance gains over prior communication baselines on diverse MARL benchmarks.

Conclusion: LLM-driven protocol design effectively bridges the partial observability gap in MARL by enabling more accurate and uniform state recovery among agents.

Abstract: Communication is a key component in multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) for mitigating partial observability, yet prior approaches often rely on inefficient information exchange or fail to transmit sufficient state information. To address this, we propose LLM-driven Multi-Agent Communication (LMAC), which leverages an LLM's reasoning capability to design a communication protocol that enables all agents to reconstruct the underlying state as accurately and uniformly as possible. LMAC iteratively refines the protocol using an explicit state-awareness criterion, improving state recovery while narrowing differences in agents' knowledge. Experiments on diverse MARL benchmarks show that LMAC improves state reconstruction across agents and yields substantial performance gains over prior communication baselines.

</details>


### [100] [Learning to Solve Compositional Geometry Routing Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18094)
*Mingfeng Fan,Jianan Zhou,Jiaqi Cheng,Yifeng Zhang,Jie Zhang,Guillaume Adrien Sartoretti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Compositional Geometry Routing Problem (CGRP) as a unified framework for routing problems with mixed geometric task types (points, lines, areas). To solve CGRP's challenges (asymmetry, coupled routes, large action spaces), the authors propose DiCon, a plug-and-play solver using differential attention and double-level contrastive learning, achieving strong performance and generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing routing problems typically focus on point-only tasks, but real-world scenarios often involve non-point geometric tasks (lines, areas, or hybrids). CGRP unifies these into a single superclass, revealing unique challenges: inherent asymmetry, tight coupling between travel routes and task geometry, and an enlarged action space with many irrelevant options, making representation learning and decision-making difficult.

Method: DiCon is a plug-and-play framework with two complementary components: (1) a differential attention mechanism that actively suppresses probability mass on less competitive candidate actions, and (2) a double-level contrastive learning objective that promotes robust global instance representations and regularizes geometry-aware task representations.

Result: Extensive experiments show DiCon achieves strong performance, broad versatility, and superior generalization across diverse CGRP instances with different geometric compositions (point-only, line-only, area-only, and hybrid mixtures).

Conclusion: DiCon effectively addresses the unique challenges of CGRP by combining differential attention with contrastive learning, offering a versatile and generalizable solution for routing problems involving arbitrary hybrid task geometries.

Abstract: We study the Compositional Geometry Routing Problem (CGRP), a unified superclass of traditional routing problems that covers point-only, line-only, area-only, and arbitrary hybrid task geometries, providing a broad abstraction for real-world routing scenarios. Beyond standard point-based routing, CGRP with non-point tasks can be inherently asymmetric, tightly coupled travel routes with the intrinsic path, and enlarges the action space with numerous feasible yet often irrelevant options, thereby posing significant challenges for both representation learning and decision-making. To address these challenges, we propose DiCon, a differential attention-assisted solver with contrastive learning, as a plug-and-play framework that tackles the problem from two complementary angles. First, we introduce a differential attention mechanism that actively suppresses the probability mass on less competitive candidate actions. Second, we design a double-level contrastive learning objective to promote robust global instance representations and regularize geometry-aware task representations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DiCon achieves strong performance, broad versatility, and superior generalization across diverse CGRP instances with different compositions.

</details>


### [101] [Safety Geometry Collapse in Multimodal LLMs and Adaptive Drift Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18104)
*Jiahe Guo,Xiangran Guo,Jiaxuan Chen,Weixiang Zhao,Yanyan Zhao,Yutai Hou,Qianchao Wang,Dandan Tu,Bing Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multimodal LLMs suffer a safety gap due to "Safety Geometry Collapse"—modality-induced drift compresses the refusal direction in representation space, making harmful inputs harder to reject. The paper proposes ReGap, a training-free method that adaptively corrects this drift via self-rectification, restoring safety without sacrificing utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs fail to transfer safety capabilities from text to non-text inputs (e.g., images), creating a persistent multimodal safety gap. The authors aim to understand and remedy this gap from a representation-geometric perspective.

Method: 1) Analyzes safety from a representation-geometric perspective, identifying a text-aligned refusal direction and a modality-induced drift direction. 2) Introduces the concept of "Safety Geometry Collapse" and quantifies it via conditional refusal separability. 3) Validates causality through fixed-strength activation intervention (counteracting drift). 4) Proposes ReGap, a training-free inference-time method that adaptively corrects modality drift using self-rectification signals.

Result: Stronger modality-induced drift correlates with weaker refusal separability and higher attack success rates. Counteracting drift restores refusal separability and improves multimodal safety. ReGap significantly improves MLLM safety across multiple benchmarks without compromising general capabilities.

Conclusion: Representation-level modality alignment is a crucial direction for real-time safety improvement. The findings provide a path toward building safer, more reliable MLLMs.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often fail to transfer safety capabilities learned in the text modality to semantically equivalent non-text inputs, revealing a persistent multimodal safety gap. We study this gap from a representation-geometric perspective by analyzing a text-aligned refusal direction and a modality-induced drift direction. We show that multimodal inputs compress the usable separation along the refusal direction, making it no longer reliable for identifying and refusing harmful inputs. We refer to this failure mode as Safety Geometry Collapse. We quantify it through conditional refusal separability and show that stronger modality-induced drift is consistently associated with weaker refusal separability and higher attack success rates. We then validate the causal role of modality-induced drift through a fixed-strength activation intervention: counteracting the estimated drift restores refusal separability and improves multimodal safety. After drift correction, we further observe self-rectification, where the model recovers its ability to recognize and refuse harmful multimodal inputs during forward dynamics. This effect also provides an internal signal of the model's perceived harmfulness of each input. Motivated by this signal, we propose ReGap, a training-free inference-time method that adaptively corrects modality drift using self-rectification. Experiments across multiple multimodal safety benchmarks and utility benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of ReGap, which significantly improves the safety of MLLMs without compromising general capabilities. Our findings highlight representation-level modality alignment as a crucial direction for real-time safety improvement and for building safer, more reliable MLLMs.

</details>


### [102] [POST: Prior-Observation Adversarial Learning of Spatio-Temporal Associations for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18128)
*Suofei Zhang,Yaxuan Zheng,Haifeng Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes POST, a unified spatio-temporal MTSAD framework using joint prior-observation adversarial learning to address spatial over-generalization, achieving SOTA in both time-wise detection and channel-wise anomaly localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MTSAD frameworks integrating GNNs with sequence models suffer from spatial over-generalization, where unconstrained structural modeling indiscriminately reconstructs anomalies, degrading detection recall.

Method: A prior-observation adversarial learning paradigm that alternately learns adjacency matrices as structural prior and models the association discrepancy between prior and data-driven observation in a minimax manner during training.

Result: Establishes new SOTA in both time-wise detection and spatial anomaly localization across public datasets and a dedicated synthetic benchmark with precise channel-wise annotations.

Conclusion: The adversarial optimization improves model sensitivity for time-wise detection and enables anomaly localization to specific channels. Code, models, and benchmark are publicly available.

Abstract: Existing Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection (MTSAD) frameworks increasingly rely on integrating Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) with sequence models to capture complex spatio-temporal dependencies. However, less attention is paid to the spatial over-generalization problem, where unconstrained structural modeling indiscriminately reconstructs anomalies, inevitably degrading detection recall. To tackle this problem, we propose a novel framework that unifies spatio-temporal modeling through a joint prior-observation adversarial learning paradigm. In the spatial dimension, the model alternately learns adjacency matrices as structural prior and models the association discrepancy between prior and data-driven observation in a minimax manner during training. Such adversarial optimization not only improves the model sensitivity for time-wise detection, but also enables the model to localize anomalies to specific channels. To systematically evaluate this anomaly localization capability, we further construct a synthetic benchmark equipped with precise channel-wise annotations. Extensive experiments across public datasets and our dedicated benchmark demonstrate that the proposed framework establishes a new state-of-the-art in both time-wise detection and spatial localization tasks. Our code, pre-trained models, and benchmark are publicly available at https://github.com/anocodetest1/POST.

</details>


### [103] [Generative AI and the Productivity Divide: Human-AI Complementarities in Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18143)
*Lihi Idan,Bharat Anand*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A randomized experiment shows that GenAI (LLM) assistance boosts knowledge workers' productivity on average, but gains are highly uneven and predicted not by prior knowledge but by AI Interaction Competence (AIC). Scaffolding reduces inequality, suggesting firms should pair GenAI with micro-training and standardized workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Little is known about the heterogeneity of GenAI's productivity effects across different users, despite its transformative potential for knowledge work.

Method: A randomized controlled experiment where early-career knowledge worker analogs self-studied a technical domain using either traditional resources or LLM assistance. AIC was measured, and a scaffolding intervention (conceptual maps) was tested.

Result: GenAI access significantly increased average task performance, but gains were highly uneven. AIC—not GPA or prior knowledge—predicted improvements. High-AIC users saw outsized gains; low-AIC users saw limited or negative returns. Scaffolding reduced outcome variance.

Conclusion: GenAI creates a new axis of capability inequality based on AI interaction skills. Firms should provide AIC micro-training and standardized procedures to ensure consistent value capture and reduce uneven adoption outcomes.

Abstract: Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) is transforming how firms create, process, and apply knowledge, yet little is known about the heterogeneity of its productivity effects across users. We report results from a randomized controlled experiment in which participants-analogs of early-career knowledge workers-were assigned to self-study a technical domain using either traditional resources or large-language-model (LLM) assistance. On average, GenAI access significantly increased task performance, but the distribution of gains was highly uneven. Improvements were not predicted by GPA or prior knowledge, but by \textit{AI Interaction Competence (AIC)} -- the ability to elicit, filter, and verify model outputs. High-AIC participants realized outsized gains; low-AIC participants saw limited or even negative marginal returns. A scaffolding intervention (conceptual maps) reduced outcome variance, indicating that standardized workflows can mitigate inequality in AI-mediated performance.
