We sketch such an architecture and derive the compositional system-level safety bounds it admits via the chain rule of probability. Three open problems stand between this and a deployable standard: bound estimation from non-i.i.d.\ traces, graceful degradation of contracts under deployment drift, and extension to multi-agent settings -- the most important unfinished business in LLM agent runtime assurance.

</details>


### [128] [Efficient Lookahead Encoding and Abstracted Width for Learning General Policies in Classical Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18674)
*Michael Aichmüller,Simon Ståhlberg,Martin Funkquist,Hector Geffner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper improves Iterated Width (IW) policies for generalized planning by: (1) introducing a holistic encoding of the IW(1) search tree that allows Relational GNNs to score all transitions in a single forward pass, and (2) defining Abstracted IW(1) that uses type-based relational abstraction during novelty checks to scale from atoms to objects. The approach achieves state-of-the-art performance on IPC 2023 benchmarks, surpassing prior work and the classical planner LAMA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing IW policies evaluate each transition individually, leading to unscalable compute costs and expressivity limitations. Additionally, IW(1) becomes inefficient with thousands of objects, as seen in IPC 2023 benchmarks.

Method: (1) A holistic encoding that jointly represents IW(1)-reachable states via relational differences to the current state, enabling R-GNNs to score all transitions in one forward pass. (2) Abstracted IW(1), which abstracts atoms by replacing all but one argument with its type during novelty checks, shifting scaling from atoms to objects.

Result: New state-of-the-art performance on the IPC 2023 benchmark and across diverse domains, including those requiring features beyond the C2 logic fragment, significantly surpassing prior work and LAMA.

Conclusion: The proposed holistic encoding and abstracted novelty check address key scalability and expressivity limitations of IW-based generalized planning, enabling effective scaling to large-instance domains.

Abstract: Generalized planning aims to learn policies that generalize across collections of instances within a classical planning domain. Recent Graph Neural Network (GNN) approaches have learned nearly perfect policies for several domains. This work improves on the recently published idea of Iterated Width (IW) policies. Therein, the policy broadens its successor scope through an IW-lookahead search that can "jump" over multiple transitions, simplifying the problem structure. Yet, each transition is evaluated individually, leading to unscalable compute costs and expressivity limitations. Furthermore, although IW(1) is attractive because it scales linearly with the number of atoms, it becomes inefficient once thousands of objects are considered, as in the International Planning Competition (IPC) 2023 benchmark. We address both limitations. First, we introduce a vastly more efficient holistic encoding of the entire search tree. It jointly represents IW(1)-reachable states only by their relational differences to the current state, enabling Relational GNNs (R-GNNs) to score all transitions in a single forward pass. Second, we define Abstracted IW(1) to improve scaling through relational abstraction during novelty checks. Rather than testing fully instantiated atoms, it abstracts each atom by replacing all but one argument with its type. The original atom is novel if any of its abstracted forms is novel. This structural compression shifts novelty search scaling from atoms to objects, while preserving meaningful subgoal structure. We evaluate our contributions on the hyperscaling IPC 2023 benchmark and across diverse domains, including domains requiring features beyond the $C_2$ logic fragment. Our policies achieve new state-of-the-art performance, significantly surpassing prior work, including the classical planner LAMA.

</details>


### [129] [Learning Quantifiable Visual Explanations Without Ground-Truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18681)
*Amritpal Singh,Andrey Barsky,Mohamed Ali Souibgui,Ernest Valveny,Dimosthenis Karatzas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a quantifiable metric for XAI quality based on continuous input perturbation, and a novel XAI method using differentiable approximation of this metric as supervision to train adapters on top of black-box models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: XAI techniques are difficult to evaluate due to lack of good ground-truth for comparison.

Method: 1) A metric based on continuous input perturbation that formally considers sufficiency and necessity of attributed information. 2) A novel XAI method using differentiable approximation of the metric as a supervision signal to train an adapter on top of any black-box model, producing causal explanations.

Result: The proposed metric aligns better with human intuitions than existing metrics. The new XAI method outperforms competing techniques across multiple quantifiable metrics without degrading model performance.

Conclusion: The framework provides both a reliable evaluation metric for XAI quality and a method to generate superior explanations via differentiable optimization of that metric.

Abstract: Explainable AI (XAI) techniques are increasingly important for the validation and responsible use of modern deep learning models, but are difficult to evaluate due to the lack of good ground-truth to compare against. We propose a framework that serves as a quantifiable metric for the quality of XAI methods, based on continuous input perturbation. Our metric formally considers the sufficiency and necessity of the attributed information to the model's decision-making, and we illustrate a range of cases where it aligns better with human intuitions of explanation quality than do existing metrics.
  To exploit the properties of this metric, we also propose a novel XAI method, considering the case where we fine-tune a model using a differentiable approximation of the metric as a supervision signal. The result is an adapter module that can be trained on top of any black-box model to output causal explanations of the model's decision process, without degrading model performance. We show that the explanations generated by this method outperform those of competing XAI techniques according to a number of quantifiable metrics.

</details>


### [130] [Democratizing Large-Scale Re-Optimization with LLM-Guided Model Patches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18692)
*Tinghan Ye,Arnaud Deza,Ved Mohan,El Mehdi Er Raqabi,Pascal Van Hentenryck*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework using LLMs as OR experts to enable end users to interactively re-optimize deployed optimization models via natural language, using a toolbox of primal-based and solver-aware techniques, validated on supply chain and exam scheduling cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world optimization models face dynamic changes (evolving rules, overlooked constraints, perturbations), and end users lack OR expertise to rapidly re-optimize, creating a dependency on OR experts and limiting sustainability of decision-support systems.

Method: An agentic re-optimization framework where an LLM acts as an OR expert: (1) translates user natural-language prompts into structured patch-based model updates, (2) selects re-optimization techniques from a toolbox (historical solutions, valid inequalities, solver configs, metaheuristics), and (3) solves the instance to return implementable solutions.

Result: On two large-scale real-world cases (online supply chain re-optimization and offline university exam scheduling), the framework demonstrated effectiveness and scalability. The toolbox-driven architecture improved computational efficiency via primal-based and solver-aware techniques, and structured patch-based updates enhanced interpretability and traceability.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enables interactive, continuous adaptation of deployed optimization models, reduces dependence on OR experts, and improves sustainability of decision-support systems through LLM-driven natural-language interaction and a specialized re-optimization toolbox.

Abstract: Optimization models developed by operations research (OR) experts are often deployed as decision-support systems in industrial settings. However, real-world environments are dynamic, with evolving business rules, previously overlooked constraints, and unforeseen perturbations. In such contexts, end users must rapidly re-optimize models to recover feasible and implementable solutions. This paper introduces an agentic re-optimization framework in which a large language model (LLM) acts as an OR expert, dynamically supporting end users through natural-language interaction. The LLM translates user prompts into structured updates of the underlying optimization model, selects suitable re-optimization techniques from an optimization toolbox, and solves the resulting instance to return implementable solutions. The toolbox leverages primal information, including historical solutions, valid inequalities, solver configurations, and metaheuristics, to accelerate re-optimization while preserving solution quality. The proposed framework enables interactive and continuous adaptation of deployed optimization models, reducing dependence on OR experts and improving the sustainability of decision-support systems. Extensive experiments on two complementary large-scale real-world case studies demonstrate the effectiveness and scalability of the proposed framework. The first considers online supply chain re-optimization, where solutions must be generated rapidly while remaining close to the deployed plan, whereas the second focuses on offline university exam scheduling, where solution quality is prioritized over runtime. Results show that the toolbox-driven architecture significantly improves computational efficiency through primal-based and solver-aware re-optimization techniques, while the structured patch-based updates improve interpretability and traceability of model modifications.

</details>


### [131] [SkillGenBench: Benchmarking Skill Generation Pipelines for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18693)
*Yifan Zhou,Zhentao Zhang,Ziming Cheng,Shuo Zhang,Qizhen Lan,Zhangquan Chen,Zhi Yang,QianyuXu,Ronghao Chen,Huacan Wang,Sen Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillGenBench is a benchmark that evaluates LLM agents' ability to generate correct, reusable, and executable skills from raw corpora (repositories and documents) under two regimes (task-conditioned and task-agnostic) and two source types (repository-grounded and document-grounded), using deterministic execution-based checks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks evaluate skill efficacy or downstream task solving, but none isolate skill generation itself as a standalone research problem. As LLM agents increasingly rely on reusable skills, the field needs a controlled, unified protocol to assess whether agents can generate correct skills from raw corpora.

Method: SkillGenBench defines a generator that receives raw corpora and produces standardized skill artifacts, which are executed under fixed harnesses with unified evaluation. It covers two regimes: task-conditioned (skill after task reveal) and task-agnostic (library before tasks known). It spans two source types: repository-grounded (code/config/scripts) and document-grounded (long-form text). Evaluation uses deterministic execution-based checks plus auxiliary diagnosis signals.

Result: Experiments across various skill-generation methods and backbones reveal substantial performance variation, highlight the difficulty of reusable skill distillation, and show distinct failure modes between software repository and long-form document sources.

Conclusion: SkillGenBench establishes a reproducible testbed for studying skill generation as an independent research problem in agent systems, filling a gap between skill-use benchmarks and downstream task benchmarks.

Abstract: As LLM agents are increasingly built around reusable skills, a central challenge is no longer only whether agents can use provided skills, but whether they can generate correct, reusable, and executable skills from repositories and documents. Existing benchmarks primarily evaluate the efficacy of given skills or the ability of agents to solve downstream tasks from raw context, but they do not isolate skill generation itself as the object of study. We introduce SkillGenBench, a benchmark for evaluating skill generation pipelines under a unified and controlled protocol. In SkillGenBench, a generator receives raw corpora and produces standardized skill artifacts, which are then executed under fixed harnesses and assessed with unified evaluation procedures. The benchmark covers two generation regimes: task-conditioned generation, where a task-specific skill is synthesized after the task is revealed, and task-agnostic generation, where a reusable skill library must be distilled before downstream tasks are known. It also spans two complementary procedural sources: repository-grounded instances, where procedures are distributed across code, configuration, and scripts, and document-grounded instances, where procedures and constraints must be distilled from long-form text. We provide standardized task specifications, pinned environments, and evaluation protocols centered on deterministic execution-based checks, supplemented by auxiliary signals for diagnosis. Experiments across a range of skill-generation methods and backbones show substantial performance variation, highlight the difficulty of reusable skill distillation, and reveal distinct failure modes in skill generation from software repositories versus long-form documents. SkillGenBench establishes a reproducible testbed for studying skill generation as an independent research problem in agent systems.

</details>


### [132] [What Does the AI Doctor Value? Auditing Pluralism in the Clinical Ethics of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18738)
*Payal Chandak,Victoria Alkin,David Wu,Maya Dagan,Taposh Dutta Roy,Maria Clara Saad Menezes,Ayush Noori,Nirali Somia,John S. Brownstein,Ran Balicer,Rebecca W. Brendel,Noa Dagan,Isaac S. Kohane,Gabriel A. Brat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper audits value pluralism in medical AI, finding that while frontier LLMs exhibit physician-level heterogeneity in reasoning (Overton pluralism), their individual decisions are near-deterministic, reflecting systematic value preferences—some of which underweight patient autonomy—risking a "deployment monoculture" that replaces clinical pluralism with a single model's ethical stance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medicine is inherently pluralistic, with ethical principles (autonomy, beneficence, nonmaleficence, justice) routinely conflicting. The ethical values that LLMs bring to medical advice have not been systematically examined, raising concerns about unintended value imposition at scale.

Method: The authors developed a framework for auditing value pluralism in medical AI, comprising (1) a benchmark of clinician-verified ethical dilemmas and (2) an attribution method that recovers value priorities directly from model decisions. They evaluated frontier LLMs on these dilemmas, comparing with a physician panel.

Result: Frontier models span physician-level value heterogeneity and exhibit Overton pluralism (discussing competing values in reasoning). However, individual model decisions are near-deterministic across repeated sampling and semantic variations, failing to reproduce the distributional pluralism of physicians. These consistent decisions reflect committed, systematic value preferences; while most fall within inter-physician variation, some significantly underweight patient autonomy.

Conclusion: Without explicit efforts to balance ethical perspectives within or across models, LLMs risk replacing clinical pluralism with a deployment monoculture, amplifying a single model's value priorities at scale to every patient served.

Abstract: Medicine is inherently pluralistic. Principles such as autonomy, beneficence, nonmaleficence, and justice routinely conflict, and such ethical dilemmas often sharply divide reasonable physicians. Good clinical practice navigates these tensions in concert with each patient's values rather than imposing a single ethical stance. The ethical values that large language models bring to medical advice, however, have not been systematically examined. We present a framework for auditing value pluralism in medical AI, comprising a benchmark of clinician-verified dilemmas and an attribution method that recovers value priorities directly from decisions. The ecosystem of frontier models spans physician-level value heterogeneity, and models discuss competing values in their reasoning (Overton pluralism) before committing to a decision. However, individual model decisions are near-deterministic across repeated sampling and semantic variations, failing to reproduce the distributional pluralism of the physician panel. Across benchmark cases, these consistent decisions reflect committed, systematic value preferences. While most model priorities fall within the natural range of inter-physician variation, some significantly underweight patient autonomy. A single LLM deployed without regard for its value priorities could amplify those priorities at scale to every patient it serves. Without explicit efforts to balance ethical perspectives with one or multiple models, these tools risk replacing clinical pluralism with a deployment monoculture.

</details>


### [133] [Actionable World Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18743)
*Kunqi Xu,Jitao Li,Jianglong Ye,Tianshu Tang,Isabella Liu,Sifei Liu,Xueyan Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WorldString is a neural architecture that models real-world object state manifolds from point clouds or RGB-D video, serving as a differentiable digital twin and foundational building block for physical world models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for modeling object action states rely on either video generation or dynamic scene reconstruction, but none explicitly model objects as fundamental, actionable primitives in a unified, principled way to build actionable object representations for physical world models.

Method: WorldString learns directly from point clouds or RGB-D video streams to model the state manifold of real-world objects using a fully differentiable neural architecture that serves as a digital twin.

Result: The architecture provides a fully differentiable structure that seamlessly enables integration with policy learning and neural dynamics, acting as a foundational building block for physical world models.

Conclusion: WorldString offers a unified, principled approach to modeling actionable object representations as digital twins, filling a gap left by video generation and dynamic scene reconstruction methods, and enabling future integration with downstream tasks like policy learning.

Abstract: Inspired by the emergent behaviors in large language models that generalized human intelligence, the research community is pursuing similar emergent capabilities within world models, with a emphasis on modeling the physical world. Within the scope of physical world model, objects are the fundamental primitives that constitute physical reality. From humans to computers, nearly everything we interact with is an object. These objects are rarely static; they are actionable entities with varying states determined by their intrinsic properties. While current methods approach object action states either via video generation or dynamic scene reconstruction, none explicitly model this basic element in a unified, principled way to build an actionable object representation. We propose WorldString, a neural architecture capable of modeling the state manifold of real-world objects by learning directly from point clouds or RGB-D video streams. Serving as a versatile digital twin, it acts as a foundational building block for physical world models; thus, we name it WorldString. Sweetly, its fully differentiable structure seamlessly enables future integration with policy learning and neural dynamics.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [134] [Haptic Rendering of Fractional-Order Viscoelasticity: Passivity and Rendering Fidelity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16389)
*Gorkem Gemalmaz,Harun Tolasa,Volkan Patoglu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper provides a unified passivity framework for fractional-order viscoelastic haptic rendering under short-memory discretization, with theoretical derivations, experimental validation, and human-subject studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Haptic rendering of viscoelastic materials with creep and stress relaxation (e.g., biological tissues) requires accurate models. Fractional-order models capture memory effects with few parameters, but their passivity under finite-memory discretization was not well understood.

Method: Closed-form passivity conditions derived for fractional-order standard linear solid (SLS) models using Grünwald-Letnikov derivative under short-memory discretization; symbolic expressions for effective stiffness and damping obtained.

Result: Passivity conditions form a unified framework that generalizes integer-order Kelvin-Voigt, Maxwell, and SLS models as special cases. Theoretical bounds were experimentally validated, and human-subject evaluations confirmed perceived realism.

Conclusion: Establishes a unified theoretical and experimental framework for fractional-order viscoelastic haptic rendering under short-memory discretization, enabling stable and realistic simulation of time-dependent viscoelastic materials.

Abstract: Haptic rendering of viscoelastic materials that exhibit creep and stress relaxation is crucial for many applications, such as medical training with realistic biological tissue models. Fractional-order viscoelastic models provide an effective means of describing intrinsically time-dependent dynamics with few parameters, as these models can naturally capture memory effects. In this study, we present analyses of passivity and rendering performance for fractional-order viscoelastic models under finite-memory discretization. We derive closed-form expressions to ensure the passivity of haptic rendering with a fractional-order (FO) standard linear solid (SLS) model based on Grunwald-Letnikov derivative under short-memory discretization. We also provide symbolic expressions for the effective stiffness and damping of such FO-SLS models. The resulting passivity conditions constitute a unified framework that generalizes previously reported results for integer-order Kelvin-Voigt, Maxwell, and SLS models, since these results are special cases of the newly derived condition. Furthermore, we provide experimental validations of the theoretical passivity bounds and human-subject evaluations of perceived realism of FO-SLS models. Overall, this study establishes a unified theoretical framework and experimental evaluations for FO viscoelastic rendering under short-memory discretization.

</details>


### [135] [OrbiSim: World Models as Differentiable Physics Engines for Embodied Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16395)
*Jiajian Li,Jingyuan Huang,Junru Gong,Qi Wang,Xiaokang Yang,Yunbo Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: OrbiSim is a fully differentiable physics engine for embodied intelligence that unifies scene assets, neural dynamics, and reinforcement learning into an end-to-end differentiable simulation loop, outperforming prior world models in predictive fidelity and control performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior world models are limited to unconstrained imagination in latent or visual domains and lack physical grounding, making tasks like differentiable contact modeling, gradient-based policy optimization under sparse rewards, and intuitive physical inference intractable for classical simulators.

Method: OrbiSim establishes a unified, physically-grounded pathway bridging structured scene assets, neural dynamics, and downstream reinforcement learning with end-to-end differentiability throughout the entire simulation loop—from explicit state transitions to visual observation generation.

Result: OrbiSim significantly outperforms state-of-the-art world models in both predictive fidelity and control performance, and shows consistent responsiveness to asset configurations and physical parameters.

Conclusion: OrbiSim demonstrates potential as a differentiable tool for enhancing robot simulation and policy training, enabling tasks previously intractable for classical simulators.

Abstract: We present OrbiSim, a novel robotic simulation paradigm that redefines world models as a fully differentiable physics engine for embodied intelligence. Unlike prior world models that focus on unconstrained imagination in latent or visual domains, OrbiSim establishes a unified, physically-grounded pathway that bridges structured scene assets, neural dynamics, and downstream reinforcement learning. By enabling end-to-end differentiability throughout the entire simulation loop -- spanning from explicit state transitions to visual observation generation -- OrbiSim supports tasks traditionally intractable for classical simulators, such as differentiable contact modeling, gradient-based policy optimization under sparse rewards, and intuitive physical inference. Empirical results demonstrate that OrbiSim significantly outperforms state-of-the-art world models in both predictive fidelity and control performance. Furthermore, its consistent responsiveness to asset configurations and physical parameters suggests its potential as a differentiable tool for enhancing robot simulation and policy training.

</details>


### [136] [Support-Safe Variational Hybrid Filtering for Contact-Mode and Sparse-Law Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16398)
*Marios Papamichalis,Regina Ruane*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VHYDRO is a variational hybrid dynamics learner that prevents branch loss in contact-rich robot dynamics by mixing learned proposals with feasible transition laws, ensuring support coverage for stable filtering and enabling sparse port-Hamiltonian recovery of discrete contact modes and continuous latent states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contact-rich robot dynamics are hybrid (continuous + discrete modes), and standard amortized filters that assign zero probability to feasible contact transitions permanently lose the branch the robot actually follows.

Method: VHYDRO mixes the learned proposal with a feasible transition law before sampling and importance weighting, jointly infers continuous latent state and discrete contact mode, and fits a sparse port-Hamiltonian law to each recovered regime.

Result: Three guarantees: (1) support coverage stabilizes filtering, (2) stabilized filter concentrates discrete contact posterior on coherent regimes, (3) mode-pure segments admit sparse port-Hamiltonian recovery. Empirically: support-safe filter stays usable under occlusion; discrete states form temporally coherent segments outperforming baselines on ARI, change-point F1, segment purity; mode-conditioned sparse fit recovers active physical terms.

Conclusion: VHYDRO's defensive proposal mechanism prevents branch loss, yielding stable filtering, coherent discrete mode segmentation, and interpretable physics recovery, with recovery error cleanly separated into filtering, derivative, mode-impurity, and physics-residual components.

Abstract: Contact-rich robot dynamics are hybrid: a single observation can match several latent states and contact regimes (free, impact, stick--slip). A standard amortized filter that places no probability on a feasible contact transition will permanently lose the branch the robot actually follows. We introduce VHYDRO, a variational hybrid dynamics learner that prevents this branch loss. At each step, VHYDRO mixes the learned proposal with a feasible transition law before sampling and importance weighting, ensuring that every transition retained by the model-feasible carrier remains covered. VHYDRO jointly infers a continuous latent state and a discrete contact mode, and fits a sparse port-Hamiltonian law to each recovered regime. On top of this, three guarantees connect: support coverage stabilizes filtering, the stabilized filter concentrates the discrete contact posterior on coherent regimes, and mode-pure segments admit sparse port-Hamiltonian recovery. The recovery error separates cleanly into filtering, derivative, mode-impurity, and physics-residual parts. Three empirical findings track the same mechanism. Under heavy occlusion the support-safe filter stays usable while a non-defensive proposal collapses. On ManiSkill demonstrations and on four Sawyer/BridgeData task families the discrete state forms temporally coherent contact-regime segments that the discrete state yields a stronger joint profile across ARI, change-point F1, and segment purity than post-hoc and mode-free baselines. On hybrid systems with known equations the mode-conditioned sparse fit recovers the active physical terms; purely predictive baselines do not.

</details>


### [137] [SCAR: Self-Supervised Continuous Action Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16412)
*Hongjia Liu,Fan Feng,Minghao Fu,Xinyue Wang,Haofei Lu,Biwei Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SCAR is a joint inverse-forward dynamics framework that learns unified, transferable latent action representations across embodiments by regularizing latent actions with a Gaussian prior and adversarial invariance, enabling stronger cross-embodiment world modeling under limited data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning transferable action representations from visual transitions is challenging when world models must generalize across embodiments under limited data. The paper argues that action is a distinct representational factor that decouples controllable change from embodiment-specific actuation, motivating a framework to learn unified action representations.

Method: SCAR builds on a pretrained generative backbone, using an inverse dynamics model (IDM) to infer latent actions from latent observation pairs and a forward dynamics model (FDM) to predict future dynamics conditioned on them. It regularizes the latent action posterior toward a standard Gaussian prior to limit arbitrary visual encoding, and uses adversarial invariance to suppress embodiment- and environment-specific nuisance factors.

Result: Experiments on Procgen and Robotwin datasets show that the learned unified latent action representation serves as a stronger conditioning interface for world modeling than embodiment-specific raw actions, yielding improved cross-embodiment low-data adaptation and cross-task transfer.

Conclusion: Action can be learned as a shared representation of controllable change across embodiments, providing an interface for more transferable and generalizable world models.

Abstract: Despite the central role of action in embodied intelligence, learning transferable action representations from visual transitions remains a fundamental challenge, particularly when world models must generalize across embodiments under limited data. We argue that action is not merely an auxiliary conditioning signal, but a distinct representational factor that decouples the controllable change from embodiment-specific actuation. In this work, we propose SCAR, a joint inverse-forward dynamics framework for learning unified action representations across embodiments from visual transitions. Built on a pretrained generative backbone, SCAR uses an inverse dynamics model (IDM) to infer latent actions from latent observation pairs and a forward dynamics model (FDM) to predict future dynamics conditioned on them. To make the latent space transferable rather than a generic visual bottleneck, we regularize the latent action posterior toward a standard Gaussian prior to limit arbitrary visual encoding, and introduce adversarial invariance to suppress embodiment- and environment-specific nuisance factors. Experiments on the Procgen and Robotwin dataset show that the learned unified latent action representation serves as a stronger conditioning interface for world modeling than embodiment-specific raw actions, yielding improved cross-embodiment low-data adaptation and cross-task transfer. Taken together, these results suggest that action can be learned as a shared representation of controllable change across embodiments, providing an interface for more transferable and generalizable world models.

</details>


### [138] [MR-SLAM: Immersive Spatial Supervision for Multi-Robot Mapping via Mixed Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16432)
*Prakash Aryan,Cem Erdogdu,Kavinaya Kumarchokkappan,Timo Kehrer,Sebastiano Panichella*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MR-SLAM is a mixed reality system that uses a Meta Quest 3 headset to teleoperate three simulated TurtleBot3 robots for multi-robot SLAM, achieving real-time occupancy grid merging, high scan rates, and cross-instance consistency on consumer hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Operating multi-robot SLAM fleets requires the operator to maintain spatial awareness of each robot's position and mapping state, a task that scales poorly on conventional 2D interfaces.

Method: The system uses a Meta Quest 3 headset for teleoperation via passthrough view with real-world occlusion, spatially anchored dashboard panels for in-situ mapping progress, and a ROS 2 back end that merges occupancy grids from independent SLAM Toolbox instances running on each simulated TurtleBot3 robot.

Result: Across five 9-minute sessions: scans at 8.83±0.16 Hz, mapped 17.9±0.8 m² of merged occupancy, 94.7±0.5% cross-instance occupancy consistency. An additional session: 6.3 ms median transform jitter, 26.7 m² coverage of a 41 m² grid.

Conclusion: MR-SLAM serves as a reference implementation for combining passthrough mixed reality supervision with multi-robot SLAM on consumer hardware.

Abstract: Operating a multi-robot fleet for simultaneous localization and mapping (SLAM) in applications such as building inspection or warehouse-aisle monitoring requires the operator to maintain spatial awareness of each robot's position and mapping state, a task that scales poorly on conventional 2D interfaces. We present MR-SLAM, a mixed reality (MR) system in which an operator wearing a Meta Quest 3 headset teleoperates three simulated TurtleBot3 robots through a passthrough view with real-world occlusion, while spatially anchored dashboard panels report mapping progress in situ. Each robot runs an independent SLAM Toolbox instance whose occupancy grid is merged in real time on a Robot Operating System 2 (ROS 2) back end. Across five 9-minute evaluation sessions, the system delivered scans at 8.83 +/- 0.16 Hz, mapped 17.9 +/- 0.8 m^2 of merged occupancy, and reached 94.7 +/- 0.5% cross-instance occupancy consistency across robot pairs. An additional session recorded 6.3 ms median transform jitter and 26.7 m^2 coverage of a 41 m^2 grid. We position MR-SLAM as a reference implementation for combining passthrough mixed reality supervision with multi-robot SLAM on consumer hardware.

</details>


### [139] [Hierarchical Two-Stage Framework for Environment-Aware Long-Horizon Vessel Trajectory Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16442)
*Ganeshaaraj Gnanavel,Tharindu Fernando,Sridha Sridharan,Clinton Fookes*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A hierarchical two-stage vessel trajectory forecasting framework combining coarse long-term and grid-aware short-term predictors with environmental module and learnable smoothing, achieving 25% ADE and 17% FDE improvements over SOTA on Australian CTS data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon vessel trajectory forecasting under real ocean conditions is critical for collision avoidance, traffic management, and route planning, but faces challenges from long-range temporal dependencies and dynamic environmental factors like currents, wind, and waves.

Method: Proposes a hierarchical two-stage framework: (1) coarse long-term branch encoding navigational intent, (2) grid-aware short-term branch using Spatio-Temporal Graph Transformer on discretized maritime cells, integrated via hierarchical fusion. Includes an environmental module incorporating oceanographic parameters (surface currents, wind, wave height) via cross-modal attention and feature-wise modulation, plus a learnable Savitzky-Golay smoothing layer.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art by 25% in Average Displacement Error (ADE) and 17% in Final Displacement Error (FDE) on Australian CTS data (North West region) with 3-hour input and 10-hour prediction horizon.

Conclusion: The hierarchical two-stage framework with environmental integration and temporal smoothing significantly advances long-horizon vessel trajectory forecasting under dynamic ocean conditions.

Abstract: Long-horizon vessel trajectory forecasting under real ocean conditions is critical for collision avoidance, traffic management, and route planning. However, achieving accurate predictions is challenging due to long-range temporal dependencies and dynamic environmental factors such as currents, wind, and waves. To address these issues, we propose a hierarchical two-stage framework that combines a coarse long-term predictor with a grid-aware short-term predictor through a hierarchical fusion mechanism. The short-term branch leverages a Spatio-Temporal Graph Transformer on discretized maritime cells to capture localized dynamics, while the long-term branch encodes overarching navigational intent. An integrated environmental module incorporates oceanographic parameters, including surface currents, wind vectors, and significant wave height, using cross-modal attention and feature-wise modulation for adaptive response to varying sea conditions. Additionally, a learnable Savitzky-Golay smoothing layer enhances temporal coherence in fused trajectories. We evaluate our approach on Australian Craft Tracking System (CTS) data from the North West region, aligned with Copernicus Marine Service products, using a 3-hour input and a 10-hour prediction horizon. Experimental results show that our framework outperforms the state-of-the-art by 25% in Average Displacement Error (ADE) and 17% in Final Displacement Error (FDE). Ablation studies further validate the contribution of each component.

</details>


### [140] [No Plan, Yet Human: A Reactive Robotics Model Predicts Human Planning Failures on a Clinical Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16514)
*Michael Migacev,Vito Mengers,Antonia Köngeter,Oliver Brock*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AICON, a reactive gradient-descent framework from robotics, reproduces human difficulty ordering on Tower of London problems and captures the behavior of groups with reduced planning capacity (e.g., Parkinson's patients) better than planning-based models, while planning models better fit healthy controls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop models that go beyond average performance and capture the specific pattern of which sequential planning problems are harder, ideally replicating human failure modes when planning capacity is reduced.

Method: Applied AICON, a reactive gradient-descent framework originally developed for robotic manipulation, to the Tower of London test without any lookahead planning or knowledge of human cognition, and compared it to structural task parameters and a planning baseline across 24 problems with leave-two-out evaluation.

Result: AICON reproduces fine-grained human difficulty ordering better than structural parameters, generalizes to held-out problems, and outperforms a planning baseline for groups with reduced planning capacity, while the planning baseline better captures healthy controls.

Conclusion: As planning capacity is reduced, human behavior shifts toward the reactive mode AICON models. AICON's core abstraction appears to reflect something real about how biological systems are organized, as it captures aspects of biological behavior across perception, eye movements, and sequential planning despite being originally built for robotics.

Abstract: Understanding why some sequential planning problems are harder than others requires models that go beyond average performance. They should capture the specific pattern of which problems are hard, and ideally fail in the same way people do when planning capacity is reduced. We apply AICON, a reactive gradient-descent framework developed for robotic manipulation, to the Tower of London test, a cognitive test used to assess planning in Parkinson's disease, mild cognitive impairment, and stroke. Without any lookahead planning or knowledge of human cognition, AICON reproduces the fine-grained human difficulty ordering across 24 problems better than structural task parameters and generalizes to held-out problems in a leave-two-out evaluation. Crucially, AICON outperforms a planning baseline for groups with reduced planning capacity while the planning baseline better captures healthy controls. This dissociation was predicted by the original AICON paper, which noted that the model's failure modes resemble those of Parkinson's patients who struggle with goal hierarchies but not move counts. This suggests that as planning capacity is reduced, human behavior shifts toward the reactive mode AICON models. The finding extends a broader pattern: AICON, originally built for robotics, now captures aspects of biological behavior across perception, eye movements, and sequential planning, suggesting its core abstraction reflects something real about how biological systems are organized.

</details>


### [141] [A Mechanistic Model for Collective Motion from Sensorimotor Regularities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16522)
*Vito Mengers,Bao Duc Cao,Oliver Brock*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a sensorimotor model of collective behavior, grounded in what individual organisms can perceive and execute, that produces diverse group patterns (polarized motion, milling, rings) without prescribed interaction forces, with behavioral transitions governed by measurable biological parameters like field of view, sensory noise, turning agility, and memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-propelled particle models are fundamentally descriptive — they reproduce collective phenomena through abstract interaction forces but leave open the question of how collective behavior is actually produced. Recent empirical findings (e.g., locusts not aligning with neighbors) highlight the need for mechanistic models operating at the sensorimotor level.

Method: A modeling framework from robotics is extended to collective motion: each agent perceives neighbors via bearing and apparent-size cues within a limited field of view, maintains uncertain internal state estimates, and selects actions through gradient descent on a desired social distance—without any prescribed interaction forces.

Result: The model produces diverse collective behaviors including polarized motion, milling, ring formations, and subgroup fragmentation. Global sensitivity analysis reveals behavioral transitions are governed by sensorimotor parameters corresponding to measurable biological quantities: field of view geometry, sensory noise, turning agility, and memory.

Conclusion: Collective behavior can be understood as emergent from interacting sensorimotor regularities, and differences across species arise from differences in embodiment and environment rather than from species-specific interaction rules.

Abstract: Collective behavior in animals has long been modeled through self-propelled particle models, which reproduce striking group-level phenomena through abstract interaction forces. Yet these models are fundamentally descriptive: they leave open the question of how collective behavior is actually produced. Recent empirical work makes this gap concrete: locusts do not align with neighbors, sensory and cognitive mechanisms mediate interaction instead. A mechanistic model must therefore operate at the sensorimotor level, grounded in what individual organisms can actually perceive, estimate, and physically execute. We present such a model based on a modeling framework from robotics, extended here to collective motion. Each agent perceives neighbors through bearing and apparent-size cues within a limited field of view, maintains uncertain internal state estimates, and selects actions through gradient descent on a desired social distance -- without any prescribed interaction forces. This simple model produces diverse collective behaviors including polarized motion, milling, ring formations, and subgroup fragmentation. A global sensitivity analysis shows that behavioral transitions are governed by sensorimotor parameters corresponding to measurable biological quantities: field of view geometry, sensory noise, turning agility, and memory. Collective behavior can therefore be understood as the emergent outcome of interacting sensorimotor regularities, and differences across species as the emergent outcome of differences in embodiment and environment.

</details>


### [142] [Nori Bot: A Sub-$1,000 Floor-to-Counter Mobile Manipulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16537)
*Antonio Li,Sungjoon Park,Wen Ni Chew*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Nori Bot is a $947 17-DoF dual-arm mobile manipulator that addresses three key limitations of sub-$1,000 platforms: fixed-height workspace (via 600mm Z-axis lift), reactive-only control (via OpenClaw proactive agent runtime), and servo burn-out (via software safety stack with current-based force feedback).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sub-$1,000 open-source mobile manipulators suffer from fixed-height workspaces, only reactive control, and lack protection against stall-induced servo burn-out (especially for cheap Feetech servos).

Method: (1) 600mm Z-axis lift integrated on the existing servo bus enabling floor-to-counter reach; (2) thin-client Raspberry Pi 4 with OpenClaw proactive agent runtime for autonomous task triggering via cron jobs/hooks; (3) software safety stack using motor current sensing on soft TPU fingers for sensorless grip-force feedback to prevent stall damage.

Result: Achieved a $947 17-DoF dual-arm mobile manipulator at ~3% the cost of comparable commercial platforms, addressing all three identified limitations.

Conclusion: Nori Bot demonstrates that proactive autonomy and hardware safety features can be integrated into ultra-low-cost mobile manipulators, with all code, CAD, and skill manifests to be released.

Abstract: Open-source mobile manipulators have reached $660 (XLeRobot) but every sub-$1,000 platform shares three limitations: a fixed-height workspace, reactive-only control, and no protection against the stall-induced burn-out that destroys cheap Feetech servos. We present Nori Bot, a 17-DoF dual-arm mobile manipulator at $947 (~3% the cost of comparable commercial platforms) that addresses all three: (1) a 600mm Z-axis lift on the existing servo bus for floor-to-counter reach; (2) a thin-client Raspberry Pi 4 paired with the OpenClaw proactive agent runtime so cron jobs and hooks trigger physical tasks autonomously; and (3) a software safety stack with sensorless grip-force feedback via motor current on a soft TPU finger. Code, CAD, and the skill manifest will be released.

</details>


### [143] [Policy Library CBF: Finite-Horizon Safety at Runtime via Parallel Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16588)
*Taekyung Kim,Hideki Okamoto,Bardh Hoxha,Georgios Fainekos,Dimitra Panagou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Policy Library Control Barrier Function (PL-CBF) uses a library of fallback policies with parallel finite-horizon rollouts as a runtime safety filter, selecting the least invasive safe mode and minimally modifying nominal policy via QP. Demonstrated on double-integrator, highway driving, and 3D quadrotor tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-critical autonomy in unstructured environments faces challenges for online safety certification under evolving constraints.

Method: PL-CBF evaluates a library of fallback policies via parallel finite-horizon rollouts, selects the least invasive safe mode, and enforces safety by solving a quadratic program that minimally modifies the nominal policy.

Result: Improved safety coverage over single-policy safety filters while retaining millisecond-level runtime on planar double-integrator, highway driving with friction changes, and 3D quadrotor navigation tasks.

Conclusion: PL-CBF effectively certifies finite-horizon safety with theoretical guarantees on policy-library coverage, outperforming single-policy filters in complex dynamic environments.

Abstract: Safety-critical autonomy in unstructured environments poses significant challenges for online safety certification under evolving constraints. We propose Policy Library Control Barrier Function~(PL-CBF), a runtime safety filter that evaluates a library of fallback policies via parallel finite-horizon rollouts, selects the least invasive safe mode, and enforces safety by solving a quadratic program that minimally modifies a nominal policy. We provide a theoretical analysis based on a finite-horizon language metric over closed-loop behaviors, characterizing policy-library coverage requirements for certifying finite-horizon safety. Simulations on a planar double-integrator (4 states), highway driving with abrupt friction changes using a realistic nonlinear vehicle model (8 states), and 3D quadrotor navigation in crowded dynamic environments (12 states) demonstrate improved safety coverage over single-policy safety filters while retaining millisecond-level runtime.

</details>


### [144] [Bayesian Networks for Path-Based Sensors: Gathering Information and Path Planning in Communication Denied Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16673)
*Alkesh K. Srivastava,George P. Kontoudis,Donald Sofge,Michael Otte*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces a Bayesian Network formulation for updating belief maps from path-based sensor observations (e.g., boolean hazard detection along continuous paths) and uses information-theoretic path planning to accelerate convergence. The method outperforms prior work in both single- and multi-robot scenarios, especially in communication-denied environments with false positives/negatives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on path-based sensor fusion approximated posteriors by averaging over alternative event histories, lacking a principled probabilistic framework. The authors aim to improve belief update accuracy and convergence speed using a more rigorous Bayesian Network model.

Method: A Bayesian Network formulation models probabilistic relationships between latent variables (spatial hazard locations) and path-based sensor measurements (binary returns: 0 = safe, 1 = hazard detected). The BN enables exact Bayesian belief updates, and paths are planned to maximize information gain (Shannon entropy reduction).

Result: The BN-based method achieves faster convergence of the belief map compared to prior approximation-based methods in both single-robot and multi-robot settings, even under false positive/negative conditions.

Conclusion: A principled Bayesian Network approach to path-based sensor fusion and information-theoretic path planning yields superior performance for spatial belief mapping in communication-denied environments with detection uncertainty.

Abstract: A "path-based sensor" produces a single observation along a continuous path. For example, a boolean path-based sensor returns a single "1" if an event of interest is detected at any point along the path and a "0" otherwise. Notably, a "1" provides no direct information about where along the path the event(s) may have occurred. Previous work has demonstrated that observations from multiple path-based sensors can be fused to create a Bayesian belief map over the spatial locations of the underlying event or phenomenon. Moreover, path planning can employ Shannon information theory to accelerate the rate of convergence of the belief map. In this paper, we present a new method to update the belief map based on a path-based sensor observation, and then plan paths to increase information gain. In contrast to prior work that approximates the posterior by averaging over the alternative event histories, we introduce a Bayesian Network (BN) formulation that models the probabilistic relationships between the latent variables and path-based sensor measurements, enabling a more principled Bayesian belief update. We consider static hazard detection in a communication-denied environment as a representative problem setting. The event of a robot returning from its path corresponds to a path-based hazard sensor reading of "0" (hazard not detected), while a robot failing to return corresponds to a reading of "1" (hazard detected). We consider false positives and false negatives. We find that the new method leads to quicker convergence of the belief map than prior work in both single- and multi-robot cases.

</details>


### [145] [DriveSafer: End-to-End Autonomous Driving with Safety Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16737)
*Shounak Sural,Raj Rajkumar*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DriveSafer is a failure-aware safety framework that reduces catastrophic failures in generative end-to-end autonomous driving planners by 48% compared to DiffusionDrive, using training-time safety constraints and inference-time safety guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern generative end-to-end planners suffer from catastrophic failures in safety-critical scenarios due to violations of physical constraints and safety requirements. The authors aim to reduce these failures rather than enhance average planning quality.

Method: DriveSafer employs a failure-aware safety framework with two components: (1) training-time safety constraints to steer learning towards safe behaviors, and (2) inference-time safety guidance to further enforce safety during deployment.

Result: On the NAVSIM benchmark, DriveSafer reduces catastrophic failures (PDMS=0) by 48% and drivable-area compliance failures by over 65% compared to the state-of-the-art DiffusionDrive model.

Conclusion: Targeted reduction of catastrophic planning failures through explicit safety constraints can significantly improve safety outcomes in generative end-to-end driving without necessarily improving average planning quality.

Abstract: End-to-End (E2E) autonomous driving models have shown growing capability in recent years, with performance improving on increasingly challenging benchmarks. However, modern generative E2E planners still suffer from a substantial number of catastrophic failures in safety-critical scenarios. We find that many such failures arise from violations of physical constraints and safety requirements, leading to unsafe behavior. Motivated by this finding, in this paper, we focus on improving safety outcomes in generative end-to-end driving with a targeted reduction of catastrophic planning failures, instead of enhancing average planning quality. Towards this end, we propose DriveSafer, a failure-aware safety framework for end-to-end planners. DriveSafer explicitly steers generative planners towards safe behaviors leveraging both training-time safety constraints and inference-time safety guidance. Compared to the state-of-the-art DiffusionDrive model, on the NAVSIM benchmark, DriveSafer reduces the number of catastrophic failures (PDMS=0) by 48%, with over 65% reduction in drivable-area compliance failures.

</details>


### [146] [LACE: Latent Visual Representation for Cross-Embodiment Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16743)
*Yoo Sung Jang,Kanchana Ranasinghe,Cristina Mata,Yichi Zhang,Jorge Mendez-Mendez,Michael S. Ryoo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LACE aligns human and robot visual representations by using correspondences between shared body parts (obtained via forward kinematics) as sparse supervision, enabling cross-embodiment policy learning from human demonstrations even with scarce robot data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-embodiment learning from human demonstrations is hindered by the visual gap between human and robot embodiments. SSL backbones fail to establish correspondence between human and robot hands.

Method: LACE aligns human and robot visual representations via: (1) correspondences between shared body parts across embodiments as sparse supervision (automatically obtained via forward kinematics), requiring only a single robot demonstration for training; (2) a semantic alignment loss that matches distributions incurred by corresponding features; (3) a Gram loss that preserves pretrained feature quality.

Result: In zero-shot transfer, policies using LACE-DINO outperform those using DINO by 65%, with consistent gains in low-data regimes and out-of-distribution environments.

Conclusion: LACE enables robot policies to leverage abundant human data when robot demonstrations are scarce through effective cross-embodiment visual representation alignment.

Abstract: Cross-embodiment learning from human demonstrations is hindered by the visual gap between human and robot embodiments. While self-supervised learning (SSL) backbones encode rich inter-class semantics of general objects, we show they fail to establish correspondence between human and robot hands. We propose LACE, a framework that aligns human and robot visual representations in the latent space of these backbones by leveraging correspondences between shared body parts across embodiments as sparse supervision. These annotations can be automatically obtained via forward kinematics, and single robot demonstration is sufficient to train the model. Our semantic alignment loss matches distributions incurred by corresponding features, lifting patch-level supervision to semantic-level alignment, while a Gram loss preserves pretrained feature quality. This alignment enables robot policies to leverage abundant human data when robot demonstrations are scarce: in zero-shot transfer, policies using LACE-DINO outperform those using DINO by a large margin (65\%), with consistent gains in low-data regimes and out-of-distribution environments.

</details>


### [147] ["I'm Not Mad, Just Focused'': Understanding Human Emotions in Human-Robot Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16816)
*Seung Chan Hong,Dana Kulić,Leimin Tian*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a vision language model (VLM)-based emotion recognition system for human-robot collaboration, demonstrating superior semantic similarity with human annotations and positive user preference for emotion-adaptive robot behavior in a delivery task user study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current emotion recognition models in HRC are limited by reliance on acted datasets and single-modality inputs (e.g., facial expressions), failing to capture contextual understanding necessary for real-world collaboration.

Method: The authors propose a VLM-based ER system, first evaluating its semantic and sentiment similarity against human annotations on an HRC dataset, then conducting a user study with a service robot in a collaborative delivery task to assess emotion-adaptive behavior modulation.

Result: The VLM-ER system achieves higher semantic similarity and positive sentiment alignment with human annotations compared to a baseline CNN-based system. Participants in the user study preferred the emotion-adaptive robot behavior facilitated by the VLM-ER system.

Conclusion: VLM-based emotion recognition leveraging contextual understanding improves emotion interpretation in HRC, and emotion-adaptive robot behavior is preferred by users, suggesting VLMs are a viable approach for affective HRC.

Abstract: Human-robot collaboration (HRC) can benefit from robots' abilities to interpret human emotional states. However, current emotion recognition (ER) models in HRC often fall short, particularly due to their reliance on acted datasets and single-modality inputs like facial expressions. We propose a novel vision language model (VLM)-based ER system that leverages contextual understanding to improve emotion interpretation in HRC. We first evaluate the VLM-ER system by assessing its semantic and sentiment similarity with human annotations on an existing HRC dataset. Then, in a user study with a service robot in a collaborative delivery task, we evaluate the effects of modulating the robot's behaviour based on the user's emotional state inferred by the VLM-ER system. The results show that the proposed VLM-ER system achieves higher semantic similarity and positive sentiment alignment with human annotations compared to a baseline convolutional neural network-based system. Further, participants in the user study preferred emotion-adaptive robot behaviour facilitated by the VLM-ER system.

</details>


### [148] [Pedestrian-Aware LLM-Driven Behavioral Planning for Autonomous Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16858)
*Aidana Baimbetova,Haruki Yonekura,Hamada Rizk,Hirozumi Yamaguchi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces an LLM-based decision-making framework for autonomous vehicles that converts scene observations into natural-language prompts to infer pedestrian intent and generate cautious driving decisions. It outperforms deep RL baselines (68% vs 17.7% collision-free rate in zero-shot, 96% with few-shot memory) and produces interpretable, human-aligned decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL-based AV control systems fail to generalize to unpredictable pedestrian interactions and out-of-distribution scenarios, relying on handcrafted rewards and opaque decisions that limit their suitability for safety-critical pedestrian-rich environments.

Method: The framework converts structured scene observations into natural-language reasoning prompts for an LLM, which infers pedestrian intent, anticipates risk, and generates tactical driving decisions. These are executed by a motion planner ensuring smooth, kinematically feasible control.

Result: Zero-shot LLM agent achieves 68% collision-free rate vs 17.7% for deep RL; few-shot episodic memory reaches 96.0% (vs DQN 82.0%); cross-behavior transfer yields 82-90% success; system produces earlier responses, wider safety buffers, and interpretable decisions.

Conclusion: The LLM-based framework significantly improves pedestrian-aware AV decision-making, offering better generalization, safety performance, and interpretability compared to traditional RL approaches.

Abstract: Autonomous Vehicles (AVs) must make reliable decisions in dense urban environments where pedestrian behavior is variable, sometimes abnormal, and often unseen during training. Reinforcement learning (RL)-based AV control systems perform well in structured traffic but struggle to generalize to unpredictable pedestrian interactions and out-of-distribution scenarios. Their reliance on handcrafted rewards and opaque decisions further limits their suitability for safety-critical, pedestrian-rich environments. To address these limitations, we introduce a Large Language Model (LLM)-based decision-making framework for pedestrian-aware behavioral planning. The system converts structured scene observations into natural-language reasoning prompts, enabling the LLM to infer pedestrian intent, anticipate risk, and generate cautious tactical driving decisions. These decisions are executed by a motion planner that ensures smooth, kinematically feasible control. We evaluate the framework in SUMO across multiple pedestrian-interaction scenarios, including unexpected jaywalking, turn-back crossing, hesitation, and bidirectional crossing. In zero-shot evaluation, the LLM-based agent achieves a 68% collision-free success rate, substantially outperforming deep RL baselines (17.7%). With few-shot episodic memory in a single-pedestrian scenario, performance increases to 96.0%, exceeding a custom DQN controller (82.0%). Cross-behavior evaluation further shows that memory derived from turn-back interactions transfers to unseen hesitation and bidirectional crossing scenarios, achieving 82.0% and 90.0% success, respectively. The system consistently initiates earlier responses, maintains wider safety buffers, and produces interpretable, human-aligned decisions.

</details>


### [149] [Plan First, Diffuse Later: Extrinsic Graph Guidance for Long-Horizon Diffusion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16863)
*Yaniv Hassidof,Adir Morgan,Yilun Du,Kiril Solovey*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: XDiffuser improves long-horizon planning with diffusion models by using extrinsic search (a lightweight plan over a state-space graph) to guide denoising, outperforming intrinsic search methods that are computationally expensive, especially on low-quality data and unseen combinatorial tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compositional diffusion models for long-horizon planning suffer from poor global coherence; intrinsic search improves it but at high computational cost due to repeated model evaluations.

Method: XDiffuser computes a plan over a state-space graph as a lightweight local connectivity oracle, then uses that plan to guide the denoising process for a single trajectory, offloading exploration outside the diffusion model.

Result: XDiffuser outperforms diffusion-based baselines on long-horizon tasks, with significant gains in low-quality data regimes and on unseen tasks such as multi-agent coordination and TSP-style reasoning.

Conclusion: Extrinsic search is more effective and efficient than intrinsic search for long-horizon planning with diffusion models, enabling generalization to unseen combinatorial tasks at test time.

Abstract: Compositional diffusion models offer a promising route to long-horizon planning by denoising multiple overlapping sub-trajectories while ensuring that together they constitute a global solution. However, enforcing local behavior over long chains is often insufficient for a coherent global structure to emerge. Recent works tackle this limitation through intrinsic search, which explores multiple paths during the denoising process. While intrinsic search improves global coherence, it comes at the cost of repeated evaluations of an already compute-heavy model. In this work, we argue that extrinsic search, performed outside the denoising process, offers a more effective mode of exploration for long-horizon planning while naturally enabling the use of classical algorithms to solve unseen combinatorial tasks at test time. Our eXtrinsic search-guided Diffuser (XDiffuser) first computes a plan over a state-space graph -- serving as a lightweight local connectivity oracle for the diffusion model. The plan is then used to guide denoising for a single trajectory, effectively offloading the burden of exploration. XDiffuser outperforms diffusion-based baselines on long-horizon tasks, with particularly large gains in the low-quality data regime and on unseen tasks beyond goal-reaching, including multi-agent coordination and TSP-style reasoning. Project website: https://yanivhass.github.io/XDiffuser-site/

</details>


### [150] [SADP: Subgoal-Aware Diffusion Policy for Explainable Robots Learned from Foundation Model Generated Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16871)
*Site Hu,Takato Horii*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SADP uses foundation models to automatically generate subgoal-annotated demonstrations, then trains diffusion policies conditioned on both task-level and subgoal-level descriptions, enabling robots to expose subgoal progress during long-horizon manipulation while achieving higher task success rates than baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most imitation learning methods lack explicit subgoal structure and cannot convey subtask execution progress during long-horizon manipulation, exacerbated by the scarcity of subgoal-level supervision in standard robot learning datasets.

Method: SADP leverages foundation models to autonomously generate subgoal-annotated demonstrations, trains diffusion policies conditioned on both task-level and subgoal-level descriptions, and uses a lightweight auxiliary head to predict subgoal completion states.

Result: SADP achieves higher task success rates than strong task-conditioned diffusion baselines in RLBench simulations and real-world UR5e evaluations, while providing subgoal-level execution signals for progress monitoring and failure diagnosis.

Conclusion: Built-in interpretability (via subgoal structuring) can coexist with high task performance, without relying on post-hoc explanations.

Abstract: Explainable robots require not only successful task execution but also the ability to expose internal decision-making process in a user-friendly manner. However, most imitation learning methods are trained solely on task-level demonstrations, without explicitly modeling subgoal structure or execution progress. This limitation is further exacerbated by the scarcity of subgoal-level supervision in standard robot learning datasets, which restricts the development of robots that can convey the subtasks they are executing during long-horizon manipulation. To address this issue, this paper proposes Subgoal-Aware Diffusion Policy (SADP), a framework that leverages foundation models to autonomously generate subgoal-annotated demonstrations and trains diffusion policies on these datasets. SADP structures policy execution around human-interpretable subgoals by conditioning action generation on both task-level and subgoal-level descriptions. A lightweight auxiliary head further predicts subgoal completion states, allowing the robot to expose its current execution stage and monitor subgoal progression. Experiments in RLBench simulations and real-world evaluations on a UR5e robot demonstrate that SADP achieves higher task success rates than strong task-conditioned diffusion baselines, while providing subgoal-level execution signals for monitoring progress and diagnosing failures. These results highlight that built-in, rather than post-hoc, interpretability can coexist with high task performance.

</details>


### [151] [Beyond Safety Filtering: Control Barrier Function-Informed Reinforcement Learning for Connected and Automated Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16894)
*Jianye Xu,Bassam Alrifaee*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Control Barrier Function (CBF)-informed reward design for Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) that converts CBF constraint values into a reward signal for safer learning, outperforming heuristic baselines in a connected vehicle intersection scenario with better task performance and less hyperparameter sensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reward design in RL typically relies on hand-crafted heuristics that are difficult to tune; the authors aim to automate and improve reward design for safe multi-agent learning.

Method: The method converts CBF constraint values under joint MARL actions into a reward signal that explicitly guides safe learning, integrating safety constraints directly into the reward structure.

Result: Achieves highest task performance and is less sensitive to reward hyperparameters compared to two heuristic reward baselines in a four-way multi-lane intersection with connected and automated vehicles.

Conclusion: CBF-informed reward design provides a principled, robust alternative to heuristic reward shaping for safe MARL, yielding consistently strong performance across hyperparameter ranges.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) uses rewards to guide learning, yet reward design is typically hand-crafted using heuristics that can be difficult to tune. We propose a Control Barrier Function (CBF)-informed reward design for Multi-Agent RL (MARL) that converts CBF constraint values under joint MARL actions into a reward signal that explicitly guides safe learning. We compare against two heuristic reward baselines in a four-way multi-lane intersection with connected and automated vehicles. Results show that our method achieves the highest task performance and is less sensitive to reward hyperparameters, yielding consistently strong performance across the tested hyperparameter range. Code for reproducing the experimental results and a video demonstration are available at https://github.com/bassamlab/SigmaRL.

</details>


### [152] [MORN: Metacognitive Object-Goal Regulation for Resource-Rational Long-Horizon Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16932)
*Xi Lin,Jiayi Li,Kangyi Wu,Jiaqiao Tang,Qingrong He,Lin Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MORN (Metacognitive Object-goal Regulation Navigation) is a cognitive-science-inspired executive architecture that augments zero-shot ObjectNav agents with a meta-controller to monitor global resource budgets, enabling dynamic goal regulation that improves completion rate from 0.23 to 0.30 and reduces wasted steps from 0.90 to 0.70 on HM3D.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-shot ObjectNav agents using VLMs are purely reactive systems that lack global resource awareness (time, battery), causing them to exhaust critical budgets on infeasible subgoals due to partial observability and fail to balance local exploration with mission viability.

Method: MORN is an executive architecture inspired by Dual-Process Theory, augmenting frozen navigation backbones with a System 2 meta-controller that monitors the System 1 locomotor. It formalizes three neuro-cognitive states—Potentiality Index, Persistence Gating, and Evidence Accumulation—to dynamically regulate mission schedules based on online estimates of progress velocity and perceptual uncertainty, neutralizing the Sunk Cost Fallacy.

Result: On HM3D dataset, MORN improves Goal Completion Rate (CR) from 0.23 to 0.30 and reduces Wasted Step Fraction (WSF) from 0.90 to 0.70.

Conclusion: In resource-constrained autonomy, metacognitive awareness of global resources is as critical as reactive navigation ability.

Abstract: Robots deployed in unstructured human environments must frequently execute long-horizon missions, such as find the mug, then the chair, then the printer, under strict operational constraints. While contemporary zero-shot Object Navigation (ObjectNav) agents leverage Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to effectively localize semantic targets, they operate as purely reactive systems that inherently lack global resource awareness. Consequently, these agents inadvertently exhaust critical budgets, including time and battery, on infeasible subgoals due to partial observability, failing to balance local exploration with global mission viability. To bridge this gap by injecting resource-rationality into the navigation loop, we present MORN (Metacognitive Object-goal Regulation Navigation), an executive architecture inspired by Dual-Process Theory in cognitive science. MORN augments frozen navigation backbones with a System 2 meta-controller that continuously monitors the System 1 locomotor. By formalizing three neuro-cognitive states, Potentiality Index, Persistence Gating, and Evidence Accumulation, MORN dynamically regulates the mission schedule based on online estimates of progress velocity and perceptual uncertainty. This mechanism effectively neutralizes the Sunk Cost Fallacy, enabling agents to abort zombie goals early and decisively commit to achievable ones. Extensive experiments on the HM3D dataset demonstrate that MORN improves Goal Completion Rate (CR) from 0.23 to 0.30 and reduces Wasted Step Fraction (WSF) from 0.90 to 0.70, establishing that in resource-constrained autonomy, the metacognitive awareness of global resources is as critical as the reactive ability to navigate.

</details>


### [153] [NORM-Nav: Zero-Shot Mobile Robot Navigation with Natural Language Behavioral Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16979)
*Dongjie Huo,Junhui Wang,Chao Gao,Yan Qiao,Dong Zhang,Guyue Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NORM-Nav is a zero-shot framework that uses LLMs to parse natural language behavioral constraints into multi-layer costmaps (geometric, semantic, directional, velocity) for socially compliant robot navigation, outperforming baselines in simulation and real-world tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile robots in human-centered environments need to follow local behavioral conventions, not just avoid collisions. Existing costmap-based navigation focuses on geometric feasibility and ignores social norms, leading to socially inappropriate behaviors.

Method: NORM-Nav uses an LLM to parse natural language instructions into structured constraints, grounds them via real-time vision-LiDAR perception, and encodes them as multi-layer costmaps (geometric, semantic, directional, velocity cues) compatible with standard grid-based planners.

Result: NORM-Nav improves task success rates and produces trajectories closer to human references compared to representative baselines in both simulation and real-world experiments.

Conclusion: Integrating natural language behavioral constraints into costmap-based planning via LLMs enables zero-shot socially compliant navigation without retraining or fine-tuning existing planners.

Abstract: Mobile robots operating in human-centered environments must generate not only collision-free paths but also trajectories that follow local behavioral conventions. Conventional costmap-based navigation emphasizes geometric feasibility and often overlooks such requirements, which can result in socially inappropriate behaviors. This paper presents NORM-Nav, a zero-shot framework that integrates natural language behavioral constraints into costmap-based planning. An LLM parses each instruction into structured constraints and grounds them using real-time vision--LiDAR perception. These constraints are encoded as multi-layer costmaps that represent geometric, semantic, directional, and velocity cues and are directly compatible with standard grid-based planners. Simulation and real-world experiments indicate that NORM-Nav improves task success rates and produces trajectories closer to human references than representative baselines. The project website is available at https://ei-nav.github.io/NORM-Nav.

</details>


### [154] [Generalizable and Actionable Parts Pose Estimation with Symmetry Annotation-Free Learning Strategy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17033)
*Wenxiao Chen,Xueyu Yuan,Liu Liu,Di Wu,Dan Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SAFAG is a symmetry annotation-free framework for generalizable and actionable parts pose estimation that uses a two-stage stepwise refinement approach and treats symmetry prediction as a self-supervised probability distribution problem, achieving superior performance without requiring symmetry annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GAPart pose estimation methods either lack sufficient design for symmetry issues or require extensive symmetry annotations, which hinders performance in data-lacking scenarios.
